[1] The Hacker News - OpenAI and Cisco Unveil AI-Assisted Linux Security Tool to Block Exploits Before Patching
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 11:32:57 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/openai-and-cisco-unveil-ai-assisted.html

[TAM METİN - 688 kelime]
OpenAI and Cisco have teamed up to launch Shirudo (meaning "shield" in Japanese), an open-source Linux runtime that allows artificial intelligence (AI) agents to defend systems without downtime.

"It lets agents write dynamic eBPF controls to shield systems from exploits, giving defenders more time to test and roll out patches," OpenAI said in a post on X.

The AI upstart called the initiative a "first step" toward "self-defending infrastructure" that can adapt itself to new threats while the services continue to run and enable defenders to test and deploy fixes without any disruption.

The development comes as AI is accelerating vulnerability discovery, finding critical bugs in foundational software used to power internet-connected software.

Shirudo is being pitched as a way for defenders to test temporary safeguards (aka shields) and check if they block behavior that exposes a vulnerability, while letting legitimate requests pass through. Once a shield is approved by an operator, it can be deployed without having to stop the service.

The idea is to avoid a complete service disruption to block a line of attack simply because both an exploit and a legitimate request may rely on the same underlying Linux operation.

To achieve this, Shirudo makes use of eBPF (short for extended Berkeley Packet Filter), a technology that makes it possible to run lightweight, sandboxed programs inside the kernel without the need for modifying the system code or restarting it.

"eBPF is a highly efficient, sandboxed virtual machine in the Linux Kernel," Cisco's Isovalent said. "These eBPF programs allow you to interact with the Linux kernel to observe and take action on any events happening on a machine. Some use eBPF to simply observe everything on their system, others use it to enforce policies (routing, security, and more)."

Notably, Shirudo is built atop the foundations of Tetragon, an eBPF-based security platform that Isovalent developed and open-sourced in May 2022. The crucial difference is that it's primarily designed for agents and leverages the capabilities of eBPF to inject code mitigations.

Central to Shirudo is a guardian agent that uses eBPF shields to block vulnerable code paths without restarting the system, while giving security teams the flexibility to monitor how a proposed shield would affect the application before enforcing it. The shields are also cryptographically signed and verified prior to being loaded.

"Agents scan open-source code and take in vendor disclosures," Cisco said. "They trace each vulnerability to the affected assets and attack paths, then write a small, targeted shield. They check that the shield covers the exploit and that the service still runs. The shield only graduates to active blocking when the evidence says it's safe, and it retires when the real patch lands."

This involves the following steps -

An agent is tasked with finding new vulnerabilities in an application, or working off external sources of new vulnerability disclosures.

Shirudo on the operator machine builds a security shield consisting of eBPF or application bytecode after reproducing and validating the exploit.

The shield is delivered as signed .bee packages via an OCI-compliant registry.

The Shirudo layer on each target pulls these .bee packages, validating each signature using TPMs to establish trust and safety.

In a controlled experiment, a Codex-generated shield blocked a Java4Shell exploit attempt against a running Elasticsearch service without requiring a restart, OpenAI said.

OpenAI and Cisco said the goal is to fight AI-based threats with AI and counter the risks posed by AI-driven attacks as soon as a vulnerability is identified at machine speed.

"We need this kind of machine-speed defense because AI models have become incredibly good at finding and exploiting software vulnerabilities, and they get better with every generation," Thomas Graf, CTO of Cisco Security, and Liz Rice, chief open source office at Isovalent, said. "They can also chain flaws together and discover creative new attack vectors that no one has thought about before."

"Yet a critical fix in widely-used software, from the Linux kernel to the libraries every application depends on, still takes days, weeks, or even months to reach the majority of systems. That timeframe is no longer acceptable given that the mean time to exploitation has become negative."

[2] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploit N-able MailAssure Flaw While AI Agents Exploit MONAI Label Zero-Day
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 11:32:45 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-n-able-mailassure-flaw.html

[TAM METİN - 951 kelime]
The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Monday added two flaws impacting N-able MailAssure and MONAI Label to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, citing evidence of active exploitation.

The critical vulnerabilities are listed below -

CVE-2026-134624 (CVSS score: 9.3) - A server-side request forgery (SSRF) vulnerability in N-able MailAssure's LDAP synchronization connection check that could allow an authenticated API user to connect to arbitrary internal hosts and ports, determine which are reachable, and send crafted data to internal services.

CVE-2026-133968 (CVSS score: 9.8) - A path traversal vulnerability in MONAI Label that could allow an unauthenticated remote attacker to write arbitrary files outside the intended datastore directory, potentially resulting in remote code execution.

The exploitation of CVE-2026-134624 stems from a disclosure last week, in which N-able said it detected a security incident that resulted in a service disruption affecting Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud on November 16, 2026.

As of November 21, 2026, 11:44 p.m. UTC, access to the Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud dashboard and APIs has been restored.

"N-able has remediated the recent security incident affecting our Mail Assure and SpamExperts environments and restored the impacted functionality," a spokesperson for the company told The Hacker News. Our investigation to date indicates that exploitation was confined to a single N-able hosted server and continued monitoring since remediation has found no further malicious activity."

CVE-2026-133968, on the other hand, has been linked to an agentic attack in which a suspected China-nexus threat actor leveraged CAIRN, an artificial intelligence (AI)-powered problem-solving engine, to conduct hacking operations with minimal human involvement.

"In this campaign, the actor provided a list of targeted IP addresses with variations of the goal to 'obtain shell access,'" CJ Moses, CISO of Amazon Integrated Security, said. "Based on those inputs, the AI agents planned, executed, and adjusted their approach as they learned, without the operator choosing each next step."

Some of these attacks unsuccessfully attempted to brute-force their way into a Redis instance at one university and a GPU server at another. The activity also singled out an unspecified target running MONAI Label, an open-source medical imaging AI application, identifying the aforementioned zero-day and exploiting it in about 11 minutes to write arbitrary files to the server.

"In a later session, the agents used the same vulnerability to write a Python reverse shell payload to sitecustomize.py, a file Python automatically imports at interpreter startup," Moses said. "The agents then triggered a training API endpoint to spawn a new Python process. The new process imported the payload and established a reverse shell running as root."

Following the discovery of the zero-day, the operator is said to have provided the AI agents with a proxy to tunnel into a second university network running the same vulnerable software, and then directed them to target other machines in that network.

The attacks are part of a broader cluster of attacks involving the use of AI-driven frameworks like ARTEX, CAIRN, and AntiFox, and CyberStrikeAI in recent months. A subset of these activities is estimated to have breached over 100 companies, including John Deere and SharkNinja. The end goals, per NorForge, revolve around establishing persistent access and financial theft -

AIA0001, which has targeted organizations, mainly in the U.S., Japan, and Australia, to deploy web shells and gain access to e-commerce and payment infrastructure for subsequent monetization.

AIA0002, which has targeted merchant payment services, gray-market payment sites, pharmaceutical sites, and a criminal carding site for financial gain.

AIA0003, which has targeted smaller e-commerce sites and very large U.S.-based commercial platforms to obtain shell access, admin panel access, or database access.

In a report published last week, Amazon Web Services (AWS) also revealed that its MadPot sensor network captured requests from AI agents linked to two offensive campaigns running on ARTEX, an AI-native penetration framework.

"The most prominent ARTEX campaign was a large-scale theft of credentials to access AI models, organized as a self-sustaining operation in which stolen keys paid for the LLM inference powering further credential hunting," AWS said. "Another campaign targeted cryptocurrency exchanges for know-your-customer (KYC) verification bypass using real-time deepfake injection."

According to Datadog Security Labs, ARTEX also appears under other names like ScopeWeaver, LUCK — 自主资产控制台 (LUCK — Autonomous Asset Console), and 观澜SEC渗透测试平台 (Guanlan SEC Penetration Testing Platform).

In-the-wild activity involving ARTEX has also targeted several AWS environments with an aim to access financial data. Initial access vectors used in these attacks include compromised long-term AWS access keys and public Cognito user pools, with the latter using them to register an account, collect tokens, and enumerate AWS accounts.

"In one instance using a valid long-term access key, ARTEX's most frequent action was FilterLogEvents," security researcher Julie Agnes Sparks said. "The most common searches were various naming conventions of cryptocurrency companies, hedge funds, and related financial institutions. The terms combine a company name with words like fund, invoice, billing, and LLC."

Telemetry data gathered by Previdian from its sensors has recorded 52 HTTP requests containing explicit ARTEX markers between October 18 and November 15, 2026, from 10 source IP addresses.

"Further investigation of these sources identified 3,651 related HTTP records involving application discovery, authentication probing, remote code execution attempts, path traversal and attempts to retrieve sensitive configuration data," Ryan Dewhurst, founder and CEO of Previdian, said.

"The most significant activity targeted Oracle WebLogic, Langflow, and Flowise, with additional payloads associated with Apache Struts, Log4j, and PHPUnit."

Interestingly, one of the IP addresses associated with ARTEX activity, 31.22.91[.]156, overlaps with the AWS-documented campaign targeting AI infrastructure, including Langflow exploitation attempts consistent with CVE-2025-3248.

In light of active exploitation, Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies are recommended to apply the latest fixes for the two flaws by November 26, 2026.

[3] The Hacker News - Webworm Suspected of Modifying SoftEther VPN to Evade Detection and Hide Remote Access
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 23:15:15 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/webworm-suspected-of-modifying.html

[TAM METİN - 567 kelime]
The China-aligned threat actor known as Webworm is suspected to be using a custom version of the open-source SoftEther VPN Bridge tool in an attempt to remove recognizable detection fingerprints, while disguising it as network-monitoring software to establish covert remote access to compromised networks.

The modified variant "implements various tricks to hide itself from security tools," Rapid7 said in an analysis, attributing it to the adversarial collective with moderate confidence.

First publicly detailed by Broadcom-owned Symantec in September 2022, Webworm is the name assigned to a China-aligned nation-state group that has targeted government entities, IT services, and electric power companies across Russia, Georgia, Mongolia, and several other Asian nations.

One of the tools used by the threat actor is a legitimate software called SoftEther VPN, with the bridge acting as a pathway connecting the compromised network and the attacker-controlled infrastructure.

The latest findings from Rapid7 revolve around a Windows executable ("netcapa.exe"), which is a modified build of SoftEther VPN Bridge ("vpnbridge_x64.exe") version 4.44, build 9807. The mutated version, rebranded as NtMonitor VPN, masquerades itself as Network Monitor Software from a company called Network Systems Solutions Inc. The artifact was uploaded to VirusTotal around mid-January 2026.

"The PDB path included in the executable reads C:\Users\Administrator\Desktop\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\src\DebugFiles\pdb\x64_Release\vpnbridge.pdb," the cybersecurity company said. "As such, the tool doesn't hide its identity from reverse engineers, only external interactions."

The renaming is also said to be inconsistent, with many registry key paths still referencing the program as "SoftEther Project," even though a number of other occurrences of "SoftEther VPN" have been replaced by "NtMonitor VPN."

One of the most noticeable changes identified in the altered version is the use of innocuous-looking HTTP endpoints "/update/alive.cgi" and "/update/picture.cgi" to replace "/vpnsvc/vpn.cgi" and "/vpnsvc/connect.cgi," respectively, so that they look like ordinary web application activity.

It also changes the protocol watermark used by SoftEther VPN as part of the handshake process when initiating communication to help the tool distinguish protocol traffic from ordinary HTTP requests. When the connecting client is found to speak the expected protocol, it proceeds with the handshake. If the check fails, the server returns a decoy HTTP response.

The modified version swaps the GIF-based watermark used by the stock SoftEther VPN utility for a PNG-based watermark so as to bypass existing detection signatures based on the GIF watermark.

Some of the other changes introduced to the program are listed below -

Reducing a resource archive named hamcore.se2 used by SoftEther VPN to its bare minimum, as it's required to be present at runtime, and embedding it directly into the executable, causing the binary's size to increase by about 1.5 MB.

Changing the name of the configuration file from "vpn_bridge.config" to "setting.conf" and making its contents encrypted with a custom algorithm. The encrypted configuration starts with the magic bytes "RCEN" in ASCII.

What's interesting here is that the attackers have not been found to introduce any new functionality to the tool, indicating the primary focus was on stealth.

"It seems like they methodically analyzed SoftEther's detection surface and implemented counter-measures to all of the easy ways a detection engineer may target it," Rapid7 said, calling the latest evolution a hybrid approach that brings together the attacker's use of custom malware and dual-use tools.

"We are observing a sort of hybrid of the two — stock SoftEther software that is not trojanized, but rebuilt from source in a way that erases much of its detection fingerprint."

[4] The Hacker News - Critical binary-parser Flaw Enables JavaScript Injection via Malicious Parser Definitions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:52:20 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/critical-binary-parser-flaw-enables.html

[TAM METİN - 277 kelime]
A critical security flaw has been disclosed in the popular binary-parser library that could allow an attacker who controls parser definitions to execute arbitrary code.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-134814 (CVSS score: 9.2 / 8.1), is a case of code injection impacting binary-parser versions prior to 2.3.1.

"Binary-parser before 2.3.1 contains a code injection vulnerability that allows attackers controlling parser definitions to inject JavaScript through string-typed options passed unvalidated by Context.generateOption()," according to a description of the flaw in the NIST National Vulnerability Database (NVD).

"Attackers can supply crafted length, lengthInBytes, key, tag, offset, or namely() alias values to execute arbitrary JavaScript in the Node.js process when the parser is compiled."

According to an advisory released by the project maintainer on November 11, 2026, the problem arose from the fact that some user-controlled string options were inserted directly into the generated JavaScript code without adequate validation.

The parser options include -

the alias passed to namely()

"Parsing untrusted binary data with a trusted parser definition is not affected," Keichi Takahashi, who maintains the library, said. "These options are now validated as identifier paths (e.g. foo, $parent.len). Expressions such as 'len - 1' are rejected; use a function instead."

CVE-2026-134814 can also be viewed as an incomplete fix for CVE-2026-1245 (CVSS score: 6.5), another code injection vulnerability that was fixed back in January 2026 and resulted from insufficient validation of field names and encoding parameters.

"[The flaw] allows attackers controlling parser definitions to inject and execute arbitrary JavaScript within the host Node.js process upon parser compilation," Upwind Security said in an X post. Users are advised to update to the latest version of the library for optimal protection.

[5] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploit Critical ScreenConnect Flaw to Hijack MSP Technician PCs Without Warning
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:11:04 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-critical-screenconnect.html

[TAM METİN - 686 kelime]
Threat actors are exploiting a now-patched security flaw in ConnectWise ScreenConnect to target managed service provider (MSP) servers.

The activity revolves around CVE-2026-84869 (CVSS score: 9.9), a critical ScreenConnect client flaw that allows a guest to transfer and execute a malicious payload on a connecting technician's host.

"On November 12, 2026, we observed one managed service provider's (MSP) technician's workstation exploited to run a guest-pushed batch file, which then reported to a Telegram bot, downloaded an installer from masterdarky[.]com, and started a rogue ScreenConnect client pointing at the attacker's own relay, all in about 9 seconds," Huntress security researcher John Hammond said.

The batch script, "update_224ac089.bat," is designed to decode a Base64 Telegram bot token and chat ID, and register the new infection. Subsequently, it downloads an MSI installer that masquerades as a Windows update ("WindowsUpdate.msi") to launch a ScreenConnect client, enabling the attacker to gain remote access to the technician's machine.

The activity, observed between October 20 and November 16, 2026, is said to have sprayed servers blindly, with Huntress observing at least 688,000 guest "run file" requests for files named update.bat or update_<8 hex>.bat hitting 42 ScreenConnect servers from 29 IP addresses. The guests have been found to make repeated requests that have blank session names or default-looking Windows hostnames.

In another instance, a guest machine at 91.224.92[.]167 pushed a seemingly harmless text file ("marker.txt") two seconds after a technician joined a session, effectively acting as a canary to alert the operator that the exploit works against the target before sending the actual payload.

What's more, a targeted attack aimed at an MSP server in October 2026 had the attacker joining as a guest across three real sessions the technician had created and asked the client to run a file designed to drop a batch script ("Sync.bat") into the technician's computer's Windows Startup folder.

It's worth noting that this activity is distinct from a recently disclosed attack wave in which unknown threat actors gained unauthorized access to partner ScreenConnect environments by leveraging an authentication flaw in a third-party extension to deploy a malware toolkit called SCKit, which then exploits the flaw to propagate the malware to a technician's workstation.

The attacks also coincide with another cluster in which thousands of unauthorized ScreenConnect guest sessions have originated from machines with cryptocurrency exchange-themed names like BITMEX-BOT, KUCOIN-SRV, and LAPTOP-BINANCE to trick technicians into connecting to them. No following malicious activity such as file transfers or command execution has been observed.

Specifically, the threat actors have been found to obtain access to publicly downloadable ScreenConnect Access installers to extract the relay address needed to register a guest client with the corresponding ScreenConnect server, and then create fake guest sessions and make those sessions appear online.

"Someone is registering unauthorized guest computers on MSPs' servers, where they sit in these session lists. They come from a scripted emulator that impersonates a Windows ScreenConnect client, and nearly all of them had connected from a single IONOS-hosted IP, 74.208.158[.]106," Huntress said.

"As of November 13, we've seen related activity across at least 118 self-hosted ScreenConnect servers belonging to 109 Huntress-protected organizations. In total, we found 2,408 unauthorized sessions enrolled since November 3, 2026."

Organizations are advised to ensure that technicians' ScreenConnect host clients are updated to 26.6.5 or later, hunt for signs of exposure, isolate affected workstations, and rotate all credentials reachable from that workstation. If immediate patching is not feasible, it's recommended to remove the TransferFiles permission from roles and session groups.

"An MSP technician's workstation typically holds RMM consoles, saved credentials, and access to every customer, making it an exceptionally attractive target," Huntress said. "When a technician joins a remote session, their ScreenConnect host client and the guest machine can exchange files. Normally, the technician decides what moves and what runs."

"A malicious guest exploiting CVE-2026-84869 can push a file to the technician's machine and get the technician's own client to run it. From the technician's view, no prompt appears. They only have to join the session, whether from an attacker-controlled machine or a real customer's support session that the attacker has slipped into as an extra guest."

[6] The Hacker News - N-able Discloses Critical Mail Assure Vulnerability Found During Breach Investigation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 17:58:09 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/n-able-discloses-critical-mail-assure.html

[TAM METİN - 567 kelime]
N-able yesterday disclosed a critical flaw it found while investigating the breach of its Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud, an email filtering service it runs for managed service providers.

The bug, tracked as CVE-2026-134624, is a server-side request forgery (SSRF) flaw. It allowed a user with high-level API access to open connections to internal systems of the user's choosing on the platform's servers.

The company fixed the flaw and restored access to the dashboard and APIs late on Saturday, closing an outage that began last Monday. Email delivery and spam filtering kept working throughout, according to the company. It has not said whether the flaw was the attackers' way in.

The bug sat in a connection check that Mail Assure runs for LDAP synchronization when a relay is configured. Before the fixed build, nothing restricted that check's destination, so the server could be pointed at any internal host and port to see which ones answered.

"An authenticated API user can cause the server to connect to arbitrary internal hosts and ports, determine which are reachable, and send crafted data to internal services.," the vendor's security advisory says.

The advisory gives the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.3, and lists builds earlier than 20261121.1943 as affected, with that same build as the fix. It does not claim code execution.

N-able informed customers of a service disruption last Monday and initially traced it to platform configuration and service dependencies, according to its incident timeline.

Last Wednesday, the company said the outage was, in fact, a security incident: an unauthorized party had accessed one of the Hosted Cloud servers. The dashboard and APIs were kept offline as a precaution while it investigated.

It responded by taking the affected server offline, rotating internal platform credentials, revoking customers' existing API tokens, invalidating OAuth tokens, and resetting local users' passwords.

The company says it has found no evidence that customer data was compromised, including tenants' mailboxes, and no sign of exploitation beyond that one server since the fix was implemented.

The investigation is still open, and the breach is unrelated to the N-central incident, according to the company. In that earlier incident, it took its hosted remote-monitoring platform offline on November 9 after a researcher published 33 unpatched flaws.

For hosted customers, the work now is reconnecting. Scripts and integrations that used the revoked API tokens or OAuth tokens will need new credentials. The exact steps differ by setup: each partner has been emailed guidance specific to its environment, and anyone who has not received it is asked to contact support.

The hosted platform itself is already patched, according to yesterday's incident update, so there is nothing for Hosted Cloud users to install.

SpamExperts Local Cloud runs on customer hardware, with software updates applied on a weekly schedule, according to the product's update documentation. The incident statements describe the Hosted Cloud only, so Local Cloud operators should confirm their servers are on build 20261121.1943.

What the account so far does not say is how the attackers first got in, or whether any of the credentials they may have reached were actually taken. An outside digital forensics and incident response team has been brought in, and the company says a formal root cause analysis is due within two weeks of yesterday's update. Separate restoration times for quarantine, logging, and mailbox synchronization, which were being brought back in phases, have not been given.

[7] The Hacker News - Hackers Exploit Critical ASP.NET Anti-CSRF Library Flaw Allowing Unauthenticated RCE
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 14:41:56 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-critical-aspnet-anti.html

[TAM METİN - 333 kelime]
Cybersecurity researchers have urged users to discontinue using a legacy ASP.NET anti-CSRF library named Idunno.AntiCsrf ("Idunno.AntiCsrf.dll") following the discovery of a critical vulnerability that has been exploited in the wild.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-131914, carries a CVSS score of 9.3 out of 10.0. The issue affects versions 0.9.1.0 and 0.9.1 Beta, and relates to an insecure deserialization vulnerability in the Idunno.AntiCsrf.AntiCsrfModule HTTP module.

At its core, the problem stems from the fact that the module deserializes untrusted CSRF form token data using an unkeyed .NET System.Web.UI.ObjectStateFormatter instance without cryptographic MAC or signature verification when handling incoming HTTP POST requests to ASP.NET WebForms (System.Web.UI.Page) endpoints.

This behavior, in turn, can allow a remote attacker to achieve arbitrary remote code execution (RCE). Google's Mandiant division has given the vulnerability a "High" exploitability assessment, adding that exploitation hinges only on a single HTTP POST request with no prior authentication or valid CSRF token.

LDB, Genwei Jiang, Dhanesh Kizhakkinan, Brian Bahtiarian, and Lucas Kossack of Google Cloud and Mandiant Incident Response have been credited with discovering the flaw earlier this month.

"Active exploitation in the wild has been observed."

Google's threat intelligence team did not disclose any further details related to the activity. When reached for comment, Google told The Hacker News that it does not have anything to share at this time.

In the absence of an official patch, organizations still using the library are advised to discontinue its use and replace it with an actively supported anti-CSRF alternative such as follows -

System.Web.Helpers.AntiForgery

ASP.NET WebForms Page.ViewStateUserKey combined with MAC-verified/encrypted ViewState on postback forms

ASP.NET Core Anti-Request Forgery (IAntiforgery)

"Audit all ASP.NET Web.config files and bin/ directories for references to Idunno.AntiCsrf or AntiCsrfModule, and review IIS logs for anomalous POST requests to .aspx endpoints returning HTTP 302 or HTTP 500 with elevated cs-bytes (where logged)," Google said.

"Environments with evidence of exploitation should rotate all secrets stored in Web.config and applicationHost.config (including <machineKey> keys, encryption keys, and database credentials) and inspect webroots for unauthorized files."

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-exploit-critical-aspnet-anti.html, thehackernews.com, 23.11.2026)

[8] BleepingComputer - South Korea’s power giant KEPCO confirms data breach impacting 1.5 million
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:54:51 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/south-koreas-power-giant-kepco-confirms-data-breach-impacting-15-million/

[TAM METİN - 507 kelime]
A threat actor breached the internal network of Korea Electric Power Corporation (KEPCO) and accessed two systems containing customer, employee, and contractor data.

The attack exposed details of more than 1.5 million individuals before the company blocked access from external IP addresses and suspended the affected services.

KEPCO is South Korea’s main electric power company and a state-controlled, major operator of the country’s critical infrastructure.

The organization detected the attack on November 20, when it confirmed unauthorized access to its KEPCO ON electricity billing platform and its electronic procurement system (SRM).

“On Friday, November 20, Korea Electric Power Corporation (hereafter KEPCO), led by President Kim Dong-cheol, confirmed that some customer information held in its IT systems had been exposed,” the power company states.

“The company is making every effort to prevent harm.”

ON is an integrated customer platform that allows customers to view and pay electricity bills. SRM is the company’s procurement system for contracting services.

According to the announcement, published on Sunday, the following was exposed:

for 650,487 KEPCO ON customers: names, ID information, phone numbers, addresses, bank names, bank account numbers, account holder names, card issuers, card numbers, cardholder names, and electricity charge and billing information.

for 426,827 company representatives: names, ID information, email addresses, and company addresses.

for 442,934 KEPCO employees: names, affiliation, employee ID number, mobile phone number, email address, company phone number.

The company says the data breach resulted from “advanced technologies such as AI,” but it shared no specific details about the initial access method or the attack chain.

KEPCO is notifying exposed individuals directly, has filed a report with the data protection authorities, and is coordinating with government and financial authorities to block abuse such as unauthorized transactions.

Potentially exposed individuals are warned that the exposed information could be used for targeted phishing and impersonation. They are advised to report any attempts to the police or Korea’s Internet and Security Agency.

It is also recommended to avoid installing unknown apps, entering information into suspicious forms, and sharing sensitive data or verification codes over calls or texts.

A threat actor using the “Noiem” alias has claimed the attack and advertised the breach on a hacker forum on November 19, saying that they had KEPCO user data, supplier records, and bidding and contract documents.

South Korea’s Personal Information Protection Commission separately confirmed that KEPCO received an email from a hacker claiming data theft on November 19.

While these claims match the available evidence, attribution to Noiem remains unconfirmed.

[9] BleepingComputer - OpenRouter drops AI providers over leaked prompts and secrets
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:00:20 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/openrouter-drops-ai-providers-over-leaked-prompts-and-secrets/

[TAM METİN - 510 kelime]
OpenRouter removed three AI providers from its platform after discovering that they had violated their stated data-handling policies and exposed sensitive data.

The company also revoked Zero Data Retention (ZDR) eligibility for 125 endpoints as a precaution and notified all potentially affected customers.

OpenRouter is an AI model broker that lets developers access AI models from different companies using a single API key.

When an app sends a request, OpenRouter forwards it to a provider that runs the selected model and passes the answer back.

Operational technology security company Dragos alerted OpenRouter after sending two million test prompts through the platform, each containing a unique tracking link or decoy credential, and detecting "activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed."

Dragos discovered that, in some cases, those credentials were leaked to outsiders and used in the wild, indicating that the test prompts had escaped.

“We observed activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed. Our seeded test prompts were used in attempts to access our controlled test systems and canary AWS accounts,” Dragos says.

OpenRouter says there was no breach on its own systems, making the leak point at the provider level and leading to the decision to remove the three leaking inference providers: Together AI, Phala, and Novita.

Another provider, Atlas Cloud, said it would voluntarily withdraw its models from OpenRouter pending security testing, following a third-party security incident.

The other three published separate announcements responding to Dragos’ findings and explaining how the leaks occurred.

Together AI said an AI coding agent exposed a repository credential on GitHub, allowing an unauthorized party to download stored prompt samples from June and July involving MiniMax M3, GLM-5.2, and Kimi K2.6.

Phala said an attacker compromised a server processing DeepSeek V4 Flash 0731 requests routed through OpenRouter between October 21 and 22, and possibly up to November 8, and obtained application credentials from affected virtual machines.

Novita said a routing misconfiguration forwarded some MiniMax requests, including those sent through OpenRouter, to MiniMax’s official API between May 28 and November 11.

All four providers are tightening security controls, strengthening their zero data retention or data deletion policies, and advising potentially impacted users to rotate any secrets that may have been exposed in the leaked requests.

OpenRouter is also adapting Dragos’ tests for ongoing use to check whether AI providers forward or leak user requests/responses to third parties, and will require validation before restoring ZDR eligibility.

[10] BleepingComputer - Microsoft Azure flaw exploited to steal customers' API credentials
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 14:42:20 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/microsoft-azure-flaw-exploited-to-steal-customers-api-credentials/

[TAM METİN - 912 kelime]
Microsoft is warning customers that a security vulnerability in Azure API Management (APIM) Classic tiers was exploited to steal stored API credentials, with Norwegian authorities linking the flaw to cyberattacks against energy companies.

Azure API Management is a cloud service that lets organizations publish, secure, monitor, and manage APIs that connect applications, services, and data.

In an Azure Service Health security advisory (Tracking ID: MZK9-GH0) sent to customers and shared with BleepingComputer, Microsoft said it identified the vulnerability on November 18, 2026, and deployed a mitigation the following day.

"Our investigation found that customers were exposed and an unauthorized party could have used the issue to access secrets stored in Azure API Management Classic instances before Microsoft identified the security issue," Microsoft said in the advisory.

Microsoft told BleepingComputer that the vulnerability impacted the Developer, Basic, Standard, and Premium tiers of Azure API Management (APIM) Classic, but that the Consumption tier of APIM Classic and all APIM v2 tiers were not affected.

According to Microsoft, the vulnerability allowed unauthorized access to certain management functionality when APIM Classic instances were not configured according to recommended security best practices.

This access could have allowed attackers to perform administrative operations, including accessing secrets stored in the service.

Microsoft said the following information may have been exposed:

Secrets stored in Named Values

Client secrets associated with identity providers

Client secrets associated with OpenID Connect providers

Tokens used to call direct management APIs

These credentials could potentially allow attackers to access additional applications, services, or backend systems, depending on their permissions.

Microsoft said it closed the unauthorized access path on November 19 and rotated Microsoft-controlled keys and credentials where appropriate.

However, the company warned that its remediation would not invalidate customer credentials that may have been compromised before the fix was deployed.

"This remediation blocks the unauthorized management-plane access path that could allow an actor to perform administrative operations, including secrets management, against Azure API Management services," Microsoft warned.

"However, it does not automatically invalidate credentials that may have been accessed before the fix was deployed."

The vulnerability affects Azure API Management Classic tiers, while Microsoft said APIM v2 tiers are not impacted.

Microsoft urged affected customers to immediately rotate potentially exposed credentials, investigate suspicious activity in systems accessible through those credentials, and review available logs for signs of unauthorized access.

In a Reddit post, an administrator claimed their organization discovered the vulnerability after detecting unauthorized access to Azure Key Vault using a valid administrator token.

"Our company has been fighting this for days, after discovering that someone logged into our keyvaults using a valid administrator token and not following the normal access pattern," the administrator claimed.

The administrator alleged that attackers exploited a zero-day authentication bypass in a deprecated legacy API to gain access to a privileged system account on APIM instances.

According to the administrator, the legacy API did not generate logs that customers could use to detect the unauthorized access. They also alleged that Microsoft initially attributed the suspicious activity to leaked customer credentials before deploying a global fix.

BleepingComputer has not independently verified these allegations.

The vulnerability has also been linked to attacks affecting Norwegian organizations, including energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi.

In a security advisory, Norway's National Security Authority (NSM) said an unknown threat actor targeted access tokens, secrets, and named values stored in Azure API Management.

The agency said Norway's energy sector first discovered the vulnerability and that organizations in other countries and industries were also affected.

Equinor disclosed an ongoing cyberattack on November 17, a day before Microsoft says it identified the APIM vulnerability. The company later announced that it had stopped the attack and found no indication that data had been stolen or altered.

Skagerak Energi also reported an attempted cyberattack but said it had no impact on its operations and no evidence that information was compromised.

While Microsoft says it deployed a remediation on November 19 that required no customer action, NSM's advisory warns organizations to verify that their APIM instances have received the security update.

The agency warns that instances running below specific runtime versions remain vulnerable and advises customers to contact their "service provider" if they have not received the update.

In a statement to BleepingComputer, Microsoft confirmed that its November 19 remediation was fully deployed to Microsoft-hosted API Management instances and required no customer action.

However, Microsoft did not clarify why Norway's NSM continued advising customers to check their APIM runtime versions and warning that instances below certain versions remained vulnerable.

Cloud security researcher Anders Kristiansen shared threat-hunting queries to help organizations investigate potential exploitation and identify credentials stored in their APIM instances.

This Azure API Management incident comes just days after Microsoft confirmed another security breach involving its Entra Connect Health service, where attackers exploited a vulnerability to steal tenant configuration information and administrator email addresses.

Update 11/23/26: Added further information from Microsoft about what APIM tiers were impacted.

[11] BleepingComputer - Spanish LaLiga confirms hackers stole soccer fans’ personal data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 13:38:40 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/spanish-laliga-confirms-hackers-stole-soccer-fans-personal-data/

[TAM METİN - 393 kelime]
LaLiga, Spain's top men's professional football league, disclosed a data breach that exposed personal information belonging to users of its services and those of some affiliated clubs.

According to the organization, the incident occurred between November 14 and 16, and the attacker extracted data from compromised systems.

LaLiga organizes Spain’s top two men’s professional football divisions.

The top league is one of the world’s most prominent football leagues, where clubs with a global audience, such as Real Madrid, FC Barcelona, and Atlético Madrid, play.

On November 16, the organization’s security team identified the source of the unauthorized access and implemented measures to block the attack and stop the data extraction.

“LALIGA was the target of a cyberattack on one of its technology platforms for fan services,” reads the announcement.

“Between November 14 and 16, a cybercriminal gained unauthorized access to the platform and extracted personal data belonging to users registered for digital services offered by LALIGA and some clubs.”

After implementing containment measures, LaLiga launched an investigation to determine the scope of the incident and introduced more thorough monitoring to detect future attempts to breach its systems.

The football organizer also reported the incident to the Spanish Data Protection Agency (AEPD) and filed a complaint with the National Police’s Central Cybercrime Unit.

Impacted clubs were also directly notified, and actions to inform impacted fans are being coordinated.

At the time of writing, no ransomware group has claimed responsibility for the attack on LaLiga.

[12] BleepingComputer - GlobeTopper loses $2.5 million after hackers steal $70k in gift cards
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 11:50:01 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/globetopper-loses-25-million-after-hackers-steal-70k-in-gift-cards/

[TAM METİN - 440 kelime]
A cyberattack disrupted operations at B2B top-up solutions provider GlobeTopper, causing an estimated $2.5 million in lost revenue, while the attacker redeemed digital gift cards worth $70,000.

Parent company iQSTEL said that the intrusion was detected on October 22 and GlobeTopper responded by suspending its platform and starting an investigation.

GlobeTopper is a business-to-business digital gift card distributor. Various retailers, fintech apps, and rewards platforms use its API. According to the platform’s website, it is used by more than 4,000 brands across 65 countries, including IKEA, Adidas, Spotify, Target, and Roblox.

The company deployed a completely rebuilt platform on AWS on October 31, while commercial operations were restored in stages, starting November 1.

“During its investigation, the Company determined that the unauthorized party obtained API credentials and integration keys of certain GlobeTopper business customers,” iQSTEL says.

“All affected credentials and integration keys have been revoked and rotated, and affected business customers have been issued new credentials subject to additional access restrictions.”

The company further stated that an investigation is ongoing to determine whether other customer, personal, payment, or provider data was accessed or acquired.

Regarding the digital gift cards the attackers redeemed, the company says it cannot block, recover, or reclaim them, and will incur the losses.

iQSTEL said the incident hurt GlobeTopper’s fourth-quarter transaction volume and operating results, but as of the filing, it has not determined it to be material.

BleepingComputer has emailed iQSTEL with additional questions regarding the incident, but the company declined to share further information.

At the time of writing, no ransomware groups have listed iQSTEL or GlobeTopper as victims on their extortion portals.

[13] BleepingComputer - AI Agents Are Creating a Shared Responsibility Gap
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 10:00:10 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/ai-agents-are-creating-a-shared-responsibility-gap/

[TAM METİN - 1309 kelime]
Frontier AI labs are very clear about their responsibilities: making the model more robust, better guarded, and harder to hijack. They are also clear about what they can’t do, which is understand the business context of an action. An agent deleting files from Google Drive could be malicious or perfectly normal. They can't tell.

That raises questions about the shared responsibility model for Agentic AI. In cloud security, there is a clear separation of responsibilities between cloud vendors and their customers. If you copy data from AWS into SFDC, each vendor is responsible for the data's infrastructure security while they have it, but the customer is responsible for the action.

The AWS Shared Responsibility Model is a classic example.

The separation isn’t nearly as clean for AI agents. Agents act as intermediaries between you and the request, and that intermediary isn't deterministic by nature. It sends authenticated, authorized requests that can be compromised or rogue.

The first scenario is somewhat clear and goes back to what the AI labs try to prevent: a rogue agent has either misunderstood something or acted in a way it shouldn’t. A human mistake, only not human and not necessarily a mistake.

Five organizations have published guidance on responsibility for AI agents: shared responsibility models from Microsoft, CSA and CoSAI, AWS's security guidance for AgentCore, and Anthropic's “Trustworthy agents in practice.” Only two assign ownership of an agent’s identity, and they split it differently. No one agrees on who is responsible for the agent's behavior.

A look at the existing approaches

The available guidance approaches responsibility from different angles. Some publications assign specific tasks to providers and customers; others describe security layers or broader stakeholder roles.

Together, they show why organizations need to determine who owns each agent, what permissions it has, and how it operates across the systems it uses.

Let's say it goes wrong

Consider a coding agent that reads a support ticket through an MCP server. That ticket contains malicious instructions that cause the agent to push a change to GitHub using the developer's token.

It isn't a contrived case. In the organizations Token monitors, 60% of Claude Code and Desktop agents called two or more outside vendors, and 47% called three or more, in the span of one month.

Now to the million-dollar question: who's responsible for catching the malicious push and stopping it?

The Agentic Universe from Token Security

Nobody has ever secured AI agents.

Learn how security teams at Roblox, Amex, and more give each agent an owner, narrow its access, and cut it off fast, then see how Token Security does it in a live demo.

Could it be the harness?

The software that runs the agent has the most visibility: it knows where the instructions were (albeit not why they were there) and the command about to run.

Claude Code, for example, checks both. A server-side probe "scans incoming tool results and flags suspicious content before Claude reads it." In auto mode, now the starting mode for interactive sessions, a separate classifier blocks actions that appear "driven by hostile content Claude read."

OpenAI, Cursor, and GitHub each have their own versions of approval prompts, automated reviewers, network limits, and sandboxes.

This capability doesn't come with responsibility, though. Harnesses put that squarely on the user's shoulders, both within the interface and in their terms.

Will the MCP catch it?

The poisoned ticket came in over MCP, a protocol built for model clients. You might expect it to carry some of the load, because it's a door purpose-made for agents. At least conceptually, it could denote expanded responsibility for the people who built this door.

The MCP spec hands the load back to the client. Clients SHOULD validate tool results before passing them to the model, and "there SHOULD always be a human in the loop with the ability to deny tool invocations," it says.

The server's duties involve tokens, inputs, access controls, and rate limits.

And so, a typical MCP server will check who sent a request, not whether it should have been sent.

It all goes back to the MCP spec, which neither uses the term "prompt injection" nor provides the server with any verified information about its caller; client information is "self-reported." It says outright that "MCP itself cannot enforce these security principles at the protocol level."

Atlassian, for its part, calls the use of MCP clients with its products "a customer-elected action."

Could GitHub be the solution?

The push reaches GitHub with a valid token. Unless an admin has set up a branch protection rule for main, nothing stops it, and by default, those rules "don't apply to people with admin permissions to the repository."

GitHub's audit log includes an agent marker, actor_is_agent, for agents that run on GitHub. Nothing in the docs says it marks a local agent's push, though. And the terms are the same as everyone's: "You are responsible for all content posted and activity that occurs under your Account."

Do agent identities close the gap?

In an ideal scenario, every agent has its own identity, which should carry narrower, shorter-lived access.

Microsoft, for example, gives agents identities of their own: a Microsoft Entra agent identity is a service principal in your Entra tenant. Microsoft's framework also marks per-action authorization as shared.

But when an agent acts on behalf of a user, Microsoft's documentation says "risky activity is attributed to the user." A Conditional Access policy aimed at an agent identity "doesn't cover agent requests made on behalf of the user."

And that's the story in a nutshell, within one vendor. Even if the framework points to shared responsibility, the product attributes the risk to the user when it's actually time to act.

You share the responsibility

That leaves you. And it's probably going to remain the norm. While platforms could, in theory, do more, agents are considered extensions of our will, even though they often have a “will” of their own.

It's a novel scenario, and the only choice is to look at the risk honestly and decide how much of it we're willing to accept. And even that is easier said than done because you can't see the agent as a single thing.

The harness has its telemetry, Atlassian has its MCP log, and GitHub has the developer's push. Coding agents like this one run under the developer's credentials by default, so their actions appear to be the developer's.

No vendor in this chain sees the whole agent:

The harness sees the instruction

The MCP server sees a token

GitHub sees a developer pushing code

Only the identity the agent uses crosses all three. That makes it the one place where a single owner can set scope, expiry, and revocation. Branch protection and allowlists help, but identity ties them together.

Ideally, you treat the agent as one identity, whatever number of vendors it touches:

Inventory per agent, across vendors. For each agent, list credentials.

Give agents identities of their own, scoped to the task and short-lived.

Decide where your log trail for agentic decision-making and action lives.

Turn on branch protection, read-only modes, tool allowlists, and everything else that can help.

Build a revocation path and practice killing an agent's access across all vendors.

Ask your vendors what they can show you about an agent that also acts elsewhere.

An organization cannot delegate accountability for an agent’s actions to a model provider or infer intent from an authenticated request. It can assign an owner, limit the agent’s access, connect its activity across systems, and test how to stop it.

Those decisions make agent adoption governable without assuming any single vendor can see the whole workflow.

If the responsibility is yours, it pays to be responsible.

When you’re ready to secure your AI Agents for growth, book a demo with the Token Security team.

[14] BleepingComputer - Microsoft testing fix for Windows 11 Bluetooth audio quality drops
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 08:28:45 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-testing-fix-for-windows-11-bluetooth-audio-quality-drops/

[TAM METİN - 452 kelime]
Microsoft is working to fix a long-standing Windows issue that has been causing significant audio quality drops for Bluetooth headphone users.

This is triggered when Windows automatically switches from the headphone (A2DP stereo) profile to the headset (hands-free, phone-call-like) profile when an app or game activates and uses the headset's microphone, because bandwidth is limited in two-way mode.

After installing the preview builds released for the Beta and Experimental channels on Friday, Windows 11 Insiders with newer Bluetooth headsets can keep playing high-quality audio even after the OS switches between profiles.

"Bluetooth LE Audio devices can now play at full-range stereo fidelity while also using the device's microphone, ensuring you continue to hear great audio quality when in game chat, in a voice call, using voice typing, or using voice chat in Copilot Chat," said Windows Insider communications lead Stephen Lines.

"When using an LE Audio device with a PC that supports Super Wideband Stereo, choose the new 2 channels, 16 bit, 48000Hz option from Settings > System > Sound > Output > {Bluetooth device} > Output settings > Format when microphone is active."

This new feature is available for all Bluetooth LE Audio devices that support 48kHz playback, such as headphones, earbuds, and speakers.

​Earlier this month, Microsoft also fixed USB audio issues that have plagued Windows users who installed security updates released since September.

As it explained when it acknowledged this known issue days after the September 2026 Patch Tuesday, USB Audio Class 1.0 devices failed to start or produce audio on some systems after installing recent updates.

Two years ago, Microsoft also blocked Windows 24H2 upgrades on systems with incompatible Intel Smart Sound Technology (SST) audio drivers due to blue screen of death (BSOD) issues and confirmed an issue that was causing game audio to go full volume when using USB DAC sound systems.

In January 2025, Microsoft addressed a bug causing audio playback problems and Code 10 errors on Windows 11 24H2 systems with USB audio device drivers after installing the KB5050094 preview update.

More recently, on Tuesday, Microsoft announced that it's testing a new feature that lets IT admins remotely lock stolen or lost enterprise Windows devices without wiping them.

[15] BleepingComputer - Call center operator gets 8 years for helping scam 30,000 Americans
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 07:02:23 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/call-center-operator-gets-8-years-for-helping-scam-30-000-americans/

[TAM METİN - 430 kelime]
A Peruvian call center operator was sentenced to eight years in prison for helping steal millions of dollars from over 30,000 Spanish-speaking US residents in a years-long government-impersonation fraud scheme.

45-year-old Carla Magaly Alcedo Mendoza of Lima, Peru, pleaded guilty to conspiracy to commit mail and wire fraud after being charged in an 18-count indictment following her March 2023 arrest and April 2025 extradition to the United States.

According to court documents, Alcedo managed and operated Peruvian call centers linked to multiple large-scale fraud and extortion schemes between January 2013 and December 2018.

The scammers first contacted Spanish-speaking Americans, claiming they qualified for free services or products. However, they later impersonated US government officials and lawyers and told the victims they were contractually obligated to pay large sums of money to receive them.

Together with her accomplices, she defrauded and extorted thousands of Americans by falsely threatening them with court action, arrest, and immigration consequences in unsolicited phone calls targeting Spanish-speaking Americans.

"The scheme spanned several years and thousands of victims made payments to Alcedo. Collectively, the scheme was responsible for defrauding more than 30,000 Spanish-speaking residents of the United States out of $15 million," the Department of Justice said.

"These criminals preyed on hardworking Americans, using fear and intimidation to steal millions of dollars. They deliberately targeted Spanish-speaking communities, impersonating government officials and threatening innocent people with arrest and legal consequences," added Acting US Attorney Yara Lorenzo Klukas.

Alcedo was the tenth defendant extradited from Peru and sentenced in this scheme, with ten other Peruvian call center owner-operators and four distribution center owner-operators involved in the scheme convicted so far.

This follows a June 2026 warning from the US Federal Trade Commission (FTC) that Americans have lost $3.5 billion to impersonation scams in 2025, with reported losses nearly tripling since 2020 and approximately $920 million stolen by government impersonators.

In April, the FBI also warned in its 2025 Internet Crime Report that US victims lost nearly $21 billion to cyber-enabled crimes throughout last year.

[16] BleepingComputer - Microsoft confirms Windows printing issues affecting Forcepoint users
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 04:11:15 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-confirms-printing-issues-after-kb5132043-windows-hotpatch/

[TAM METİN - 468 kelime]
Microsoft confirmed that some Forcepoint users may experience printing issues after installing the November 2026 Windows 11 hotpatch.

In a Friday service alert seen by BleepingComputer, Microsoft said this known issue affects only systems running Windows 11 24H2 and later.

According to Microsoft, the printing problems stem from a compatibility issue between the KB5132043 Windows hotpatch update and the Forcepoint DLP Endpoint Agent endpoint data loss prevention solution.

"After installing the November 2026 Windows hotpatch update (the Originating KBs listed above), devices running Forcepoint DLP Endpoint Agent might experience printing issues," Microsoft said. "Affected users might find that the print option is greyed out or encounter errors when selecting a printer. This issue occurs because the Print Spooler service fails to start."

While a fix is not yet available, Forcepoint has shared a temporary workaround for its customers in this support document, which can be accessed after signing in to the Forcepoint support portal.

Last week, Microsoft also warned that the KB5132043 hotpatch is causing installation and launch issues affecting Office and third-party applications on Windows 11 systems.

These two known issues should not affect systems with the standard November 2026 Patch Tuesday security updates, only those that have installed the hotpatch updates pushed through Microsoft's Windows Autopatch, a cloud-based enterprise service designed to automate Windows and Microsoft 365 updates.

However, as the company confirmed two weeks ago, the November 2026 Patch Tuesday security updates may also break printing for users trying to print documents with complex formatting on server (Windows Server 2016 or later) and client (Windows 10 or later) devices.

Microsoft unveiled Windows Autopatch in April 2022, saying it would be available for free to customers with a Windows 10/11 Enterprise E3 license or greater starting in July 2022. Windows Autopatch was made available in public preview in early June 2022 and reached general availability in July 2022.

In March, it announced that it would turn on hotpatch security updates by default for all eligible Windows devices managed through Microsoft Intune and the Graph API on systems with the May 2026 Windows security updates installed.

One month later, Microsoft fixed a Windows Autopatch bug that deployed driver updates restricted by administrative policies on some Autopatch-managed Windows devices belonging to customers across the European Union.

Windows 11 KB5132057 update released with 28 changes and fixes

Microsoft says November security updates cause printing issues

Microsoft testing fix for Windows 11 Bluetooth audio quality drops

Microsoft now automatically installs Windows 11 26H2 on unmanaged devices

[17] Security Affairs - Cleverbridge Confirms Attackers Copied Payment Card Data From Its Platform
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 19:14:17 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203851/data-breach/cleverbridge-confirms-attackers-copied-payment-card-data-from-its-platform.html

[TAM METİN - 583 kelime]
Cleverbridge Confirms Attackers Copied Payment Card Data From Its Platform

Cleverbridge confirms attackers stole payment card numbers, expiry dates and CVV codes, warning customers to watch for fraudulent transactions.

Cleverbridge, which runs checkouts, payments and subscriptions for other companies, has confirmed that attackers copied payment card data from its platform. The company says it found the problem on 4 November and started notifying affected people on 20 November. That’s a gap of 16 days.

“On 4 November 2026, Cleverbridge detected unauthorised activity in our platform and took immediate steps to contain the incident.”  reads the incident update published by the company.  “Whilst our investigation remains ongoing, regretfully, we have been able to determine that a third party copied payment card data stored on our platform.”

Affected systems went offline for a short time and were back online the same day. Only later did the investigation show that a third party had copied payment card data stored on the platform, and the company says that the investigation is still running.

The company’s website lists customers such as SUSE, Red Hat and Parallels, but the company has not said which businesses were affected.

If you bought a product or service from a company that uses Cleverbridge, it may have handled your payment and stored your card details. Cleverbridge provides payment and subscription services to software and SaaS companies.

Cleverbridge has not clearly explained what information was stolen on its public incident page. It says the exposed data may include payment card details and directs affected customers to check their individual notifications. However, a notification reviewed by BleepingComputer provides more details: the stolen information may include card numbers, expiration dates and CVV or CVC security codes.

That last item is the one that matters. The card industry’s own rules generally bar companies from keeping the security code once a payment is authorised, so BleepingComputer asked Cleverbridge why the codes were accessible at all.

The company did not disclose technical details about the attack, however, its answer excluded a ransomware attack.

“None of our systems were encrypted.” continues the update.

Cleverbridge says it hired TransUnion/Cyberscout, a third-party vendor, to support the notifications, and that a notice sent on its behalf is legitimate. It has offered credit or identity monitoring where available, with sign-up details in the notice. Asked why people weren’t told sooner, it says it moved fast and notified them without undue delay.

Cleverbridge advises users to watch for phishing attempts and warns that it will never request card details, passwords or security codes by email, phone or text.

“As always, we recommend that our users follow general best online security practices, remain vigilant, and be aware of phishing attempts.” the company states. “Out of an abundance of caution, please be cautious of unexpected emails, calls, or messages about this incident. Cleverbridge will never ask you for your card details, passwords, or security codes by email, phone, or text message. ”

Call your card issuer sooner rather than later. Cleverbridge says the issuer may decide to replace your card after you report the incident.

We recommend that you check your card statements carefully and tell your card issuer right away about any transaction that you do not recognise.” concludes the report. “You may also wish to contact your card issuer to tell them about this incident and follow any advice they give, as they may decide to replace your card. ”

[18] Security Affairs - Microsoft Entra Connect Health Breach Exposes Tenant Metadata and Admin Details
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 14:33:37 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203820/data-breach/microsoft-entra-connect-health-breach-exposes-tenant-metadata-and-admin-details.html

[TAM METİN - 438 kelime]
Microsoft Entra Connect Health Breach Exposes Tenant Metadata and Admin Details

Microsoft Entra Connect Health breach exposed tenant metadata and admin details, potentially enabling targeted phishing and social-engineering attacks.

Microsoft has disclosed a data breach involving its Entra Connect Health service, which exposed tenant metadata and administrator contact details.

“On 9 November 2026, we detected a vulnerability in the Microsoft Entra Connect Health API that was introduced on 23 July 2026. The vulnerability could allow an unauthorized party to access tenant and service-monitoring metadata and potentially make changes to Connect Health monitoring configuration.” the company wrote on Reddit.

Microsoft Entra Connect Health is a cloud service that helps administrators monitor the health, performance and alerts of their on-premises identity systems. The IT giant says no passwords, authentication tokens or valid cryptographic keys were compromised, but the exposed information could still help attackers craft convincing phishing and social-engineering attacks.

The issue came from a vulnerability in the Entra Connect Health API, introduced on July 23, 2026. Microsoft identified the flaw on November 9 and deployed a server-side fix on November 19.

“We deployed and fixed the vulnerability on 19 November 2026. On 20 November 2026, we concluded our investigation and determined that unauthorized read-only access to tenant-level metadata associated with your organization had occurred. We did not identify any unauthorized configuration changes.” the IT giant added.

Customers do not need to install a software update because Microsoft addressed the problem on its side.

The attackers could only view the data, but the exposure still created risks. They gained access to administrator email addresses, tenant IDs, domain names and synchronisation status. Attackers could use this information to plan targeted attacks and impersonate Microsoft support staff, service providers or internal IT administrators.

Microsoft has not disclosed how many tenants were affected. However, several administrators and managed service providers have reported receiving notifications from the company, suggesting that the breach may have affected many customers.

Microsoft says no software update or customer remediation is needed because the Entra Connect Health API vulnerability has been fixed. However, it recommends several precautionary steps. Organisations should alert security and IT teams to possible targeted phishing and social-engineering attempts, ensure administrator accounts use phishing-resistant MFA, and apply Privileged Identity Management for just-in-time access.

They should also run Microsoft’s read-only PowerShell script to review potentially exposed metadata, check Connect Health settings, and examine Entra audit logs, sign-in logs and Azure Activity Logs for suspicious activity. Microsoft advises verifying the security notice directly through Azure Service Health rather than clicking email links.

[19] Security Affairs - Bulgaria Discovers Massive Government Breach After Five Years of Undetected Spying
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 09:46:56 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203794/intelligence/bulgaria-discovers-massive-government-breach-after-five-years-of-undetected-spying.html

[TAM METİN - 576 kelime]
Bulgaria Discovers Massive Government Breach After Five Years of Undetected Spying

Bulgaria uncovered a five-year cyberespionage campaign that stole 130GB of government files. The attacker and full impact remain unknown.

Bulgaria ‘s ministry for innovation and digital transformation announced it had uncovered a hacking operation running since 2021. Reuters reports that more than 130 GB of documents, about 62 million pages, were downloaded from government databases over those five years. Minister Ivan Vassilev said there’s no evidence so far that Bulgarian citizens’ personal data was compromised.

“The minister, Ivan Vassilev, said that since 2021 more than 130 GB, ​or 62 million pages, of documents had ​been downloaded, but that there was no evidence ⁠so far that Bulgarians’ personal data had been compromised, ​Nova TV reported on its website.” Reuters reports.

The breach came to light only after Bulgaria introduced a new national cybersecurity monitoring system, which spotted suspicious activity in August. Until then, the country apparently had no system in place capable of detecting an intrusion that had been going on since 2021. This raises questions not only about the attackers’ capabilities but also about the lack of security monitoring inside government networks.

Several international hacking groups may be behind the attack, but investigators haven’t linked it to any specific country or nation-state actor. The stolen files reportedly include cabinet documents, reports and communications between ministries and government agencies.

“We discovered that several international hacker groups had accessed Bulgarian systems starting in 2021,” Vassilev said. “The information they stole included sensitive details of how Bulgarian state institutions operate”

“At this stage, there is no evidence that the personal data of Bulgarian citizens has been affected,” the minister added.

After the attack was discovered, several agencies joined the response, including intelligence services, the defense ministry, police, cybersecurity experts and international partners such as the EU’s cybersecurity agency. Authorities have isolated the affected systems, blocked the attackers’ access and removed the malware. Prosecutors have also been informed. Vassilev wants them to investigate not only the hackers but also whether government agencies failed to detect the intrusion earlier.

The government also has questions to answer. Bulgaria went through several unstable governments and repeated elections during the five years the attackers remained inside its systems. This political instability may have affected long-term investment in cybersecurity. An attack that went unnoticed from 2021 to 2026 doesn’t only raise questions about the attackers’ skills. It also highlights how weak security monitoring allowed them to stay hidden for so long.

This isn’t first time Bulgaria faced a major breach, and the contrast is worth noting. In 2019, hackers stole roughly 11 GB of data from the National Revenue Agency, exposing personal details on nearly five million citizens, about 71% of the population at the time, and a Russian link was floated before a 20-year-old local suspect was arrested. This time the target was institutional correspondence rather than citizen records, and the scale of documents taken, 130 GB against 11 GB, dwarfs that earlier case.

What’s missing from Wednesday’s announcement is almost everything that would let the public judge the real damage: who did this, what specific documents went out the door, and whether any of it has already been used. Bulgaria’s own cybersecurity agency, BTA, described the find as “Government Uncovers Apparent Years-Long Breach of Administrative Networks”, language that tells you an investigation is still very much open, not closed.
