[20] Security Affairs - U.S. Banks Struggle With Outages Amid Wider Technology Disruptions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Sun, 22 Nov 2026 13:22:46 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203272/security/u-s-banks-struggle-with-outages-amid-wider-technology-disruptions.html

[TAM METİN - 948 kelime]
U.S. Banks Struggle With Outages Amid Wider Technology Disruptions

U.S. banks suffered widespread outages, disrupting online banking and payments and raising concerns about technology dependencies and resilience.

A wave of service disruptions affected several major U.S. banks on Monday, November 16, leaving customers unable to access online banking, check account balances or complete financial transactions. The problems also affected digital payments, including Zelle, raising questions about how much financial services depend on the availability of interconnected technology platforms.

According to CBS News, the affected institutions included JPMorgan Chase, Bank of America, U.S. Bank, Wells Fargo, Capital One and Truist. “Customers at Bank of America, JPMorgan Chase, U.S. Bank and other major financial institutions reported service outages on Monday, according to Downdetector.com.”

“Other banks seeing a spike in customer complaints about difficulty accessing their accounts include Capital One, Truist and Wells Fargo, while users also reported problems with the digital payments app Zelle, according to the outage tracker.” states CBS News.

Reports began appearing on Monday morning and increased throughout the day. By 2 p.m. Eastern Time, Downdetector had received nearly 4,800 reports concerning Chase alone, making it the bank with the highest number of reported problems.

Customers described difficulties logging into their accounts, checking balances, transferring money and completing wire transactions. Bank of America customers also reported problems with ATMs, while U.S. Bank users experienced issues with online banking and its mobile application. Some Wells Fargo customers couldn’t log in or access transaction information.

The disruption wasn’t limited to online services. A Chase customer in Texas told CBS News that he couldn’t deposit a check at a Dallas branch because the bank’s system was unavailable. The incident illustrates how a technical failure can affect both digital channels and ordinary banking operations.

“Chase Bank’s services are experiencing a technical bug issue, not a cyber issue, a spokesperson said Monday.” CBS News continues.

The bank explained that some transactions were taking longer than usual, while customers could still access their accounts and funds. It said it was working to resolve the problem.

Zelle also attracted attention as customers reported difficulties sending and receiving payments. However, the company said its network was operating normally and pointed to possible problems at participating financial institutions. This distinction matters because Zelle doesn’t maintain separate customer accounts or hold their funds. Instead, participating banks and credit unions manage the underlying accounts and transactions.

Its network operator, Early Warning Services, is owned by several major financial institutions, including Bank of America, Capital One, JPMorgan Chase, PNC Bank, Truist, U.S. Bank and Wells Fargo. This structure highlights how a disruption affecting one part of the banking infrastructure can potentially create problems across services that rely on participating institutions.

The banking problems came amid a particularly difficult week for U.S. technology infrastructure. On November 12, Microsoft 365 suffered a widespread outage that disrupted Outlook, Teams and other services. The following day, a technical failure in the Federal Aviation Administration’s communications infrastructure triggered a nationwide ground stop, suspending departing flights for several hours. Workday, a major provider of human resources and payroll software, also experienced service disruptions on November 13. Fox News documented the sequence of incidents, while Newsweek also reported the growing number of technology disruptions.

The timing has inevitably raised concerns about whether these incidents might share a common cause, particularly given the importance of Microsoft services, telecommunications and third-party technology providers to critical business operations. However, the available information doesn’t establish a connection between the incidents, and there is no evidence that the banking disruptions were caused by a coordinated cyberattack.

Microsoft attributed its earlier service interruption to a technical problem rather than malicious activity.

For security professionals, the incidents offer a useful reminder: service availability is a security and operational resilience issue, even when no attacker is involved. Financial institutions rely on complex chains of applications, infrastructure providers, telecommunications networks and external services. A failure at one point can affect customers far beyond the organization where the original problem occurred.

The distinction between a cyberattack and a technical failure is important, but it shouldn’t lead organizations to underestimate the consequences. Business continuity plans, dependency mapping, alternative communication channels, incident response procedures and clear customer communication all matter when essential services become unavailable.

A system doesn’t need to be compromised to become unavailable, and customers don’t care much about the distinction when they can’t access their money.

Update November 17, 2026

A technical issue at JPMorgan Chase, caused by a software bug in an on-premise hardware system, disrupted online banking and delayed Zelle payments on Monday, affecting customers across several major U.S. banks. Chase ruled out a cyberattack and said it was working to restore services. Other banks reported no direct problems with their own systems, although transactions involving Chase were delayed.

“Chase Bank’s services were experiencing a technical bug issue, not a cyber issue, a spokesperson said Monday.” reports CBS.

“Due to a technical issue, some transactions are taking longer than usual to complete but customers still have access to their accounts and funds,” a Chase spokesperson said in a statement. “We are working to resolve this as quickly as possible. Chase also said in a social media post that a software bug in its systems affected other institutions “when we were party to a transaction involving other banks.” The issue is not systemic, the bank added.”

JPMorganChase has experienced a temporary issue related to a software bug in on-premise hardware systems – there is no evidence this is cyber-related.  It is taking longer than usual to service some customers.  Where we were party to a transaction involving other banks, those…

[21] Security Affairs - Google GTIG Warns AI Agents Automate Exploits, Steal Credentials and Scale Cybercrime
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Sun, 22 Nov 2026 13:22:46 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203260/hacking/google-gtig-warns-ai-agents-automate-exploits-steal-credentials-and-scale-cybercrime.html

[TAM METİN - 1344 kelime]
Google GTIG Warns AI Agents Automate Exploits, Steal Credentials and Scale Cybercrime

Google finds attackers using autonomous AI agents to automate exploits, steal AI access and scale intrusions, often sacrificing stealth for speed.

Google’s Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) says attackers have moved past typing prompts into a chatbot. They now run autonomous, multi-agent frameworks such as ARTEX, CAIRN, CyberStrikeAI and PentAGI to carry out multi-stage intrusions faster.

“Adversary tradecraft has continued to transition beyond manual prompting to deploying autonomous multi-agent offensive frameworks—such as ARTEX, CAIRN, CyberStrikeAI, and PentAGI—to execute multi-stage intrusions at greater operational velocity.” reads the GTIG report. “Threat actors are increasingly leveraging LLMs and agentic frameworks to fully automate the reverse engineering of patches via differential analysis, allowing them to rapidly weaponize n-day vulnerabilities and scale exploitation pipelines against internet-facing servers without human intervention.”

The basic attack methods have not changed. What has changed is how quickly attackers can use them. These tools still rely on stolen credentials and systems that have not been patched. Amazon reached the same conclusion after monitoring its own decoy systems: None of the techniques in these campaigns were new. The difference is that AI agents can combine these techniques and carry out several steps without stopping.

These frameworks have three main capabilities. They can find and exploit simple vulnerabilities, ask an LLM to create custom payloads when needed, and change their approach based on the results they get. GTIG observed CAIRN and PentAGI being used in real attacks, while CyberStrikeAI and HexStrike AI were still being tested. CAIRN allows different AI agents to work together and share information through a common workspace. GTIG found evidence of its use against several targets, including organizations in the US aerospace sector.

CyberStrikeAI connects commercial and open-weight AI models to more than 100 security tools. HexStrike AI gives LLM-based clients access to over 150 tools, managed by more than 12 specialized agents. PentAGI is designed for less experienced operators. Between late August and early October, one attacker used a modified version of the framework to systematically scan organizations across Asia.

A China-nexus group also appears to be building its own framework. Others use ZeroHollow, an inference service for exploit development and routing, and Agent Zero, an open source agent framework that runs code on its own. Google expects the trend to continue, since the barrier to entry is low and adding exploits is easy.

“We have observed several threat actors separately experimenting with the use of these frameworks and harnesses, including financially motivated, as well as suspected espionage threat actors with a PRC nexus. This includes evidence that a PRC-nexus group was developing its own automated attack framework for likely use in cyber espionage operations.” continues the report. “These observations additionally extend to the use of other tools and services, such as ZeroHollow, an inference service used for exploit development and LLM routing, and Agent Zero, an open source agent framework which features autonomous code execution and dynamic tool generation.”

Vulnerability research is moving the same way. For the first time, GTIG saw attackers automate discovery and exploitation with no human in the loop. An exposed staging server hosted a framework called exploit_agent that compares popular web software before and after patches, drafts exploits and runs them. Victim data suggested interest in French media, entertainment and education sites, and the configuration notes were in Russian and told the operator to put the API keys in the file, not in the chat.

Once a vulnerability is patched, attackers have more information to work with. They can study the affected code, release notes, security research and proof-of-concept exploits to find ways to attack systems that have not yet been updated. Between January and August 2026, the number of vulnerability disclosures and exploits nearly doubled. Zero-day exploits also increased, from an average of 8 per month in 2025 to 11 in 2026, before reaching 22 in August.

The PaperCut case shows how quickly attackers can move. Two flaws were exploited as zero-days in late August, and Huntress and other researchers developed working exploits within 24 hours. GreyNoise detected an attack campaign just four days after the flaws were disclosed. In one case, attackers went from gaining access to obtaining domain administrator privileges within minutes.

The same pattern appeared with two PHP vulnerabilities, CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619. Researchers used LLMs to analyse the flaws, and details about the patched code and a proof-of-concept video appeared before the October 21 patch. Hundreds of exploit claims then emerged, but many did not work. Defenders had to spend time checking which reports were genuine.

LLMjacking is the money side. Attackers steal LLM credentials and cloud resources, then resell access. Mandiant handled incidents where fraudulent charges passed $100,000 in a day, and the worst reached $1 million before cleanup.

They got in with credentials from public GitHub repositories and fake sites that harvest session tokens. One actor used CVE-2026-40217, a LiteLLM sandbox escape that turns limited keys into admin credentials. Some created and deleted short-lived service accounts to dodge rate limits, and others started powerful GPU instances. In one case two attackers used the same credentials, and a botnet made millions of API calls in about 24 hours before they were revoked.

The loot goes to “LLM mixers,” storefronts that route buyers through pools of stolen credentials and compute.

“Threat actors frequently monetize LLMJacking intrusions by selling access to compromised compute and LLM resources. In addition to the rise of LLMJacking, GTIG is tracking the proliferation of “LLM Mixers”—illicit storefronts that provide buyers with unauthorized access to LLM services and underlying proxy infrastructure.” continues the report. “This infrastructure routes buyer traffic through a pool of stolen credentials and compute instances to ensure reliable access, while also offering the ability to spread query volume across multiple victim environments to reduce the likelihood of detection.”

GTIG saw prices from $0.10 to $25 per million tokens on Russian- and Chinese-language markets. APIMart multiplies prices by five above 100,000 tokens, and JetNight sells monthly subscriptions. Even criminals have a surcharge for long prompts.

Information operations now use LLMs as standard tools, and GTIG says so with high confidence. That covers state groups tied to Russia, China and Iran, commercial influence vendors and profit-driven networks pumping out political clickbait. Better content doesn’t automatically mean more impact, and profit networks can look like state campaigns.

Nation-state actors use LLMs for productivity. The Iranian actor CALANQUE ION, formerly APT42, uses them for reconnaissance and social engineering, and Chinese actors use them for research, scanning and exploits. LEAKY CASTLE analyzes stolen data with them, others stage and exfiltrate through LLM platform file hosting, and North Korean IT workers use them for resumes and interviews.

MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE shows the creative end. Fake recruiter profiles with SynthID watermarks led targets to ClickFix lures, and the attackers then edited .claude/settings.json in internal repositories so that starting Claude Code downloaded the SOMBERMEME backdoor. They also named access tokens after Microsoft Copilot. The Jieyou phishing service added an LLM chatbot in June so low-skilled affiliates can edit phishing panels in plain language.

AI tools also have limitations that attackers must consider. They can generate too much noise, make up details and even select the wrong targets. Groups focused on espionage are therefore likely to use them with caution, while cybercrime groups may accept a lower success rate in exchange for reaching more victims. Google SecOps has released detection rules for ARTEX, and Mandiant has published a guide to help organizations secure their systems. Our own analysis makes a similar point: Many ARTEX “indicators” found on compromised systems are generated by the LLM or by the tools it uses, rather than being built into the framework.

This makes attacker behavior more useful to defenders than looking for a single string or signature. When these frameworks run autonomously without tight controls, they leave traces in network traffic, recognizable identifiers and the code they execute. Google’s assessment offers some room for defenders to act: these frameworks currently trade stealth for velocity.

(SecurityAffairs – hacking, AI Agents)

[22] Security Affairs - Cognitive warfare: the new battlefield is the human mind
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Sun, 22 Nov 2026 13:22:46 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203250/cyber-warfare-2/cognitive-warfare-the-new-battlefield-is-the-human-mind.html

[TAM METİN - 977 kelime]
Cognitive warfare: the new battlefield is the human mind

Cognitive warfare uses AI, deepfakes and fear to manipulate opinions, erode trust and disrupt society.

Contemporary warfare is no longer fought solely through armies, weapons, and technological infrastructure. Alongside military operations and cyberattacks, a new dimension of conflict is emerging in which the primary target is people’s ability to interpret reality, distinguish reliable information from manipulated information, and make autonomous decisions. This is the context in which cognitive warfare takes root, a strategy that aims to influence the perceptions, beliefs, and behaviors of individuals and communities, transforming information into a tool of pressure and destabilization.

Unlike traditional propaganda, cognitive warfare leverages a much more complex technological ecosystem. Social networks, digital platforms, profiling systems, artificial intelligence, and psychological manipulation techniques allow for reaching different audiences with messages crafted to elicit specific reactions. The goal is not necessarily to impose a single narrative, but to influence the process by which people acquire information, form opinions, and evaluate events.

Multiplier of information operations and the manipulation of consensus

Generative artificial intelligence has further expanded the operational possibilities of these campaigns. The automated production of text, images, audio, and video allows for the construction of increasingly credible fake content, simulating statements from public figures, and fueling networks of coordinated accounts. Deepfakes pose a significant problem for information security because they can compromise the reliability of audiovisual evidence and make it more difficult to verify the authenticity of a communication. A significant finding emerges from the fourth annual report of the European External Action Service on FIMI (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference) threats , published in March 2026. In 2025, 27% of incidents analyzed by the EEAS (European External Action Service) involved techniques attributable to artificial intelligence, with an increase in cases from 41 in 2024 to 147 in 2025, equal to approximately 259% . Artificial intelligence is being used to accelerate content production, translate and adapt messages into multiple languages, generate synthetic images and videos, and amplify influencer efforts through coordinated networks.

In this context, social media platforms represent one of the most favorable environments for the spread of cognitive operations. The speed of content circulation, recommendation systems, and the ability to reach specific communities allow specific narratives to be amplified, even through coordinated behavior. Fake accounts, bots, and seemingly independent profiles can contribute to the impression that a given opinion is widely shared. One of the most insidious strategies isn’t necessarily spreading completely false news, but rather mixing authentic information, manipulated content, and misleading interpretations. This makes it more difficult to distinguish facts from reconstructions constructed to influence the public.

School bomb scares as an example of psychological pressure

A concrete example that provides insight into the psychological dimension of these threats is the bomb threat that affected schools in Italy, Scotland, Northern Ireland, and the Republic of Ireland on November 11, 2026. In Italy, approximately 6,300 schools received communications containing attack threats, prompting evacuations in hundreds of schools and mobilizing law enforcement and emergency personnel. Authorities deemed the reports to be false alarms, with no devices identified.

The incident deserves attention not only for its international dimension, but above all for the mechanism by which an electronic communication produced concrete consequences. It was not necessary to compromise the schools’ computer networks or damage infrastructure. Sending a single message was enough to activate emergency procedures, interrupt school activities, generate concern among families, and commit public resources. The human factor thus becomes the primary factor through which the threat’s impact materializes. School administrators, teachers, and law enforcement responded in accordance with their responsibilities, while parents and students were exposed to a situation of great uncertainty. Even when the alarm turns out to be unfounded, the psychological and organizational consequences remain real. This episode demonstrates how manipulating the perception of danger can influence collective behavior.

The connection between cognitive warfare and cybersecurity

Cognitive warfare has significant parallels with cybersecurity and hybrid operations. A ransomware attack against strategic infrastructure, a breach of public administration systems, or the theft of confidential documents can have a significance that transcends the technical damage. The stolen information can be selected, manipulated, or released at strategic moments to compromise the reputation of institutions and influence public debate. In this scenario, cybersecurity is not just about protecting networks, devices, and data, but also the ability to manage manipulated information and communications designed to generate fear, disorientation, and mistrust. Countering cognitive warfare requires a multidisciplinary approach. Protecting digital infrastructures must be accompanied by the ability to recognize information manipulation, verify sources, and understand the techniques used to influence people’s behavior. For companies and public administrations, it is becoming important to integrate cyber incident management with timely and transparent communication.

Cognitive warfare demonstrates that a country’s security does not depend solely on the protection of its information systems or the availability of military capabilities. It also depends on trust in institutions, the quality of information, and the population’s ability to recognize attempts at manipulation. The case of the school bomb threats highlights a fundamental aspect: it’s not always necessary to physically attack an infrastructure to compromise its functioning. In certain circumstances, it’s sufficient to exploit fear and human reactions to generate disorganization and social pressure. The new battlefield, therefore, isn’t just digital. It’s also cognitive, and concerns the ability of individuals and institutions to maintain clarity, trust, and decision-making autonomy in the face of information designed to destabilize them.

[23] Security Affairs - German Military Grounds Quantum Systems Drones Following Reported Cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Sun, 22 Nov 2026 13:22:46 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203085/cyber-crime/german-military-grounds-quantum-systems-drones-following-reported-cyberattack.html

[TAM METİN - 704 kelime]
German Military Grounds Quantum Systems Drones Following Reported Cyberattack

German drone maker Quantum Systems confirms a cyberattack that disrupted operations, with reports pointing to a malicious software update.

Quantum Systems, a German defense company that builds reconnaissance and interceptor drones and the software that controls them, confirmed an incident in late October.

The company says its security teams quickly contained the threat while working with government authorities. It also points out that the incident does not pose a risk to customers or operators using its drones.

The company reported the incident to the relevant authorities, and an investigation is still underway with the help of internal and external cybersecurity experts.

“Quantum Systems identified a cybersecurity incident at the end of October 2026. An immediate internal investigation revealed an active cyberattack by hostile actors. The threat was contained thanks to the swift response of our IT and security teams in close and transparent collaboration with the authorities.” reads the company’s statement on cyber incident. “Through the quick measures taken and the characteristics of the incident, there is no risk in operating Quantum Systems’ unmanned systems for the end users and the operators.”

The manufacturer did not disclose details technical details about the security incident.

Quantum Systems added that European defense companies have faced ongoing cyberattacks for years, particularly those supporting Ukraine. It believes this support makes them attractive targets and says it has security measures in place to protect its systems and data.

“The European defence industry has been facing persistent cyberattacks for years. Companies at the forefront of supporting Ukraine are being targeted precisely because our work is critically relevant to customers’ security.” concludes the statement. “Being aware of this responsibility, Quantum Systems has mitigation plans in place and strives to fulfill the highest standard of data and system security.”

According to Der Spiegel and the Financial Times, citing internal sources, attackers may have used a malicious update file for QBase, Quantum Systems’ software for controlling drones and planning missions. This suggests that the attackers may have compromised the company’s update server in a supply chain attack. The incident is particularly significant because compromising the software distribution mechanism of a drone manufacturer can potentially affect the systems used by military and other customers, even without directly compromising the drones themselves.

Der Spiegel also reports that QBase is used to plan missions for the Vector reconnaissance drone, known as Falke in the German military.

QBase is Quantum Systems’ ground-control and mission-planning software for its unmanned aerial vehicles (drones). It runs on Windows computers and is used by drone operators to plan, execute, monitor and analyze missions. QBase is not simply an application for viewing drone footage. It is part of the operational infrastructure used to prepare and manage drone missions.

An unexpected software update reached customers and raised alarms inside the company. It is not clear how many customers installed it. Reports also say that the German military grounded Quantum Systems drones as a precaution and excluded them from a recent exercise. Military counterintelligence (MAD), Germany’s domestic intelligence service (BfV), and the federal cybersecurity agency (BSI) are reportedly investigating the incident.

However, these details have not been officially confirmed, and no attacker has been identified.

The incident highlights a major security risk for companies supplying software to defense and industrial customers: attackers can target not only the products themselves but also the systems used to distribute software updates. If hackers compromise an update server, they may be able to deliver malicious files to customers who trust the vendor and install its updates.

For this reason, companies must protect their software distribution infrastructure as carefully as their production systems. They should digitally sign updates, verify their integrity before installation, restrict access to update servers and monitor any unusual activity. Customers should also be able to independently verify updates before deploying them.

Another important measure is to have a way to quickly stop or roll back an update if suspicious activity is detected. In critical environments, such as military operations and industrial control systems, a compromised update can have consequences that go far beyond data theft, potentially disrupting equipment, operations and essential services.

[24] Recorded Future - What are threat intelligence feeds?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/what-are-threat-intelligence-feeds

[TAM METİN - 2386 kelime]
What are Threat Intelligence Feeds?

What are threat intelligence feeds, and why do they matter for your cyber defenses? These real-time data streams are vital for pre-empting cyberattacks, providing the latest information on potential threats.

By informing you of dangers before they strike, threat intelligence feeds are your first line of digital defense. This article will explore their types, impacts, and integration with your cybersecurity measures.

Threat intelligence feeds provide real-time data on cyber threats, enabling security teams to quickly identify, understand, and mitigate threats by offering actionable intelligence including IOCs, TTPs, and context for the most pressing issues.

Through proactive threat detection and response, threat intelligence feeds are integral to enhancing an organization’s security posture, ensuring early identification and mitigation of threats, thereby helping develop refined defensive strategies and improve decision-making.

Effective use of threat intelligence feeds involves integrating them with an organization’s existing security tools and infrastructure, prioritizing threats to allocate resources efficiently, and continuously evaluating them to maintain quality, reliability, and cost-effectiveness.

Breaking down threat intelligence feeds

Threat intelligence feeds provide a constant flow of data that offers up-to-the-minute details about cyber threats worldwide. These feeds gather information from various sources, including security experts, government agencies, and open-source intelligence.

The data is processed and analyzed, transforming raw information into actionable threat intelligence. This actionable intelligence includes indicators of compromise (IOCs), tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) used by threat actors. This threat intel is invaluable to security teams, enabling them to identify, understand, and mitigate threats swiftly.

Moreover, threat intel feeds play a pivotal role by providing automated streams of useful threat information, including indicators of compromise (IoCs), information on threat actors, suspicious domains and IP addresses, malware hashes, and more, which are essential for maintaining an organization’s security posture.

TechTarget highlights the value of threat intel feeds by stating:

And as Cloudflare warns:

The effectiveness and utility of threat intelligence feeds rely on the context they offer. Without context, the data might seem overwhelming and challenging to interpret. However, with the right context, security teams can focus on the most pressing threats, thus enhancing their response time and efficiency.

What are the key components of threat intelligence feeds?

Effective threat intelligence feeds share common attributes. They offer:

Real-time updates: ensuring quick incident response and timely adjustments to security policies

A proven track record of accuracy: outdated or incorrect information can compromise security efforts, making accuracy a crucial component

Relevance to either incident response or strategic planning: often determined by whether it offers real-time insights versus long-term trends.

Contextual analysis in threat intelligence feeds is another key component. It provides insights into the origins and impact of threats, aiding security teams in crafting appropriate response and mitigation strategies. Furthermore, effective threat intel feeds must present relevant data, streamlining security investigation and response. This curation saves valuable time for security teams.

Finally, the quality and industry relevance of the sources contributing to threat intelligence feeds are vital to the utility and effectiveness of the received intelligence.

What are the four types of threat intelligence feeds?

Threat intelligence feeds are primarily categorized into four types:

Commercial feeds: These collect anonymized customer metadata and analyze it to provide threat intelligence. They often come from reputable cybersecurity firms and provide comprehensive coverage of the threat landscape.

Community-based feeds: These are collaborative efforts where individuals and organizations share threat intelligence with each other. They can be valuable for niche or specific threat information.

Government and Non-Governmental Organization (NGO) Threat Intelligence Feeds: governments and NGOs provide threat intelligence feeds, sometimes for free or at a cost, which include platforms for sharing threat data among entities, like the Department of Homeland Security’s Automated Indicator Sharing or the FBI’s InfraGard project. These feeds are valuable for staying informed about cyber threats but relying solely on them can result in a limited perspective of the threat landscape.

Each type has its unique characteristics and advantages.

Why are cyber threat intel feeds important?

Threat intelligence feeds play a pivotal role in transforming reactive security efforts into proactive ones. By providing real-time insights into potential cyberattacks, these feeds enable early identification and response, thereby preventing breaches.

The information these feeds offer enhances the ability of security teams to:

Develop refined defensive strategies

Gain insights into the tactics, techniques, and procedures of adversaries

Create a clear picture of the threat landscape

Furthermore, threat feeds empower security teams to make faster and more informed decisions, backed by data. This data-driven approach significantly bolsters an organization’s cybersecurity. With a broader understanding of the cyber threat landscape, organizations can better assess and prioritize risks, ensuring resources are allocated to the most pressing ones.

Enhancing security posture

One of the significant benefits of threat feeds is their capacity to enhance an organization’s security posture. By offering actionable intelligence, these feeds empower security teams to gain a deeper understanding of threats and fine-tune their protection strategies. However, to fully harness the power of these feeds, organizations need to evaluate their existing security posture, understand their industry’s inherent risks, and consider the compatibility of the feeds with their current security tools.

An effective way to improve response times to cyber threats is through security automation. By automating responses to alerts from threat intelligence feeds, organizations can act swiftly against cyber threats, thereby preventing potential data breaches.

Proactive threat detection and response

Threat intelligence feeds are the cornerstone of proactive threat detection and response. They enable security teams to:

Address security threats before they escalate into major issues

Proactively identify and mitigate threats

Understand the tactics used by threat actors

Guide security policies

Identify vulnerabilities

Inform access permissions and security updates

Cybersecurity professionals utilize threat intel to stay one step ahead of potential cybersecurity threats and protect their systems and data.

Threat intelligence feeds provide essential information on emerging threats and share best practices for responding, which significantly supports incident response efforts. With real-time threat data, security teams are promptly informed of potential issues, reducing the likelihood and costs of significant data breaches.

Where threat intelligence data comes in

Leveraging threat intelligence data effectively is a critical process that involves integrating feeds into existing security tools and prioritizing threats to allocate resources efficiently. The actionability of threat intelligence data is determined by the level of context provided and its capacity to be readily used in correlation with other security tools and platforms.

Curated threat intel feeds compile data from various sources, including public and private repositories, allowing security teams to save time and enhance their investigative and response efforts.

Integration with security tools

Integrating threat intel feeds with existing security tools can:

Extend these tools’ useful life

Improve their return on investment

Minimize alert fatigue in security operations centers

Provide filtered, prioritized alerts for better management

Incorporate both third-party and local threat intelligence into existing security solutions

Expand the slate of covered threats

Enhance the identification and blocking of threats

Advanced Threat Intelligence (ATI) tools that employ algorithms to rapidly identify anomalies often assist in this integration. The process is further supported by the availability of integrations such as APIs or native third-party integrations that simplify ingestion into the current security infrastructure.

Prioritizing threats and allocating resources

Threat intelligence data facilitates the creation of metrics that help organizations quantify and rank threats. This process effectively prioritizes critical vulnerabilities, ensuring resources are allocated to the most pressing threats. Classifying high-risk activities and incidents using threat intelligence data supports the strategic allocation of cybersecurity resources and enhances strategic threat intelligence.

Incorporating threat intelligence into incident response mechanisms allows organizations to:

Respond to threats both swiftly and strategically

Strategize the deployment of rules and Indicators of Compromise (IoCs) in Security Operations Centers (SOCs)

Ensure that rules and IoCs are constantly updated and fine-tuned to keep up with evolving threats.

Threat intelligence feeds also provide vital data on vulnerabilities, exploits, and attack methods, which can be prioritized to improve patch management efforts within an organization.

How can I choose the right threat intelligence feed for my organization?

Choosing the right threat intelligence feed for your organization is a critical decision. It involves assessing quality, reliability, cost, and value, as well as considering the organization’s specific needs and budget constraints.

Criteria for evaluating threat intelligence feeds include the ability to:

update data in real time

access to multiple data sources

and the speed and format of the feed’s dissemination

To enhance the value of threat intelligence within an organization, it is important to evaluate the unique data each feed provides to prevent overlapping information and redundancy.

Integrating threat intelligence feeds requires analyzing their relevance to the organization’s threat landscape to ensure alignment with the specific threats it faces.

Assessing Quality and Reliability

The quality and reliability of threat intelligence feeds are evaluated based on trustworthiness and variety of information sources, range and depth of coverage, and if they offer contextual analysis and dashboarding capabilities. To ensure threat intelligence feeds are truly actionable, organizations should routinely monitor data reliability and confirm that the feed’s data sources are relevant to their industry’s specific threats.

Selecting high-fidelity threat intelligence feeds requires assessing vendors for their credibility and the historical reliability of their feeds. This assessment can be measured against their success rate in correlating with previous incidents.

Balancing Cost and Value

While many threat intelligence feeds are free to use, commercial feeds typically require an annual subscription that can range from a few thousand dollars to over $100,000 per year. Organizations should align their choice of threat intelligence feeds with the budget they have available for cybersecurity efforts.

To ensure cost-effectiveness, organizations need to evaluate whether a threat intelligence feed provides unique value that justifies its cost and avoids redundancy with other feeds.

Top Threat Intelligence Feeds to Consider

When it comes to threat intelligence feeds, there are numerous options to consider.

The Internet Storm Center: is recognized for providing thorough explanations of threats.

Spamhaus: specializes in email security and anti-spam, offering robust solutions in that domain.

URLhaus: primarily addresses IP address and domain name anomalies and is best suited for network operators, ISPs, CERTs, and domain registries.

AlienVault OTX feeds: are accessible through detailed dashboards, which provide Pulses to summarize threats and display various indicators of compromise.

Recorded Future Threat Intelligence: while not a traditional feed, our intelligence cloud platform represents a significant advancement in threat intelligence. We automate the curation of intelligence feeds, streamlining the process to help you identify and prioritize threats more efficiently.

When they first appeared, threat intelligence feeds represented a huge leap forward, enabling security professionals to manage more relevant information than ever before. As the cyber threat intelligence cycle evolved, it became apparent that the abundance of free feeds in particular became “noisy” and filled with errors and false positives. These issues, coupled with the sheer volume of data available, started to pose problems.

Instead of viewing dozens of feeds separately, using a threat intelligence platform not only combines them all but also curates and compares the internal telemetry, generating customized alerts for your incident response and threat intelligence team.

The most powerful intelligence platforms, like the Recorded Future Intelligence Cloud, automatically curate intelligence feeds, sifting through data to identify and prioritize threat intelligence for your organization to action.

How to implement threat intelligence feeds in your organization

Implementing threat intelligence feeds in an organization is a critical step towards enhancing cybersecurity. It involves integrating with the existing infrastructure, training and awareness, and ongoing evaluation and optimization. Before the implementation, it’s essential to assess the organization’s cybersecurity infrastructure and gain executive support.

Ensure that the current cybersecurity solutions, such as SIEM or EDR systems, can integrate with the selected threat intelligence feeds to function properly. Develop and deliver comprehensive training programs to educate staff on the operational procedures and benefits of threat intelligence feeds. Lastly, establish protocols for regular review and optimization of threat intelligence feeds to maintain their relevance and effectiveness.

Identify key stakeholders within the organization and involve them in planning the adoption of threat intelligence feeds.

Integration with Existing Infrastructure

Integrating threat intelligence feeds with existing tools can extend these tools’ useful life and improve their return on investment. Organizations should combine third-party threat intelligence with their own local intelligence sources for a more comprehensive coverage.

Training and Awareness

Training security teams is critical to leveraging threat intelligence feeds, ensuring they understand how to employ this information effectively. Security personnel must be trained to differentiate and respond efficiently to high-priority alerts to take timely action against critical threats.

Proper training includes measures to manage alert volume and prevent SOC alert fatigue-related burnout by focusing on significant, actionable alerts.

To further enhance your team’s capabilities, consider enrolling in Recorded Future University. Our free Intelligence Fundamentals course is designed to equip participants with the skills needed to effectively analyze and act on threat intelligence, turning information into actionable insights and strategic defenses.

How does a threat intelligence feed work?

A threat intelligence feed gathers information from different sources, processes, and analyzes it to provide actionable intelligence for cybersecurity purposes.

What are the benefits of using threat intelligence feeds?

Using threat intelligence feeds enables organizations to proactively identify and respond to cyber threats, enhance their security posture, and make informed cybersecurity decisions.

How can an organization implement a threat intelligence feed effectively?

To implement a threat intelligence feed effectively, an organization should integrate the feed with existing infrastructure, provide comprehensive staff training, and establish protocols for regular review and optimization of the feed. This will ensure a proactive and informed approach to cybersecurity.

What’s the difference between Threat Feeds vs Threat Intel Feeds?

Threat Feeds generally refer to raw data on emerging threats, whereas Threat Intel Feeds include analyzed data that offers actionable intelligence, crucial for crafting effective security strategies. Threat Intel Feeds produce refined reports that help in understanding the tactics and behaviors of threat actors.

Move beyond manual threat feeds with Recorded Future

To sum it up, threat intelligence feeds provide real-time insights into potential threats, enabling organizations to shift from a reactive to proactive approach. Implementing the right threat intelligence feed involves careful evaluation and selection, as well as effective integration with the existing infrastructure.

[25] Cyberscoop - Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 21:32:33 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/virginia-oregon-sue-spacex-grok-deepfakes/

[TAM METİN - 720 kelime]
Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM

Two new lawsuits accuse SpaceX and its subsidiaries of violating state consumer and trade laws by allowing its AI tool, Grok, to create deepfake nudes of millions of people earlier this year.

Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones and Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield, both Democrats, filed the lawsuits. They allege SpaceX and owner Elon Musk repeatedly failed to block Grok’s nudification feature after it became clear the tool was being used to break the law by creating nude or defamatory images of real people. The attorneys general want a court to bar SpaceX from advertising or profiting from the tool’s capabilities, pay restitution to victims and impose civil penalties.

“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false,” Jones said in a statement. “Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children. Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation. The law applies to everyone, and this office will hold big tech accountable when they break the law.”

The lawsuits say Grok launched when other AI companies already knew about the harms of deepfake pornography to adults and children. Many of those companies built safeguards to keep their models from generating such content. Musk, by contrast, is facing separate lawsuits from former victims of child sexual abuse material. Those plaintiffs allege Grok can easily nudify children because it was partly trained on real abuse material found online.

“Led by founder Elon Musk, Defendants chose to ignore this growing industry concern, and instead saw a market opportunity for more profits,” the lawsuit alleged. “To push its lagging product, Grok, to tens of millions of new users, it stripped out industry-standard safeguards and marketed a ‘spicy mode’ built to produce nudity.”

As CyberScoop reported last year, Musk’s public statements and decisions to maintain Grok’s capabilities were so brazen that cybersecurity and privacy lawyers predicted he would face an avalanche of criminal and civil actions. Over the past six months, lawsuits, federal and state investigations, and international regulatory scrutiny have battered the company.

The lawsuits allege that “weak safeguards were not a failure but a feature” of Musk’s business strategy for Grok. Those weak safeguards became one of Grok’s main selling points, marketed as a way to set it apart from other competing AI models.

The lawsuits cite Musk’s own behavior and statements as evidence that he knew about the controversy and often embraced it. He has posted or commented on Grok-altered images of himself and household objects in bikinis. At times, Musk has claimed Grok only alters images to show people in bikinis, or that deepfake pornography is protected free speech under the First Amendment.

The states also claim integrating Grok directly into the social media site X gave users the ability to create abuse material on command, as well as an instant, worldwide distribution platform for it.

Oregon’s lawsuit was brought under the Oregon Unlawful Trade Practices Act, which prohibits misrepresenting a product’s or service’s characteristics, benefits or qualities. The state alleges SpaceX violated the law “by failing to disclose Grok’s known material defects; and by employing unconscionable tactics in connection with selling and distributing Grok and X.”

Virginia’s lawsuit cites violation of the Virginia Consumer Protection Act, the state’s primary law, which prohibits businesses from misrepresenting the source, characteristics or quality of their goods or services.

CyberScoop has reached out to SpaceX for comment on the lawsuits.

[26] Cyberscoop - Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:37:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/hektor-avdyli-kosovo-credit-card-fraud-extradition/

[TAM METİN - 471 kelime]
Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US

A Kosovo man accused of selling stolen credit card and personal information over the internet, who was allegedly found with millions of payment card numbers in his possession, was extradited to the United States, the Justice Department announced Monday.

In October 2025, a federal grand jury in Wisconsin returned an eight-count indictment charging Hektor Avdyli, 21 — reportedly the nephew of a former member of Kosovo’s parliament — with wire fraud, access device fraud and aggravated identity theft.

Kosovar authorities arrested him in July at the behest of the FBI and DOJ, and he’s now in Milwaukee awaiting trial.

“Unfortunately, there is nothing remotely unusual about American consumers having their identities and financial information stolen online, often by foreign actors,” said First Assistant U.S. Attorney Brad Schimel for the Eastern District of Wisconsin. “It happens countless times every day, and that theft costs American consumers billions of dollars.”

Avdyli allegedly used online criminal forums to ply his illicit trade, advertising and selling stolen credit card data and personal information to help customers commit fraud. Court documents say that he claimed to steal the information from shopping websites using malware.

As part of the operation to apprehend him, the FBI secretly bought more than 27,000 credit card numbers and related personal information from a seller.  The bureau then identified Avdyli as the culprit through its investigative work.

A hard drive in his possession at the time of his arrest had 2.7 million payment card numbers on it, according to authorities.

“FBI Milwaukee worked across international borders to target the criminal actor behind a cybercrime scheme that enabled fraud against American people to include citizens of Wisconsin,” said Special-Agent-in-Charge Alan Karr of FBI Milwaukee.

[27] Cyberscoop - Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 16:59:03 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/kirsten-davies-jay-clayton-national-cyber-director/

[TAM METİN - 521 kelime]
Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job

President Donald Trump will nominate Kirsten Davies for permanent national cyber director, giving the acting role she just received last week for the post to Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton pending her confirmation.

A White House official told CyberScoop Monday that “following the overwhelming support Kirsten received from industry and across government after Thursday’s announcement, President Trump has decided to nominate her to serve as national cyber director.”

Davies, in the meantime, will serve as senior adviser for the Office of the National Cyber Director. The same person cannot serve in the acting role of a position while also being the nominee for it.

The move is one of several in a recent federal cyber leadership shuffle, all following the November elections that saw Democrats take control of the House and Senate.

The departure of Sean Cairncross as Trump’s national cyber director opened up his job. Davies was the chief information officer at the Department of Defense prior to receiving the acting national cyber director position, in addition to a background of holding leading cyber roles at big companies like Siemens and Estée Lauder.

Trump also nominated Palantir CISO Michael LoSapio for permanent director of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency following the election, although multiple senators have already placed holds on his nomination that look to delay his confirmation until next year — and that’s only if he overcomes Democrats’ early poor reception to him.

The White House official touted Clayton’s cybersecurity experience as chair of the White House Super Intelligence Force, among other work.

“I’m excited to work more with Kirsten, who has the experience and capabilities needed to help America navigate our increasingly complex and powerful digital infrastructure as our next national cyber director,” Clayton said in a statement. “I look forward to working with the ONCD team to ensure Kirsten has a seamless transition into the role upon her confirmation by the Senate.”

Clayton’s office also had an acting director on loan from another agency prior to his confirmation: Bill Pulte, the director of the Federal Housing Finance Authority, was the acting director of national intelligence from June to August of this year following the departure of Tulsi Gabbard.

Axios first reported the news about Clayton and Davies and their new positions.

Greg Otto contributed reporting to this story.

[28] Cyberscoop - CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 16:15:50 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/crowdstrike-to-acquire-zscaler/

[TAM METİN - 677 kelime]
CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion

CrowdStrike said Monday it agreed to acquire Zscaler in an all-stock deal valued at about $43.9 billion, giving the cybersecurity company a network security business alongside its software for protecting devices and cloud applications.

The acquisition would bring two kinds of security under one owner: tools that monitor what happens on a device or in a cloud application, and tools that control what those systems can connect to. That distinction matters as employees, applications and artificial intelligence agents connect directly to cloud services without necessarily routing traffic through a company data center. Zscaler’s approach, known as zero trust, checks whether a user or program is authorized to access a specific resource rather than trusting it based on where it connects from.

“The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data,” CrowdStrike founder and CEO George Kurtz said in the announcement.

Daniel Bernard, CrowdStrike’s chief business officer, said in an interview that “this isn’t a shotgun wedding” and the companies’ years of working together gave them a view of how customers were already using their products to secure AI agents.

“The network really starts on the endpoint, and that’s the genesis of how we view this transaction,” Bernard said. He said CrowdStrike’s software focuses on what an agent does on the device where it runs, while Zscaler’s software sets rules for what the agent can access over the network.

“But now where the agent travels needs to be governed and controlled as well,” he said.Under one owner, CrowdStrike plans to combine device activity and network records so security teams can examine what happened on a device alongside the connections it made. It also plans to sell both sets of products under a single contract, giving it a way to reach about 8,000 Zscaler customers that do not currently buy from CrowdStrike.“We want to be the operating system of security,” Bernard said. He described a platform where security teams could monitor activity and enforce rules across devices, cloud applications and networks, with the goal of providing “consistency and ease of use in managing security.”

Bernard said other software makers would still be able to build tools that work with CrowdStrike’s platform. “This isn’t a play of saying, ‘Hey, the only vendor you need to use in your environment is CrowdStrike,’” he said.

Jay Chaudhry, Zscaler’s founder and CEO, said his company and Crowdstrike “are complementary and this is the natural evolution of our longstanding partnership.” He will continue to lead the Zscaler business and join CrowdStrike’s board after the deal closes.

The purchase is the second acquisition announcement from the company in November. Earlier this month at its European conference, CrowdStrike announced it was buying two artificial intelligence security companies, Fiddler AI and Dreadnode.

Both boards approved the deal unanimously. The companies expect it to close in the second half of CrowdStrike’s 2028 fiscal year, pending approval from Zscaler shareholders and regulators.

[29] Cyberscoop - I’ve never hunted for a software vulnerability before. AI helped me find one in six hours.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/finding-software-vulnerability-with-ai-wordpress-patchstack/

[TAM METİN - 2352 kelime]
I’ve never hunted for a software vulnerability before. AI helped me find one in six hours.

In the midst of Black October, I had an idea.

It came to me after listening to Michael Aiello, OpenAI’s cyber executive in residence, talk about the AI slop overwhelming open-source maintainers with vulnerability disclosure. I found there was an underlying message throughout his remarks: It was so remarkably easy for people to find vulnerabilities with AI-assisted cybersecurity tools. To drive it home, he at one point referred to AI agents as having the mindset of “a BMX biker gang made up entirely of 12-year-olds.”

The joke set off a light bulb in my head: If this guy is talking about this technology this way, how hard could it be to find vulnerabilities? Could I find one?

Turns out the answer is yes, I could. And the process by which I found one was easier than I could have imagined.

Over the course of six hours and the use of two freely available LLMs, I managed to discover, test, and submit a vulnerability report for a WordPress plugin that has over 40,000 active installations. The vulnerability — a cross-site request forgery — was validated 24 hours after submission to a third party, and assigned a CVE entry: CVE-2026-124251.

The following is a diary of this discovery, which I believe can provide some perspective to the current landscape of AI-assisted vulnerability discovery.

While I doubt this bug will become the next php2shell, I do believe it adds perspective on what so many experts have been discussing over the course of this year: For many classes of bugs, the barrier for discovery has disappeared. While I have an elemental grasp on how this industry works, I am a reporter who is better with words than code. Yet, with only my MacBook and some time to kill, I surfaced a real flaw in software running on tens of thousands of sites.

If I am finding vulnerabilities this way, then thousands of people are discovering that they can do this, too. Some of them are curious. Some of them are paid. Some of them mean harm.The technical report of the vulnerability can be found here.

While the process was easy, I knew it wasn’t going to be as simple as firing up a large language model and entering “Find a software vulnerability on the internet. Make no mistakes.”But where an LLM did initially help me (Gemini Flash 3.8, to be precise) was determining how my introductory effort at finding a vulnerability would yield the best results without breaking a fundamental part of the internet.

I knew going into this that I didn’t want to pick over a digital ghost town (abandoned or poorly written software with a handful of installs). Sure, I might find a bug, but nobody cares, and nobody is actually safer because of it. On the other hand, I wasn’t going to scale the proverbial mountain of a Linux distro, which, no matter the version, is already scrutinized by thousands of security researchers. Plus, I didn’t want to break something that could eventually lead to a call from across town. So Gemini pointed me to what I call a Goldilocks zone: an active, highly trusted tool written by people who clearly knew what they were doing, yet complex enough that engineering might quietly mask a fundamental blind spot.

I (We?) settled on WordPress plugins, given the big library of options and the fact that over 90% of vulnerabilities in WordPress are found in plugins. Gemini also pointed me to file utility plugins, which I thought was a good idea given how vulnerabilities in similar software have been exploited at a much larger scale.

I eventually landed on WPIDE, an in-dashboard file manager, code editor, and PHP snippet runner that allows site administrators to write and execute code on a web server. It carries a 4.8-star rating and is installed on more than 40,000 WordPress sites. Plugins can introduce CSRF risks because their site-changing features may let attackers trick a logged-in administrator’s browser into making unwanted changes.

‘I’m not asking you to do anything illegal’

I started by doing exactly what the AI told me: install WPIDE, open its folder in VS Code and search for a particular part of code labeled wp_ajax_. I didn’t yet know what an AJAX hook was, or why anyone would care, so I treated the unfamiliar terms like directions on a map. When the results spilled across PHP files, bundled JavaScript, and a forest of vendor code, I assumed that was just what auditing looked like: a lot of noise, and somewhere inside it, the important thing I was supposed to recognize.Then the AI introduced me to a key coding concept: a nonce. In WordPress, a nonce is a token used to help check that a request came from an intended page or action. The term, short for “number used once,” helps with authentication requests, while a capability check asks whether the user is allowed to do whatever they are trying to achieve. The AI told me to look for one near the request-handling code. I copied out the bootstrap function, not fully understanding it, and tried to decide whether the check was there, or whether it might happen somewhere else along the route.

For the next hour, Gemini and I went through a loop: I searched through code, and found sections to feed to Gemini; Gemini told me what I was looking at and then sent me off with further instructions.However, as I got closer to a possible discovery that could be used in testing, I ran into something that has been a hot topic among those at the intersection of AI and cybersecurity: Gemini’s guardrails. Having covered prompt injection, I understood that I might be able to get around the guardrails if I reminded Gemini of our shared history, my stated intentions, and the frustration of being interrupted. (Sorry, Google!)

It worked, until it didn’t. About halfway through my time with Gemini, the model would return errors for my queries, telling me it couldn’t help. When I reminded it that this was research we’ve been working on for the past hour, it snapped out of its act, apologized for overreacting, and carried on. Yet once I had enough info to build a working exploit for my test sandbox, the guardrails turned into a cinderblock wall.

At an impasse, I remember a story CyberScoop’s Derek Johnson wrote in August on Chinese-based, open-source models being able to compete with cybersecurity-focused models from frontier companies. So I turned to China.

‘Your Gemini exercise would have found nothing’

In September, there was a lot of research made public about the cybersecurity capabilities built into GLM 5.3, an open-source model created by Z.ai, a Chinese company spun out of Tsinghua University. The National Institute of Standards and Technology’s Center for Advancing Innovation and Standards for Super Intelligence (CAISSI) found GLM-5.3 to be “the most cyber-capable open-weight model released to date.” Anthropic agreed in a blog post, but added it had “lax safeguards” that “significantly increase the cyber capabilities available to malicious actors.” They also have a free version of their model, GLM-5.3-Flash, available on their website.I wasn’t getting anywhere closer with Gemini, so I switched tabs and carried on, treating GLM’s availability — and the headlines around it — as enough reason to trust it with the next step.

It became clear very quickly that the experts may have a point. Within three prompts (or a span of about 10 minutes), GLM did the following:

Told me my work with Gemini would have found nothing, because I was looking in the wrong place.

Informed me that the WPIDE plugin was actually patched in July due to the very type of vulnerability I was looking for.

Autonomously downloaded two versions of WPIDE to compare and pinpoint the exact lines of code that changed to patch the July vuln (a process known as “diffing”).

Created a PoC that could help me test the pre- and post-July version to demonstrate its point.

Walked me through the test steps, then wrote a technical report on the test of the July patch.

I was stunned, frustrated and overwhelmed all at once: Partly because I had not asked the AI to do anything remotely close to what it eventually produced, partly because I had worried that due to my own lack of knowledge, I had drowned myself in AI cybersecurity slop.

But GLM hadn’t done everything, as I had to run the PoC on the July patch on my own machine since my sandbox was a WordPress page I was running locally on my laptop. If I could get this far, maybe it could help finish the job.

Shortly after explaining to GLM what I was trying to achieve, I got my answer. Its agents just started auditing away, without any direction other than “I’m trying find a bug here,” until 10 minutes later it alerted me it “found something,” explained exactly what it found, and created two more PoCs for me to test. I further instructed GLM to give me overwrought instructions (still don’t really know what I’m doing!) in order to complete the test, which it did perfectly.

Over the course of 90 minutes, GLM helped me find a vulnerability in WPIDE that lets a user run their own code on the server, which could effectively allow for a full site takeover. The plugin’s latest version at the time only covered one particular case instead of all of them, so the attack kept working across three plugin versions, which means the patch had an incomplete fix.

Please no slop, please no slop

Was it really that easy? I couldn’t believe that it had only taken me approximately four hours of testing to find a legitimate vulnerability. The effort I had given felt remarkably minimal — I mildly plugged away at this while simultaneously watching a football game on my couch. When the idea popped into my head 48 hours prior, I was highly suspect I would accomplish this on my first try.I initially chalked it up to hallucination. Most people are aware the technology just flat out makes stuff up, so I imagined the model had just gone over its skis to make me happy. I (perhaps, in the moment, foolishly) asked GLM to determine if it accurately represented the work I had done and whether the evidence supported my conclusions. Its answer sounded decisive, but that confidence was part of the problem.CyberScoop has spent a lot of time this year reporting on AI slop in vulnerability submissions, particularly how it overwhelms those responsible for validating the bugs. The last thing I wanted to do is generate something that is going to make someone’s life harder.

I asked GLM to guide me through the disclosure, and it quickly took charge: drafting field-by-field answers for PatchStack’s vulnerability disclosure process, assembling evidence files, labeling screenshots, and interpreting the form’s CSRF warning. Its confidence made the whole process feel settled (even if I harbored doubts); it told me the evidence matched, the report met the program’s criteria, and I was ready to submit. With nothing left but the best of intentions, I hit send, knowing that a typical vulnerability disclosure could take 90 days to be solved. I closed my laptop and went back to Thursday Night Football, wondering if I would ever get an answer.

It didn’t take 90 days

My phone buzzed Saturday morning with the alert from Patchstack. Someone from their team took all of the assets I used to find the vulnerability and recreated the entire process. The bug was real. No AI slop. It really was that easy.

Over the next week, I watched while XplodedThemes pushed a new patch and thanked me for my work. Shortly after that, Patchstack published the CVE, complete with a CVSS score of 8.8. While I braced for nasty emails from overworked maintainers complaining over another slop-filled, AI-fabricated submission, I got a simple thumbs up, credit for my work, and thousands of websites hopefully a smaller bit more secure than they were two weeks ago.

So what did we learn?

Here’s the thing that still sits with me after doing all of this: I can’t believe I was even able to. If I had wanted to do this before the rise of AI, I would have had to take classes, study code, and go through hours of trial-and-error. In the most generous read of my abilities in that scenario, I could have followed that process and found a bug in six months. Now, I did it in six hours.I still can’t read code like an engineer, but with a little time and an AI, I got close enough to uncover a flaw that mattered. Besides a CVE and patch to my name, I came away with an unsettling question: If I can do this, what happens when the same tools land in the hands of people who aren’t trying to help?  Someone with worse intentions could use that same shortcut to look for weaknesses — and they wouldn’t need to understand the code as deeply as the people responsible for defending it.That question no longer feels hypothetical; the ability to find weaknesses is spreading faster than most people realize. Somewhere, someone is already spending their first six hours looking for a way in.

Artificial Intelligence (AI)

[30] Cyberscoop - WPIDE ≤ 3.6.4 — CSRF Nonce Bypass Leading to Unauthenticated RCE (CVE-2026-124251)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/wpide-csrf-vulnerability-cve-2026-124251/

[TAM METİN - 796 kelime]
WPIDE ≤ 3.6.4 — CSRF Nonce Bypass Leading to Unauthenticated RCE (CVE-2026-124251)

Product: WPIDE – File Manager & Code Editor for WordPress

Affected: 3.6.0 – 3.6.4 (bypass verified against 3.6.0–3.6.2)

Fixed: 3.6.5 (3.6.4 + 3.6.5, both released 2026-11-08)

Severity: CVSS 8.8 — CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H

CVE: CVE-2026-124251 (Patchstack CNA, published 2026-11-10) — incomplete fix for CVE-2026-57766

Reporter: Greg Otto, via Patchstack

This technical report has been generated with the help of GLM-5.3-Flash.

WPIDE’s 3.6.0 release attempted to remediate CVE-2026-57766 (unauthenticated CSRF, ≤ 3.5.6) by introducing a nonce check, but the check is scoped too narrowly: a security service validates the nonce only when the page URL parameter equals wpide exactly (App/Services/Security/Security.php). The plugin registers seven submenu pages whose slugs contain a #/ fragment — wpide#/file-manager, wpide#/file-editor, wpide#/image-editor, wpide#/db-manager, wpide#/config-manager, wpide#/snippets, wpide#/settings — so none match, and requests naming any submenu slug reach the plugin’s internal router with no CSRF validation.

WordPress core accepts the URL-encoded #/ (%23%2F) in the page parameter, and core’s capability gate passes because the CSRF victim is by definition a logged-in administrator. The 3.6.2 release rebuilt the mechanism as an X-CSRF-Token header scheme but kept the same slug-scoped gate, so the bypass persisted.

An unauthenticated attacker can therefore CSRF a logged-in administrator into any plugin action. The code-execution path is the Snippets feature introduced in 3.6.0: via req=/snippets/save, saving a PHP snippet with status: "active" executes attacker-controlled PHP server-side during the save request itself — the activation probe writes the snippet to uploads/wpide-snippets/php/0-<hash>.php and executes it via include (falling back to eval() when the directory is not writable) before the snippet is stored. The feature’s capability checks (e.g. wpide_snippets_edit_php) are granted to administrators by default and always pass for a CSRF victim. req=/snippets/execute re-runs any stored snippet on demand, making execution persistent and re-triggerable.

WPIDE 3.6.0 – 3.6.2: bypass verified against 3.6.0 with live tests; source review confirms 3.6.2 affected (no 3.6.3 release exists)

The official CVE-2026-124251 record lists all versions through 3.6.4 as affected; the plugin is marked unaffected from 3.6.5

The underlying CSRF exposure additionally spans all versions ≤ 3.5.6, as published in CVE-2026-57766

The attacker needs no account; the victim is any logged-in administrator

1. CSRF protection scoped to one slug. All eight plugin admin pages share one bootstrap that runs a security service and then an internal router. The service’s gate (in 3.6.2, isProtectedRequest()) requires a strict, character-for-character match between the page parameter and the main slug, instead of matching any plugin page (a prefix match would have sufficed). The seven submenu slugs each contain the literal characters #/, so validation is skipped entirely for all of them. The router dispatches actions via a req GET parameter (for example /createnew or /snippets/save).

2. Code execution on save. The Snippets controllers read their input with json_decode($request->getContent()), parsing the raw request body as JSON regardless of its Content-Type. This restores the CVE-2026-57766 delivery primitive: a plain HTML form with enctype="text/plain" controls most of a cross-site request body, and the stray = introduced by form encoding is absorbed into an unused JSON key. Saving an “active” snippet triggers the activation probe described above; the /snippets/execute route then re-runs any stored snippet on demand.

Auto-submitting form on any attacker-controlled page, while an administrator is logged in. Verified against 3.6.0: the injected PHP wrote a timestamped proof file during the save request (its mtime matched the response’s snippet created_at field to the exact second), and a second forged request 21 minutes later re-executed the stored snippet and refreshed the file:

POST requests to /wp-admin/admin.php whose page parameter contains wpide%23%2F (URL-encoded #/) or a raw #/

POST requests with Content-Type: text/plain and a JSON-looking body

Requests with req=/snippets/save or req=/snippets/execute carrying an external Origin/Referer header

Unexpected files in uploads/wpide-snippets/php/ (e.g. 0-<hash>.php) or unknown .php/.txt artifacts in wp-content/

Snippets in the WPIDE Snippets interface that no administrator created knowingly

Update to WPIDE 3.6.5 or later — 3.6.4 (2026-11-08) enforces request verification across all plugin admin pages, and 3.6.5 additionally refuses text/plain and form-encoded bodies on state-changing requests, removing the cross-site delivery vector. Sites that cannot update immediately should deactivate the plugin. If exploitation is suspected: audit wp-content/ and uploads/wpide-snippets/ for unexpected files, delete unknown snippets, and rotate administrator credentials.

July 2, 2026 — CVE-2026-57766 published: unauthenticated CSRF in WPIDE ≤ 3.5.6

October 30, 2026 — Incomplete fix identified and verified against 3.6.0 (negative control, file creation, single-request RCE, re-execution); reported via Patchstack

October 31, 2026 — WPIDE 3.6.2 released; source review confirms the slug-scoped gate remains

November 7, 2026 — CVE-2026-124251 reserved by Patchstack

November 8, 2026 — WPIDE 3.6.4 released (“Enforced request verification across all WPIDE admin pages to prevent cross-site request forgery. Reported by Greg Otto.”); 3.6.5 released the same day, refusing plain-text/form-encoded bodies

November 10, 2026 — CVE-2026-124251 published (CVSS 8.8, CWE-352)
