[31] The Register - Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 06:13:51 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/24/orgs-that-leak-data-prioritize-corporate-interests-over-customers-says-have-i-been-pwneds-troy-hunt/5311802

[TAM METİN - 875 kelime]
Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt

Leakers move through denial, bargaining, delay, legal threats, then claiming Hunt is the real villain

Cleverbridge admits attackers stole customers' credit card and CVV numbers. Yep, you read that right
20 hours ago

Visa, Mastercard theft included info useful for phishing, but not draining bank accounts, say researchers
3 days ago

2013 breach at Russian cybercrime forum resurfaces with millions of victims' credentials
12 days ago

Fireworks AI gets burned twice as customer data exposure widens
13 days ago

Who Gives A Crap? Hackers, apparently, after toilet paper maker's customer data exposed
14 days ago

Troy Hunt, the founder of data breach reporting site Have I Been Pwned (HIBP), has revealed how organizations respond when he informs them they’ve leaked data – if they respond at all.

Hunt gave Anthropic’s Claude access to an email inbox containing over eight years of his email correspondence and then asked the AI to write a post summarising them, a method he said “was the best way to highlight the behaviors I keep seeing across many breaches and subsequent disclosure efforts.”

For the organization, every day without a public statement is a day without bad headlines

Writing in person, Hunt described those behaviors as “standoffish behavior, deliberate delays, heavy involvement from lawyers, and a constant theme: prioritizing corporate interests over those of their customers.”

Once Claude takes over writing duties, it opens with the observation that Hunt sent his standard notification email to 73 organizations between March 2018 and November 2026 and also used website contact forms.

Claude/Hunt focuses on the breach notifications that don’t go well, for example when first contact “often goes nowhere” because “many organisations have no obvious way to report a security incident.”

Some organizations Hunt contacted logged his notifications as customer support requests. “In one case, Troy received three automated surveys asking him to rate the support he'd received,” Claude wrote, before observing “A message that an organization’s customer data is being traded among criminals ended up in the same queue as a question about a late delivery.”

Once someone at victim organization gets the message, a common response is to say they can find no evidence of the breach. Others try to play down the incident, saying it was only a website defacement or that Hunt is re-reporting a previous breach the victim has already disclosed.

If an organization accepts Hunt is probably onto something, it typically says it needs to investigate the matter.

Claude’s take on Hunt’s mails suggests he responds by pointing out that this is an irresponsible approach, because while the investigation takes place, “people whose data has been taken don't know they need to change a password or watch for phishing.”

Next, victims send in the lawyers who ask if their employer or client is legally required to reveal the breach.

Hunt typically replies by saying it is up to the victim to decide if the incident meets the threshold that triggers mandatory disclosure requirements under local laws, but that he intends to notify HIBP users regardless.

Victims don’t like hearing that and respond in three ways:

Calling to discuss disclosure, rather than committing to it;

Calling to discuss disclosure, rather than committing to it;

Asking to see the wording HIBP will use in its disclosure, a request that sees Hunt refer the victim to the HIBP site for examples of the thousand-plus breach reports already on the site;

Asking to see the wording HIBP will use in its disclosure, a request that sees Hunt refer the victim to the HIBP site for examples of the thousand-plus breach reports already on the site;

Arguing that disclosure, not the breach, is the real harm.

Arguing that disclosure, not the breach, is the real harm.

Hunt/Claude suggests that each argument victims use to avoid disclosure “sounds reasonable on its own,” but argues “Together, they mean the people most affected by the breach are the last to hear about it.”

The post also reports on some better interactions, such as a small booking platform that was initially skeptical about Hunt’s report, then admitted he was right and acted swiftly to inform customers.

A European travel app hardened its systems, rotated its keys, and published a disclosure. “Troy held off loading the data so he could link to their statement, and thanked them for their transparency,” according to the Claude-generated text.

The bot’s analysis also offers examples of when breach notifications go well.

“Someone with authority over security answered quickly,” Claude concluded. “They treated Troy's data as help rather than as a threat. They told their customers what they knew, even when they didn't know everything yet, and updated the details later. None of them needed lawyers to manage the conversation.”

Hunt appears to have asked Claude to offer its opinion of his email trove. “I don't think the main obstacle to disclosure is technical, or even legal,” the post states. “It's about incentives. For the organization, every day without a public statement is a day without bad headlines.”

Hunt also said he won’t make a habit of having AI write his content, but said Claude “absolutely nails it!” Unlike many of the orgs Hunt contacted. ®

[32] The Register - Cisco and OpenAI pitch eBPF kernel plugins as the way to tame the patchpocalypse
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 02:46:56 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/24/cisco-and-openai-pitch-ebpf-kernel-plugins-as-the-way-to-tame-the-patchpocalypse/5311795

[TAM METİN - 568 kelime]
Cisco and OpenAI pitch eBPF kernel plugins as the way to tame the patchpocalypse

‘Shirudo’ project promises to whip up compensating controls in a hurry

Critical Cisco remote code execution bug under attack
14 hours ago

Old-school Linux desktops find fresh routes to Wayland
22 hours ago

Google claims last week’s critical VMware bug was under attack in September
1 day ago

Flaws in VMware and KVM need attention, if you have time while fixing Java, PHP, and everything else
4 days ago

XXRI-OS explores the lowest end of the Linux desktop
4 days ago

Cisco and OpenAI have released an open-source tool called Shirudo to implement compensating controls in Linux, potentially improving security for users who can’t quickly patch newly discovered software flaws.

Shirudo relies on eBPF, the technology that makes it possible to run sandboxed programs in the Linux kernel. eBPF programs are effectively kernel plug-ins but don’t get unrestricted access to system resources.

Developers have mostly used eBPF to create network functions that enable things like the open source Kubernetes observability and security tool Cilium, a Cloud Native Computing Foundation project.

Cisco and OpenAI think eBPF can help organizations cope with the flood of 100,000-plus software flaws reported this year, many after the debut of AI-powered bug finders.

The increased volume of flaws, and patches to fix them, is a problem for many organizations which already struggle to test fixes or find change windows in which to implement them. That problem has seen greater interest in “compensating controls” – changes to applications or infrastructure that harden them against flaws or malware, without requiring a patch.

With Shirudo, Cisco and OpenAI are suggesting two things:

The privileges eBPF programs enjoy make them a fine way to implement compensating controls for Linux;

The privileges eBPF programs enjoy make them a fine way to implement compensating controls for Linux;

Agents should scan your infrastructure for vulnerabilities, then use tools like OpenAI’s Codex to write compensating controls as eBPF programs at machine speed.

Agents should scan your infrastructure for vulnerabilities, then use tools like OpenAI’s Codex to write compensating controls as eBPF programs at machine speed.

“Shirudo” is apparently the Japanese word for “shield,” so Cisco and OpenAI have called the eBPF programs the tool creates “shields.” The two companies envision humans checking every shield Shirudo creates before putting it into production.

Shirudo is open source and available here. The two companies say they’ve already demonstrated it could mitigate the recently discovered flaws in Java without requiring downtime for an Elasticsearch implementation.

Cisco and OpenAI are not alone in advocating for compensating controls. Third-party software maintenance outfits like Rimini Street have used them for years to protect software when their clients no longer have the right to access patches. VMware has introduced “virtual patching.” Palo Alto Networks and Rapid 7 also like this approach, and offer automation to develop controls.

Where Cisco and OpenAI differ is their decision to make Shirudo open source, and their ambition that other players adapt and advance it.

That offer is not entirely altruistic, as if Shirudo takes off it could create more demand for Codex. Cisco’s angle comes from the fact it owns Isovalent, an early champion of eBPF and the creator of both Cilium and the Tetragon sub-project on which Shirudo is based. Switchzilla is using eBPF in other products, so normalizing its use through an open source project can’t hurt. ®

[33] The Register - Inception - possibly a ShinyHunters reboot - claims seven victims in a week
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 22:52:23 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/23/inception-possibly-a-shinyhunters-reboot-claims-seven-victims-in-a-week/5311768

[TAM METİN - 680 kelime]
Inception - possibly a ShinyHunters reboot - claims seven victims in a week

Whatever they call themselves these days: be on the lookout for voice phishing attempts

FBI confirms 'multiple' arrests related to ShinyHunters hack
October 5, 2026

FBI to ShinyHunters: 'We know how to find you'
September 29, 2026

Dutch police arrest 'security pro' in ShinyHunters probe
September 29, 2026

ShinyHunters tells The Reg: We hacked the FBI to 'protect our business'
September 25, 2026

ShinyHunters claims FBI hack: 'This is NOT financially motivated'
September 22, 2026

Don't mind the recent arrests. The ShinyHunters extortion gang may still be operating, just under the new name of Inception, according to Okta's threat intelligence group.

ShinyHunters is the brash cybercriminal group that, most recently, breached rival gang Clop before hacking the FBI, resulting in at least three arrests of key suspects in September and October. The crims - and their old data leak site - have since gone dark.

“While this threat cluster has changed their brand affiliation, the operators remain active in targeting organizations with voice phishing attacks and account takeovers resulting in data theft and extortion,” Okta threat hunters Moussa Diallo and Katie Nickels said on Monday.

Okta tracks this threat cluster as O-UNC-053 - the “O-UNC” prefix stands for an Okta Uncategorized cluster, and it uses this label before formally attributing the activity to a named threat group.

While Okta hasn't formally confirmed O-UNC-053 to be the same crime crew as ShinyHunters, the identity and access management provider’s researchers note that it does use nearly identical social engineering tactics: voice phishing, during which the scammers trick targets into entering credentials into phishing pages and approving multi-factor authentication (MFA).

Plus, they note: O-UNC-053 operators change the themes of their phishing domains about every four to six weeks, and they also post claimed victims on ShinyHunters’ data leak site. In November, however, organizations targeted by O-UNC-053 began appearing on a new data leak site branded as Inception and not ShinyHunters.

The first five - Advanced Micro Devices, Cigna Healthcare, Paychex, PepsiCo, and Henry Schein - appeared November 17 on the site, and all were marked “leaked.” Soon after, security maven Dominic Alvieri questioned if Inception was linked to ShinyHunters. “ShinyHunters are not all in jail,” he wrote on social media.

On November 19, AutoZone appeared on the site, with Inception again claiming to have leaked the auto-parts retailer's files. On Saturday, EV technology firm Lucid Group joined the claimed victims.

None of the seven companies have confirmed breaches, and none responded to The Register’s inquiries. We will update this story when we receive more information.

Okta Threat Intelligence “is unable to validate if published data is authentic,” the researchers said in response to our questions.

In the Monday report, the Okta team does note that Inception’s new data leak site listings “included multiple targets for which Okta Threat Intelligence previously observed O-UNC-053 infrastructure registration.”

The infrastructure was registered between six and 19 days before Inception listed the companies on its leak site, and this gap “is consistent with prior shifts from Com-affiliated actors,” Diallo and Nickels added.

The Com (short for community) is a loosely knit network of online groups whose young members participate in a range of illicit activities including hacking, swatting, digital extortion, real-life shootings, and other physical violence. Many security researchers consider ShinyHunters to be connected to The Com, along with groups such as Scattered Spider and LAPSUS$, with overlapping memberships and shared infrastructure.

“Before and after the change in DLS publication, O-UNC-053 maintained consistent infrastructure and TTPs, leading us to assess that the cluster has very likely changed their brand affiliation,” the Okta researchers wrote. “We cannot currently make a high confidence assessment as to whether O-UNC-053 is operating the DLS themselves or is partnering with another extortion actor.”

Regardless, members of the group formerly known as ShinyHunters and/or O-UNC-053 are still roaming the internet, looking for new victims, and conducting voice phishing attacks to steal data and then extort victims' companies for exorbitant sums of money. Consider yourself warned - and turn on phishing-resistant MFA.®

[34] The Register - Bluetooth brakes are broken as researchers find anyone can control them
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 20:29:59 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/23/bluetooth-brakes-are-broken-as-researchers-find-anyone-can-control-them/5311748

[TAM METİN - 866 kelime]
Bluetooth brakes are broken as researchers find anyone can control them

Security fixes are available for two Elecbrakes models, but an older controller remains vulnerable and unpatched

As cars go digital, US consumers pay an extra $12 billion a year for repairs
1 day ago

California tells Waymo to take the next exit after freeway mishaps
5 days ago

Smart meters to cost the UK billions: 2G switch-off means replacing 12-13 million
13 days ago

RIP to the world’s ugliest car
17 days ago

Feds warn grocers: Don't chill on cyber defenses as freezer hacking spree heats up
18 days ago

Imagine something as mission-critical as the brakes on a trailer being vulnerable to unauthorized control. One Australian manufacturer made the interesting choice of building Bluetooth into a trailer brake controller without adequately securing its critical braking functions.

Anyone within Bluetooth range of an unpatched Elecbrakes trailer controller could potentially trigger its brakes or apply more braking force than intended, without needing to authenticate.

The discovery comes courtesy of a pair of security researchers from Element Digital, Patrick Forck and Jeremy McCarthy. Their investigation began earlier this year when Forck was driving down the road and noticed that his physical Elecbrakes In-Car Remote had connected to someone else's trailer without pairing or prompting him.

“It had connected mid-drive, without pairing or a prompt, to a brake controller that wasn’t mine, on a trailer I’d never seen, belonging to a driver who had no idea,” the researchers wrote. That one-off connection triggered a full-scale investigation by the pair.

For those unfamiliar with trailer brake controllers, these devices are commonly used on larger trailers. Australian manufacturer Elecbrakes sells such controllers in Australia and New Zealand. Depending on jurisdiction, trailers over a certain weight are required to have their own braking system, which Elecbrakes manufactures. Elecbrakes offers two versions that are designed to be easier to install than traditional controllers: One that’s mounted in a vehicle, and one that is mounted directly on a trailer. That trailer-mounted unit can receive Bluetooth commands from the Elecbrakes smartphone app or a separate in-car remote.

Given the importance of such control units to road safety, it’s easy to imagine how serious such a vulnerability could be if it proved to be easy to exploit, and boy, did it.

Forck and McCarthy admitted they’re infrastructure security guys, not software devs, so they turned to Claude Code to dissect a decompiled copy of the Elecbrakes app, which Claude was happy to oblige.

“What came back from the analysis was a map of the device’s entire Bluetooth GATT profile: 47 characteristics across four services, commands sent as plain unencrypted byte arrays, no checksums, no sequence numbers,” the researchers noted. To make matters worse, there was no authentication required for critical braking commands, even though other Bluetooth functions required pairing.

Claude then built a web app that ran in Chrome which was able to access the Bluetooth controls for a nearby Elecbrakes system, and they were able to modify practically every aspect of brake control on a unit owned by one of them.

Pairing failed on a few attempts, but not where it mattered: It allowed the connection without issue for actual brake control. They sent a few bytes to the brake control characteristic, and were shocked.

“The electromagnetic hum at the brake drums was immediate and, frankly, a little sickening,” the pair said. “It said yes to four bytes from a total stranger then energised several tonnes’ worth of brakes and reported back that it had done so, pleased with itself.”

Further experiments managed to modify braking strength based on deceleration speed as well, making it so that the trailer acted unpredictably based on the amount of force a driver applied to stop their vehicle.

To say this is worse than a typical internet-connected device bug is to put it mildly - it even earned a trio of CVEs from CISA, with two of them scoring a CVSS of 9.6.

“The attack surface is ‘anyone within Bluetooth range,’ the skill required is ‘can write four bytes,’ and the victim selection is ‘optional,’ ” Forck and McCarthy said. The fact AI was able to get them to that point in just a couple of days is even more concerning, they added.

“This kind of research used to require a fairly rare and specific skill set,” the pair noted. “It now requires curiosity, a device you own, and an AI that can read bytecode faster than any human.”

Luckily, they appear to have been dealing with a relatively responsive company in Elecbrakes - patches have been deployed for both the in-car and trailer-mounted models of their brake controllers. Installation is a manual process, we note, so if you own a controller from the company, be sure to stop and install the update.

Unfortunately, Elecbrakes has a third controller out there that hasn’t been manufactured in several years, and it got skipped in the patching process. Owners of an ELBC2000 should be aware that their controller is still vulnerable, and there’s no patch available yet, as the vendor didn’t confirm that the ELBC2000 was vulnerable until the end of October, after it faced pressure from the researchers.  ®

[35] The Register - Critical Cisco remote code execution bug under attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:56:56 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/23/critical-cisco-remote-code-execution-bug-under-attack/5311722

[TAM METİN - 466 kelime]
Critical Cisco remote code execution bug under attack

CISA, F6 sound the alarm after PoC exploit made public

Cisco and OpenAI pitch eBPF kernel plugins as the way to tame the patchpocalypse
6 hours ago

PHP 8.6: Things are gonna get a lot more strict around here
3 days ago

Ye olde server consolidation has made Cisco push top-of-rack switching to 100G
13 days ago

Trump taps Palantir security chief to run battered US cyber defense agency
13 days ago

Cisco to link all future products to its SaaS-y Cloud Control management tool
14 days ago

Attackers are currently exploiting a critical vulnerability tracked as CVE-2026-76466 in Cisco Expressway that allows them to remotely execute commands as root without any authentication, according to warnings from the US government and security researchers.

CVE-2026-76466, which carries a 9.8 CVSS rating, affects Cisco Expressway-C and Cisco Expressway-E gateways configured in clusters, both virtual and physical appliances running vulnerable software. These provide firewall traversal, VPN-less remote access, and call routing for remote users connecting to voice, video, and messaging collaboration services.

The bug involves hard-coded authentication credentials and insufficient certificate validation in clustered Expressway deployments.

At the time, the networking vendor said it wasn’t aware of any abuse of the vulnerability.

That all changed late last week, however, when Russian security firm F6 said it was investigating “the first observed case” of miscreants exploiting CVE-2026-76466, shortly after a public proof-of-concept exploit became available.

“The attackers initially compromised two Cisco Expressway internet-facing devices,” F6’s Cyber Defense Center said on Thursday. “Through one of them, they gained access to two additional cluster nodes located on the organization’s internal network. After obtaining root access, the attackers began preparing tooling for further lateral movement across the infrastructure.”

Specifically, the criminals prepared custom Python code to scan the victim’s network and identify files and folders shared over the Server Message Block (SMB) protocol. The scripts were also designed to interact with SQL Server and WinRM, and search GitLab repositories for credentials and other sensitive information.

The Python scripts used in the attack indicated that the operator used an AI assistant, according to F6.

The security shop also noted that one of the key indicators of compromise is a nodeup “wombat@127.0.0.1” entry in the Erlang system log (erlang.log) on affected Cisco Expressway devices.

A day later, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added CVE-2026-76466 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog, and told federal agencies to patch by Monday.

Cisco updated its advisory on Monday to acknowledge CISA’s addition, while noting that it has no direct evidence of active exploitation. The Register reached out to the networking and security vendor for additional details about the scope of the attacks, but did not receive any response. We will update this story as we learn more. ®

[36] The Register - Bulgaria evicts cyber snoops who lurked inside government networks for 5 years
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:01:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/23/bulgaria-evicts-cyber-snoops-who-lurked-inside-government-networks-for-5-years/5311629

[TAM METİN - 459 kelime]
Bulgaria evicts cyber snoops who lurked inside government networks for 5 years

Minister says intruders downloaded more than 130 GB of data as political churn left security neglected

Bulgaria hack: 20-year-old infosec whizz cuffed after 'adult population's' finance deets nicked
July 18, 2019

Bulgaria nixes 'metadata' law, Paraguay delays
March 13, 2015

Tomb-raid boffins find golden hoard of the warrior Thracians
November 9, 2012

Oldest town of the Old World found in Bulgaria
November 1, 2012

Jobs neck and neck with 'angry people' for Time award
November 9, 2011

The Ancient Thracian heartland of Bulgaria says it has shut down a cyberespionage operation that gave intruders access to government networks for five years – the longest-running known campaign against its administration.

Bulgaria's Ministry of Innovation and Digital Transformation said the unauthorized and officially unattributed attackers first gained access to infrastructure used by government institutions to exchange information in 2021.

It explained that the intrusion went unnoticed for so long because "the previous means of protection did not work as a single, integrated system," and that without a new, recently installed product, it may have gone undetected for much longer.

"The incident was discovered thanks to the new national cybersecurity system, which for the first time provides a single picture of what is happening in state networks," the ministry added.

"Without it, this activity would most likely have remained hidden for an indefinite period of time."

Bulgaria acknowledged that "sensitive information related to the work of the state" was accessed, but did not specify the scale of the compromise.

Innovation and Digital Transformation Minister Ivan Vasilev said attackers had downloaded more than 130 GB of data over five years. He also put the volume at 62 million pages.

The Register contacted the Bulgarian government for confirmation of these numbers.

The ministry said that it has not yet found any evidence that citizens' personal data was compromised, and that the affected infrastructure "will be under increased surveillance for 90 days" in case the attackers try to regain access.

Vasilev said multiple international hacking groups had accessed government systems since 2021. He did not specify the names of these groups, however, nor did he name any specific countries that may have been involved.

In a follow-up interview with bTV, Vasilev blamed political instability and frequent changes of ministerial leadership for leaving government systems inadequately protected.

A representative of the Progressive Bulgaria party, which back in May won Bulgaria's eighth general election in five years, Vasilev said the "constant change of leadership" means "important things for the state do not actually happen."

Vasilev said the intrusion was discovered over the summer as the national cybersecurity system was upgraded, and that teams had spent the past three to four months removing the attackers. ®

Jessica Lyons (11:00)

Yes. It's a definite yes on that one.

Yes.

Jessica Lyons (11:33)

Right. And we see some real attacks too, with the refrigeration attacks and the water system attacks in America over the summer. So there are real attacks that are happening and affecting critical systems too. So it's not like these outages are occurring in a vacuum with nothing being  perpetrated by criminals, or spies, or nation-state attackers.

Yes. You think about it like the modern internet that we have... Liam, you lived on the continent for a while. So, in history, Europe was a whole bunch of very small, very isolated communities. They developed their own languages, their own culture, everything. And then they grew and they became one connected mass. But in some ways they still can't talk to each other. And I feel like that's the kind of technology that we have now. We have all these disparate systems that existed as separate entities. The FAA's 50-year-old systems didn't need to be connected to the internet and talking to X, Y, and Z. And now they do. But all those other systems are talking a different language too. So we have a massive technical debt. Every week, with PHP2Shell, the refrigerator attacks, everything. . Every week we turn around and we learn one more way in which there's a huge technical debt that wewe're now in the point where we're like, "oh oops, you know, uh..."

Yes, this is a growing problem. I want to start to wave my arms around and talk about synergistic network effects. I have interviewed some of the people behind the original ARM operating system on The Register and I still attend the meetings of the London Acorn User Group. Over the years they've had some pretty big names in the industry. We had Sophie Wilson, the inventor of the ARM chip, there a couple of times. One time somebody asked a question: "Do you feel happy and proud that there's an ARM chip inside every iPhone and every Android phone?" She laughed at the question, and when the person looked surprised, she said, "There isn't an ARM chip inside every iPhone. There are six or eight ARM chips inside every iPhone." Bear in mind this was more than a decade ago. She said there's a central ARM chip with several cores powering it, but there's an ARM chip in the GSM mobile signaling stack, there's an ARM chip in its USB controller, there's an ARM chip in its memory controller, there's an ARM chip in its Bluetooth controller, because it's cheaper to embed an ARM chip and run a bit of software on it than design dedicated silicon to manage all the different wireless networks. It's all ARM chips all the way down. Your iPhone isn't an ARM device; it's a loose cluster of multiple different ARM devices of different generations. And nobody is entirely sure anymore if they're all running the same stack.

A very visible example of this was in Intel chips until about the early Core 2 Duo era: they had an embedded management processor that was derived from the accelerator in a Nintendo Famicom game from the nineties called Quark. Then Intel replaced it with a cut-down x86 core running Minix, the experimental operating system that inspired Linux. They never gave back any of the code, and the Minix project faltered and is dormant because Dr. Andy Tanenbaum, the guy who founded it, retired. Nowadays in any modern internet-connected server there'll be Linux in its instrumented lights-out monitoring chip. There'll probably be Linux in its network interfaces. There'll be Linux running on the main CPU cluster. I wrote recently about a Linux-powered RISC-V graphics card which runs Ubuntu on loads of RISC-V cores in the GPU.

Are they all on the same Linux version? Have they all been patched against vulnerabilities? No.

Right. So basically the problem goes down to the individual device level.

Yes. There's legions of distributed systems inside your pocket right now, and inside every server. They're running loads of copies of Linux, probably some BSDs, Wind River, ThreadX, QNX, and other embedded operating systems. And some of the same vulnerabilities are in all of them. The Microsoft stack is held together with Linux.

Yes. That's not to say that distributed systems are bad, obviously.

If we had more distributed systems, many of the outages we experienced in the past week wouldn't have been such a problem. But it makes me wonder if there's anything we can do... Can we stop the castle from sinking into the swamp without completely tearing it down and rebuilding it from new foundations? Are we stuck with what we've got?

We can do what they have done in Rome and Istanbul:build a new layer on top.

Jessica Lyons (17:34)

I think it's going to get worse before it gets better because AI is going to continue to unearth all of these vulnerabilities. Last week we surpassed a hundred thousand CVEs, and that's more than double 2025's total and we're there in mid-November.

Jessica Lyons (17:49)

That comes on the heels of two Microsoft Patch Tuesdays with more than a thousand CVEs that they patched and disclosed.

And that's not slowing down.

Jessica Lyons (18:07)

No, it's not. As vendors, security researchers, and attackers are all using AI to find all these long-hidden vulnerabilities, that creates a massive backlog of software that needs to be patched. Eventually we're going to get to the other side of this where it will make for more secure code and more secure digital infrastructure. Until then, we're going to see more outages. We're going to see more of the same, and it's not going to be very fun for anybody.

Liam, you seem to express some doubt there as to whether it would come out on the other side.

I'm not convinced that this problem is fixable. We have to allow the possibility that maybe they're going to keep finding more holes in this. There's a famous talk by Ken Thompson called "Reflections on Trusting Trust" [PDF]. He embedded a remote access hole intentionally into the original C compiler, and the compiler inserts it into anything it compiles without the code being in the source code of the compiler. You have a compiler which is a binary that runs source code to generate a new binary. The binary can contain the hole, which is never in the source code, as long as it knows to put it in the next one. He did it as an exercise and revealed it at his Turing Award lecture. Stuff as fundamental as that exists because virtually every one of those controller chips has code written in C. And we don't really know what's in all that C, but virtually all of it came from the same early 1970s root of the development of C. There could be nasties lurking in there that are in all of them. They're in all the BSDs, in Linux, and Windows NT is built in C and inherited code from DEC VMS. There could be stuff in there for decades that we have no idea about that's going to crop up, and it's in everything. Some of these devices have no way of being updated; the software's burned in ROMs that are not visible to the operating system that's running. This could keep crumbling away.

I once was a big fan of a website called LoperOS.org by Stanislav Datskovskiy. He was a Lisp evangelist. He's gone very quiet in recent years, but he compared building software out of C to building a model of a bridge or the Eiffel Tower out of toothpicks or matchsticks. The end structure is not very strong because it's mostly weak wood and glue. You build it out of this poor structural material because loads of people can work together on it. And C and tools like C are good at this; you can farm out development to whole teams of people. They're working with tweezers and toothpicks instead of bricks and cement because it's easier to do this over a team.

Somebody said, "If we built houses the way that we build software, then the first termite colony to come along would destroy civilization," because we built it out of bad materials. I'm not personally Christian, but I read the Bible and there's that line about building your house on sand. It doesn't matter how tough you make the house: if you built it on sand and the sand shifts, you've got a big problem.

This problem goes all the way down. This might be a good time to invest in companies developing software in languages like Ada that aren't built on C and don't contain any C whatsoever. When Ada was introduced – Ada is a French language originally from INRIA – it was criticized as being too big and heavy compared to C. But now, compared to C++, it's quite small and quick, and there's a good GNU toolchain and tools like SPARK and AdaCore that have open source development and distribution systems for it. Maybe we're going to have to replace the whole thing.

God, I hope not. One of the things I think about happening is how in past generations of software we slapped new things on top of old things to protect the old things from the new things.

And it makes me wonder if the next version of slapping something on top of our entire infrastructure is AI. We put AI on top of it. We make AI handle bug hunting, vulnerability remediation, and security. It creates a situation where the stuff is safe for now, but software always abstracts layers as you get further up the stack. I wonder if AI is going to be the next level of abstraction to the point where we don't really understand what the code's doing anymore at all.

We just rely on the agents that are running it to handle protecting us. What do you think? Is that a potential next generation that we see here, Jess?

Jessica Lyons (24:24)

I think it definitely could be. There's a lot of push in the security industry to use AI for both finding and fixing bugs.On the code-writing side of things, it's not there yet. But it's appealing when there's a shortage of human workers who can do this and AI is cheaper. You don't need to pay AI.

Jessica Lyons (24:53)

Right. Over time. So I think it's likely that is going to be one of the ways we try to solve this problem.

I don't see another way out of it because of the sheer volume of flaws that are being found. Like Liam said, there's low-level stuff getting discovered now that no human would have found, but the fact that an AI found it means someone's going to exploit it at some point.

I think for management this is going to look like a very tempting option, but it will accelerate the problem. We're in an iterative race at the moment – the Red Queen's race. You have to run as  fast as you can to stay where you are. We've got new tools that are finding problems faster than ever, so it's tempting to try and use similar tools to fix them faster than ever, but that means nobody understands the fix. Then if another hole is found deeper down, the whole layer of fixes collapses.

One of the predominant development tools on the PC platform used to be Turbo Pascal. DOS wasn't written in C. Way back when, people accused Microsoft of plagiarizing CP/M. Microsoft didn't write DOS; Tim Paterson of Seattle Computer Products wrote the original. CP/M was written in a high-level language, PL/M, whereas DOS was hand-coded in Assembler. It wasn't plagiarism because they went a level down. In the early days people wrote this stuff in Pascal. I remember Unix catching on and C becoming trendy because it was so fast. For a little while a company called TopSpeed sold a Modula-2 compiler because not only was the code faster than C, but it compiled faster than C compilers back then. Ultimately the sheer inertia of C and C-related languages caught on and all that stuff got sidelined. Borland went through various transitions and Turbo Pascal became Delphi, which went to Embarcadero and is still around, though I'm not sure anybody cares anymore. I worked through one industry shift where Assembler and Pascal-related languages were replaced by C. It could happen again. It wasn't all that bad last time. I know it seems unimaginable now, but I'm not that old. Working in this field for less than four decades, I've watched an entire software industry get swept away and replaced. And we all kept our jobs, and we all kept working on pretty much the same hardware. Maybe that's going to happen again. I know it sounds unimaginable, but it honestly wasn't that bad.

Speaking of hardware, given these outages, connected services going offline are causing chaos. Setting aside the FAA – that's a public service that stranded travelers – banks, Apple, and Microsoft are business problems. We're seeing with AI where everyone is weighing the cost-benefit between hosted services where you pay for tokens versus turning the server closet back into a server closet. Is it time to come back on-premises with a lot of these things? Is it time to run your own Exchange server again or host your own file shares on your own network? It makes me wonder if the solution to some of these outage concerns going forward is going to be a return to on-premises.

Personally I think that's an excellent idea.

Jessica Lyons (29:11)

I agree, but so many of these companies are entrenched in every other company – Microsoft, for example. It's going to be pretty hard to completely swing to the opposite end of the spectrum here.

Sure. Maybe they need to start offering more self-hosted services again. If you're a business concerned about outages that will probably keep happening,the safest thing to do would be to bring it back on-premises. But if everything you do is in Google or Microsoft 365, it's going to be hard to get yourself out of that, and probably very expensive, at least at first. Especially with the cost of hardware right now not going down given the AI push. So maybe we're all just trapped.

This is part of the digital sovereignty push in Europe at the moment. And it's interesting that on either side of the border between Denmark and northern Germany, there have been two very different stories in the last couple of years. In the last year or so, Schleswig-Holstein has successfully migrated from Microsoft 365 over to Open-Xchange, which is a standards-based email server that can look like and support Outlook. Across the border, Denmark weighed up going all open source. They did a pilot program that failed. They reverted to their Microsoft-based solution just in time to have a massive outage. Have fun.

Jessica Lyons (30:58)

But you know what, I bet some of this came down to senior government officials saying, "I don't understand this new email client you're showing me. Give me Outlook back, or I'm going to fire the lot of you."" Maybe somebody needs to go in there and say, "We will send somebody around to teach you to use it, or if you're not able to use it, maybe it is time that you give way to somebody who can, because you've just cost us 300 million dollars because you won't change email clients."

We do have standards-based solutions. HTML, SMTP, and IMAP give us standards for moving around email, messages, and documents. We can replace Microsoft-based software iteratively: first the applications, and when people get used to the applications, the operating system. When Ubuntu launched twenty-two years ago, it only filled about half a CD – it took about 300 megabytes – and they wondered what to put on the rest of the CD because in those days they mailed CDs rather than making you download it. So they put Windows installers for all the apps on the CD. You could put it in your Windows box and install LibreOffice, Thunderbird, Firefox, and VLC to get used to the apps. When you were comfortable with the apps, you installed Ubuntu and got the same apps with the new desktop.

Sweet, happy times. You can do this.

You can do this with a government department.

Jessica Lyons (32:47)

It requires political will, and in America I don't think we have that. So that's a

It's a problem stateside. But the argument that some EU states are making is: why don't we spend hundreds of millions of (insert local currency symbol here) with local companies? We're not claiming we'll save you any money, but we'll keep the money in the country or with an EU partner instead of sending it across the ocean, and we free ourselves of a dependency. That works with senior political types and bureaucrats. This is fixable, but the price may look unacceptable right now to a lot of people. The outages I fear are going to come closer and closer together.

Right. That's going to be the determining factor. It's going to be interesting to watch over the next few months to see what new outages we experience: whether the pace keeps up or increases. We'll see. The US government is overhauling a lot of old systems in aerospace and other sectors, so the push to move to more independent systems or back on-premises is going to be driven by the acceptability of outages.

Yes. "I'm sorry guys, we can't afford to fix it."

Jessica Lyons (34:14)

Right. What's the pain tolerance? When is it too much, or do people get used to these outages as a part of life? "Today we won't be able to do any work because Microsoft is out again."

Yes. The economic movers and shakers are not going to accept that if it keeps happening. There's going to be a point where the pain tolerance is reached and people say, "No, we can't keep doing this."

You know what? I actually disagree there because, to take a different example: I used to be a motorcyclist. Early in my career I switched from tech support to IT manager to designing and building networks, and I turned up to work on a motorbike. Then I had a really bad crash, switched to three-wheelers, and discovered how bad London traffic had gotten. My injuries prevented me from going back on a solo bike most of the time, but years later I switched to bicycling. You can still cut through traffic very well on a bicycle in a lot of Europe. Car drivers got used to the delays; it gets ten minutes longer to get to work every year, but you don't notice ten minutes over a year, so you put up with it. My next-door neighbor didn't know what underground line he lived on because he drove everywhere. People got used to it taking two or three hours to drive to work and just put up with it. I've seen photos of massive West Coast freeways with eight lanes of stationary traffic. People will put up with amazing levels of delay and non-functionality if it happens gradually enough.

Yes. We will see what comes of everything. You could be right; it could be a slow enough roll that everyone just accepts that we're going to lose some money, but you can't disrupt the tech.

Right. No matter what happens, if there are more outages, we will be here to talk about them on The Kettle. If there are fewer, we probably won't, but one can hope.

Thanks for coming on today, guys, and thank you for listening in. We will talk to you soon.

Thank you for having me. Goodbye. ®

[38] The Register - Norway links energy firm cyberattacks to critical Azure API Management flaw
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 13:29:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/23/norway-links-energy-firm-cyberattacks-to-critical-azure-api-management-flaw/5311598

[TAM METİN - 585 kelime]
Norway links energy firm cyberattacks to critical Azure API Management flaw

Microsoft says mitigation is in place, but admins must investigate possible compromise and rotate secrets

Microsoft mega outage: Report reveals testing gaps and emails that never arrived
16 hours ago

Everything going down at once wasn't evidence of a conspiracy – but the truth may be scarier
18 hours ago

Airbus may be leaving Microsoft Office, but for its 36,000 defense staff, Office isn't leaving them
23 hours ago

Microsoft's last non-security preview of 2026 packs a punch
3 days ago

Microsoft suggests Registry hack to work around hotpatch woes
3 days ago

Microsoft has issued a critical vulnerability advisory after attackers were caught raiding Azure API Management (APIM) instances for secrets and performing admin operations.

According to a nonpublic advisory, seen by The Register, Microsoft said the attacks, which have already affected two major energy companies, only impact customers on Azure API Management Classic tiers, not Azure API Management v2.

It said of the now-patched vuln: "Our investigation found that customers were exposed and an unauthorized party could have used the issue to access secrets stored in Azure API Management Classic instances before Microsoft identified the security issue."

Microsoft linked exposure to Classic-tier configurations that did not follow its network security guidance or stored secrets directly in APIM rather than referencing them from Azure Key Vault.

Microsoft directs customers to its security guidance for the recommended configuration.

The advisory lists potentially exposed secrets, including named values, client secrets associated with identity providers and OpenID Connect providers, subscription keys, and tokens used to call direct management APIs.

Security expert Anders Kristiansen warned that stolen credentials could also give attackers access to services behind APIM.

"If someone obtains a credential from that configuration, they may be able to use it directly against the service it protects, outside APIM," he wrote. "What they can reach depends on the credential's permissions and whether it is still valid."

Norway's National Security Authority (NSM) urged organizations using APIM to assume their secrets and named values had been exposed and rotate all tokens and secrets.

The agency also advised mapping the information accessible through each APIM instance to assess the potential scope of any compromise.

NSM credited Norway's energy sector with first detecting the vulnerability last week. It named gas giant Equinor and hydropower company Skagerak Energi among the affected organizations.

The authority commended Equinor for its early detection and rapid information-sharing, and warned that growing use of AI increased the risk of further exploitation.

"The vulnerability affects several sectors in Norway and also beyond Norway's borders," it said. "NSM has close dialogue and cooperation with national and international partners and businesses about the situation.

"When a vulnerability becomes known, multiple actors will be able to attempt to exploit it. Rapid escalation in the use of artificial intelligence is affecting the situation. This poses a risk to Norwegian businesses."

Customers who spoke to The Register warned that forgotten development and test instances could leave more organizations exposed. The extent of exploitation remains unclear.

Microsoft said it identified the security issue on November 18 and automatically applied a mitigation to close the access path the following day.

The Windows giant said customers still needed to investigate whether their instances had been compromised before the mitigation was applied.

NSM nevertheless advised organizations to check their instance's runtime version against its published patch thresholds and contact their provider if it remained unpatched.

Microsoft did not immediately respond to our request for comment. ®

[39] The Register - Cleverbridge admits attackers stole customers' credit card and CVV numbers. Yep, you read that right
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 12:48:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/cyber-crime/2026/11/23/cleverbridge-admits-attackers-stole-customers-credit-card-and-cvv-numbers-yep-you-read-that-right/5311573

[TAM METİN - 530 kelime]
Cleverbridge admits attackers stole customers' credit card and CVV numbers. Yep, you read that right

Payments platform hasn't disclosed how the data was captured or the scale of the breach

Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
3 hours ago

Cisco and OpenAI pitch eBPF kernel plugins as the way to tame the patchpocalypse
6 hours ago

Inception - possibly a ShinyHunters reboot - claims seven victims in a week
10 hours ago

Bluetooth brakes are broken as researchers find anyone can control them
13 hours ago

Critical Cisco remote code execution bug under attack
14 hours ago

Software e-commerce platform Cleverbridge has admitted that hackers stole customers' card numbers and CVV security codes, raising questions about how the attackers captured data that payment security rules prohibit retaining after authorization.

The Germany-headquartered company, which handles software sales and subscriptions on behalf of other businesses, detected unauthorized activity on its platform on November 4. In an incident update, Cleverbridge said it had cut off the intruders' access and launched an investigation that later established payment card information had been copied from its systems.

The public notice doesn't spell out exactly what was stolen, but an email sent to affected customers, seen by The Register, fills in some rather important blanks.

"Based on our investigation to date, the information involved includes payment card details you used to make a purchase through Cleverbridge, notably your card number, expiry date, card security code (CVV/CVC), and contact details," the company told customers.

Email addresses, billing addresses, and phone numbers were also among the information potentially exposed, although Cleverbridge reckons account passwords escaped the breach.

More concerning are the stolen CVV codes. PCI DSS rules prohibit retaining card verification codes after authorization, even in encrypted form.

Cleverbridge hasn't explained how the attackers obtained the codes, whether they were retained after transactions, or whether the criminals intercepted them during payment processing. The company didn't respond to The Register's questions about the breach.

Cleverbridge handles the business of selling software online, from taking payments to managing subscriptions, and its client roster includes some familiar names in enterprise IT, such as Red Hat, SUSE, Tenable, Veeam, Jamf, Parallels, and Plesk.

That doesn't mean customers of all those companies had their card details stolen. Cleverbridge hasn't named the vendors affected by the intrusion, nor has it disclosed how many people were caught up in the breach or how long the hackers were poking around its systems.

In its notification to customers, the company said it had contacted data protection authorities, payment card schemes, and its payment partners, and was working with a specialist police cybercrime unit. It began notifying affected individuals on November 20, more than two weeks after discovering the intrusion.

Affected customers are being offered 24 months of free credit monitoring and fraud assistance. That may help detect identity misuse, but it won't stop a crook armed with their card number, expiration date, and CVV from attempting a fraudulent purchase.

Two years of credit monitoring is a nice gesture, but customers whose card numbers and CVVs are now in criminal hands might prefer a new bit of plastic. ®

[40] TechCrunch Security - CrowdStrike to acquire Zscaler for $44 billion
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 16:20:11 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/23/crowdstrike-to-acquire-zscaler-for-44-billion/

[TAM METİN - 374 kelime]
CrowdStrike to acquire Zscaler for $44 billion

Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike has agreed to buy Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal, the companies announced Monday.

CrowdStrike and Zscaler said that the deal will create the “largest single addressable market” for cybersecurity, which will enable the companies to better position themselves for the AI era.

Zscaler is a San Jose, California-based tech company that provides cloud cybersecurity products to about 8,000 enterprise companies. The company offers zero-trust technology, a security feature that requires employees to authenticate each and every time they access an internal system or resource. The aim is to protect against hackers from stealing credentials and using them to gain unfettered access to a company’s network.

CrowdStrike, which secures the computers, laptops, phones, and other devices connected to a company’s network, said the deal will unite its endpoint protection software with Zscaler’s zero-trust platform to better defend their customers against data breaches. By combining the two products, CrowdStrike and Zscaler say that they can offer “superior protection” to their customers.

In remarks on Monday, the companies said that the acquisition will help customers safely adopt AI.

The deal, which is subject to regulatory approval, is expected to close around this time next year.

[41] TechCrunch Security - Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:29:49 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/23/dutch-privacy-authority-urges-consumers-not-to-buy-pervert-glasses/

[TAM METİN - 476 kelime]
Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’

The Dutch Data Protection Authority is urging consumers not to buy AI-enabled camera glasses, warning that they compromise the privacy of nearby bystanders and risk the safety of their users.

The authority, whose recent statement falls just short of an outright ban, said that use of the internet-enabled and always-on glasses to record people in public is “almost always prohibited.”

Critics have dubbed these smart wearables as “pervert glasses” for their propensity to be used to video people without their permission, particularly to harass women. While some glasses have warning lights when users are recording, privacy advocates have argued that these alerts are not clear enough. Some people have even made phone apps capable of detecting nearby smart glasses wearers.

The Dutch data protection authority warned that the glasses also produce wrong results and unsafe results. In one test carried out by the Dutch authority, a pair of unnamed glasses “gave dating advice to an adult who indicated that they are interested in a minor.” The glasses also incorrectly read a medicine label and claimed to have called an ambulance when it had not.

The Dutch government’s advice follows a similar move by the Norwegian government, which in September said it was considering a ban on camera-enabled devices.

[42] Dark Reading - Industrial Serial Gateways Broadcast That They're Insecure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 22:02:46 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/industrial-serial-gateways-broadcast-insecure

[TAM METİN - 1095 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
5 Min Read

![Image 1: Serial and Ethernet ports.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt301a7a187c53b85f/6b04a5d4187d8b84c6ba02e4/Serial_to_IP-Gri-spb-Getty.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Gri spb via Getty Images

Internet scans indicate that some substantial fraction of serial device servers deployed at [operational technology](https://www.techtarget.com/news/operational-technology/872649/) (OT) sites worldwide not only lack authentication but broadcast the fact publicly.

We all have a friend or a family member who's deeply insecure, and it's obvious as soon as they start talking. The same can be said of serial-to-IP gateways. Recently Adrian Cheek, senior cybercrime researcher for Flare, [probed](https://flare.io/learn/resources/blog/exposed-industrial-serial-gateways) the wider Internet for these sorts of devices, finding that a substantial chunk of them are discoverable. Worse, they often responded to straightforward requests by saying whether they were or were not protected by authentication — and many were not.

An [insecure serial-to-IP gateway](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/serial-ip-devices-thousands-of-bugs) is a serious risk to anyone downstream of it. These devices are responsible for translating information between the Internet and industrial machinery. So in a cyberattack, a reachable and unauthenticated one can inadvertently translate an Internet-based threat into one that affects major, safety-critical machines.

Related:[How to Assess OT Incident Response Preparedness](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/assess-ot-incident-response-preparedness)

## Crucial OT Devices Spill the Beans

Using a specific vendor's [UDP](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-User-Datagram-Protocol-UDP/858069/) discovery service, rather than probing all vendors' devices, Cheek found 2,474 serial-to-IP gateways exposed on the Web. They seemed to map to real devices rather than honeypots, with half of them located in Russia.

For some reason, in response to the researcher's unsolicited probes, around half of the devices — 1,265 — proffered their authentication status. Of those that did, 973 reported that authentication was disabled. In other words, 39.3% of all Internet-exposed gateways visibly lacked authentication, and among those whose status could be gleaned, that figure was 77%.

"These devices ship with authentication off. An integrator sets one up on a bench, gets the meter or the drive or the Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) talking to the SCADA host, and moves on to the next site," Cheek laments. "The device then does one job for 10 or 15 years, and nobody logs into it again. In many cases, the operator doesn't even know it exists because it went in as part of someone else's equipment."

There are also understandable, if not entirely justifiable reasons why operators fail to conceal and lock down their serial device servers. Cheek cites a multitude of reasons, including that enabling such scurity features can break software on the host side.

"Older SCADA packages and virtual serial port drivers may not support the secured modes, so an operator who switches them on can lose the link to the field," he says. "Then someone has to drive to an unstaffed site to fix it. Firmware updates carry the same risk at a remote site with no spare on hand. People who have been burned by that once are slow to touch a working device again."

Related:[Why Europe's Freezer Hacks Spread So Far, So Fast](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/europes-freezer-hacks-spread-so-far-so-fast)

Still, an exposed, unauthenticated serial gateway is a welcome mat for bad actors. Not only does it give arbitrary actors entry into sensitive OT systems and eliminates the need to exploit any kind of vulnerabilities, it also allows attackers to use the vendor's own supplied tools, which could blend malicious activity in with normal IT-OT traffic.

Cheek made special mention of the risk to mobile network operators and, even more specifically, private access point name (APN) service providers. Last year's [Polish power grid attack](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/cisa-critical-infrastructure-warning-poland-energy-hack/811819/) used a private APN to pivot from a wind farm's network to the power plant where the attackers ultimately shut down a steam turbine, water treatment system, and seven serial-to-IP devices. According to Flare, 322 of the 2,474 discovered devices in the study lived in address space associated with known mobile network operators in Europe and the Middle East.

## How to Protect Serial Gateways

OT operators can begin to secure their OT access layer by first doing an inventory of the devices they're running. "For every cellular unit, find out what plan the SIM is on and whether it gets a public address. Then ask your carrier in writing whether devices on your private network can reach each other or devices belonging to other customers," Cheek notes. "Don't assume the managed product isolates them. In the Polish incident, that missing isolation is what let the attacker move between two unrelated organizations."

Related:[Fresh European Fridge Attacks Put Cold-Chain Security in Focus](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/fresh-european-fridge-attacks-cold-chain-security)

Next, he advises, "Lock down the data ports as well as the login. Most of these devices let you restrict which IP addresses can open a connection. Configure them so only the SCADA host can reach the serial ports and only an engineering workstation can reach the management interface. Switch off the services you don't use, which on most units means the discovery service, Telnet, [older SNMP](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-Simple-Network-Management-Protocol-SNMP/858154/), and often the Web interface. Remove SSH and Telnet entirely from anything that faces the carrier network," and plop a strict firewall between the gateway and control network.

There are also general practices organizations can put in place, like ensuring that the next time they purchase serial device servers, a person or team is assigned to be responsible for the devices, and that they have necessary credentials, configuration, and network details from system integrators.

At the end of the day, though, nothing compares to preventing an attacker's ability to find and speak to a device in the first place.

"Even a fully patched gateway with authentication on is usually connected to equipment on the serial side that has no security of its own," Cheek points out. "A meter, a drive, or an RTU will accept any correctly formed command that reaches it. So the useful question is who can reach the gateway at all, because whoever can reach it gets whatever is behind it. Authentication and patching both matter, but restricting what can reach the gateway does more good than either."

[43] Dark Reading - CrowdStrike Makes AI Security Push With Blockbuster Zscaler Deal
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 21:21:42 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/crowdstrike-ai-security-push-zscaler-deal

[TAM METİN - 1358 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A Zscaler office building.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltffaa274e5fcd829e/68386b0dd0a57a8348fa8ace/Zscaler_SundryPhotography_Alamy.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Sundry Photograph via Alamy Stock Photo

CrowdStrike has agreed to acquire [Zscaler](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/zscaler-buyout-red-canary-telemetrys-value) for $43.9 billion in stock, combining endpoint protection and zero-trust network security to help enterprises control AI agents running across their systems.

The deal, announced Monday, aims to give chief information security officers (CISOs) a single supplier for security controls spanning endpoints where agents operate and the network connections used to reach applications and data.

The proposed transaction surpasses Google’s $32 billion [acquisition of Wiz](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/google-bets-agentic-defense-strategy-outpace-attackers), making it the largest announced cybersecurity acquisition on record. For customers, what remains to be seen is whether the companies can turn their existing integrations into unified enforcement while preserving support for competing products.

CrowdStrike co-founder and CEO George Kurtz said the [proliferation of AI agents](https://www.darkreading.com/identity-access-management-security/ai-agents-bring-enterprises-new-access-control-challenges) has changed the role of network security, making Zscaler’s traffic inspection complementary to the Falcon platform's visibility into endpoints and cloud workloads.

Related:[Microsoft Debuts Offensive, Defensive Security Tools for the AI Era](https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/microsoft-offense-defense-ai-security-tools)

"The network used to connect people to places," Kurtz said during an investor call announcing the deal and discussing CrowdStrike's fiscal third-quarter results. "Now it connects agents to data."

CrowdStrike expects the transaction to close in the second half of fiscal 2028, subject to customary closing conditions. The companies will operate independently until closing, chief financial officer (CFO) Burt Podbere said.

Bringing network and endpoint security into a common control plane makes sense even without the growth of AI agents, according to cybersecurity analyst Frank Dickson, founder of Dickson Research. Their increasing use makes the combination more compelling, he says.

## Connecting Endpoint Activity With Network Enforcement

CrowdStrike's Falcon Guardian discovers AI agents, governs their access, and protects them at runtime, Kurtz said. Zscaler's Zero Trust Exchange provides inline inspection and access controls for traffic moving to applications and services. Combining those capabilities would connect what an agent does on a device with where it sends data, according to the two companies.

Kurtz described the planned integration with an example of an agent on a laptop pulling sensitive information and sending it to an unapproved AI model. Falcon would see the agent's activity on the device, while Zscaler would see the destination on the network.

"Together, it will be one detection, one decision, and one kill switch that stops the agent on the device and cuts its path on the network," Kurtz said.

Related:[Agentic Browsers Rewind Web Security by 20 Years](https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/agentic-browsers-rewind-web-security-20-years)

The companies already share 16 integrations, and customers send Zscaler data into CrowdStrike's Next-Gen SIEM. Kurtz said the combination would bring their telemetry together in that platform and use the expanded dataset to support CrowdStrike's security AI models.

Zscaler founder, chairman, and CEO Jay Chaudhry said combining the companies will allow them to go beyond what they can accomplish through existing partner integrations, including coordinating policy enforcement across endpoints, cloud environments, and the network.

"Imagine being able to have a unified policy that can do all this stuff," Chaudhry said during the conference call.

Dickson says Zscaler's network-centric architecture should make integrating the endpoint software less burdensome than it might seem, because much of Zscaler's processing happens in its distributed network rather than on the device.

"The integration is going to be much lighter because much of the heavy lifting done by Zscaler is done in the network," he tells Dark Reading.

Kurtz said CrowdStrike's next-generation endpoint sensor already has network traffic inspection and proxy capabilities that should help with integration. Asked during the call by Deutsche Bank analyst Brad Zelnick how the deal would affect CrowdStrike's single-software agent architecture, Kurtz reiterated that commitment without providing a delivery date for the combined agent.

Related:[Agentic AI Challenges Progress in Confidential Computing](https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/agentic-ai-challenges-progress-in-confidential-computing)

"The brand promise, single agent. That's not going to change," he said.

## Competitive Implications of the Zscaler Acquisition

Dickson believes the combination could change the competitive landscape, identifying Microsoft as the vendor with a comparable breadth while questioning whether it could match the depth of the software giant's combined offerings.

"It fundamentally changes the competitiveness of the industry, 100%," he says.

That does not mean Palo Alto Networks, for example, needs another acquisition to respond. Dickson says it already has the technologies to compete, pointing particularly to its identity capabilities and its [CyberArk deal](https://www.darkreading.com/identity-access-management-security/palo-alto-networks-grabs-iam-provider-cyberark-for-25b).

Palo Alto Networks is also pursuing a different route into enterprise AI. Its Nov. 18 agreement to acquire Applied Compute for $4.5 billion in stock would extend its reach into training and deploying open-weight AI models. While CrowdStrike is emphasizing controls over where agents run and communicate, Palo Alto is moving further into the infrastructure used to [build and operate enterprise AI](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Gartner-forecasts-massive-AI-infrastructure-write-downs/856923/).

"They're changing the terms of competition away from functionality to trust," Dickson says of Palo Alto's approach.

Dickson also points to [Cisco's expansion in identity](https://www.darkreading.com/identity-access-management-security/cisco-adds-nhi-security-stack-with-astrix-widefield), underscoring the wider competition to connect identity with security enforcement.

For other security vendors, Dickson says the [Zscaler acquisition](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/crowdstrike-acquire-zscaler-439-billion-turbulent-threat/875477/) increases the importance of partnerships and differentiation by industry or customer size. He says Microsoft’s broad security portfolio already required that approach; the CrowdStrike-Zscaler combination would add another large platform competitor.

"You need as many friends as you can, and then niche as hard as you can,”"Dickson says.

## Customer Choice and Purchasing

For CISOs, the competitive shift also raises the question whether a broader platform can simplify security operations without limiting product choice.

After closing, CrowdStrike intends to bring Zscaler's offerings into Falcon Flex, allowing customers to expand across both portfolios through the same subscription model. Podbere said cross-selling and broader adoption would help generate approximately $1 billion in annualized revenue savings within 30 months after closing.

Deutsche Bank's Zelnick asked what the transaction would mean for customers that want another endpoint or [secure access service edge](https://www.techtarget.com/news/secure-access-service-edge-SASE/858879/) (SASE) provider. Kurtz emphasized the single-agent strategy rather than detailing how those mixed-vendor deployments would work.

Separately, responding to a question about vendor lock-in, Kurtz said CrowdStrike would continue working with outside technologies and accepting data from other vendors.

Dickson says coexistence with competing network security products is already common, and he does not see the acquisition itself as a reason for those customers to change course. Deeper integration could make adding Zscaler more attractive to existing CrowdStrike customers, he says.

## Identity Remains a Challenge for CrowdStrike-Zscaler

Despite his enthusiasm, Dickson says the CrowdStrike-Zscaler combination needs stronger identity capabilities to deliver on its AI-agent security ambitions. Discovering an agent and inspecting its traffic are not the same as establishing its identity and determining precisely what it is authorized to do.

Agents need a registry, authentication tied to their identities, and fine-grained authorization, Dickson says, though he questions whether either company’s current portfolio fully provides that combination of capabilities.

CrowdStrike is [working to address](https://www.darkreading.com/identity-access-management-security/ai-agents-bring-enterprises-new-access-control-challenges) that area. During the investor Q&A, Kurtz said the company expects to introduce an agent identity provider around January, with controls intended to provide just-in-time access and zero standing privileges across human, machine, and agent identities.

Dickson says the challenge is expanding as employees outside software development gain the ability to build agents. Security teams must contend not only with more agents but also with greater autonomy and creators who may lack experience building secure applications.
