[44] Dark Reading - US Cyber Director Pick Signals Shift Toward Expertise
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 20:13:48 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/us-cyber-director-pick-signals-shift-toward-expertise

[TAM METİN - 775 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
3 Min Read

![Image 1: The White House in Washington, D.C.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt9a0966356bb6bbf4/6b048a6e805d232dd3e79e51/white_house-uschools-GettyImages-160196566.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: uschools via Getty Images

The Office of the National Cyber Director will have a new, more technically experienced director at the helm, but how that might impact US cyber policy remains to be seen.

[President Donald Trump](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/trump-tech-giants-strike-voluntary-ai-safety-accord) on Nov. 19 tapped Kirsten Davies to lead the White House Office of the National Cyber Director, replacing Sean Cairncross, who is [leaving the administration](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/national-cyber-director-sean-cairncross-trump-white-house/875294/). Last December, Davies was appointed the chief information officer of the Department of Defense.

In a Nov. 20 [news release](https://www.whitehouse.gov/releases/2026/11/president-trump-taps-pentagon-cyber-chief-kirsten-davies-as-national-cyber-director/), the White House said Davies "will drive an America First cyber strategy that treats cybersecurity as national security and keeps the United States dominant and secure in cyberspace." Upon last Thursday's announcement, Davies became Acting United States National Cyber Director, though she requires Senate confirmation to gain permanent directorship.

In [a post](https://x.com/_KirstenDavies_/status/2123595758465089690) on social media platform X, Davies wrote that the US has an opportunity "to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come … but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on, as well as build cyber talent for our future."

Related:[Behind the Alias: How One Researcher Unmasked a Decade of Ransomware Actors](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/alias-researcher-unmasked-ransomware-actors)

## A More Experienced Cyber Director

Though not officially tied to Davies' appointment, Cairncross's departure occurred amid [a number of outages](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/technology-outages-cyberattacks-ai-house-hearing/875148/) affecting the public sector. Just this month, there was the outage against the Federal Aviation Administration that lasted for days, as well as an NSA communications network outage that apparently resulted from a software update. Other disruptions hit major banks and Microsoft services.

Melinda Marks, senior research director and chief analyst of cybersecurity at Omdia (an Informa TechTarget property), tells Dark Reading the [outages](https://www.techtarget.com/news/everything-is-down-what-a-week-of-outages-exposed/875381/) seen in recent weeks have created significant speculation about US security posture. As such, Davies's appointment comes at a time where cybersecurity's role in national security is "more important than ever."

A source close to the White House told Dark Reading sister publication [Cybersecurity Dive](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/national-cyber-director-sean-cairncross-trump-white-house/875294/) that Trump fired Cairncross over concerns regarding his cybersecurity credentials; [a source told Semafor's Ashley Gold](https://x.com/ashleyrgold/status/2123588476931358953) Cairncross resigned of his own volition.

Jacob Krell, senior director for secure AI solutions and cybersecurity at Suzu Labs, tells Dark Reading that Cairncross is primarily an election lawyer [who admitted at his June 2025 confirmation hearing](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/sean-cairncross-national-cyber-director-confirmation-hearing/749993/) that he had no technical background in cyber. Meanwhile, Davies has previously held security leadership roles at Unilever, The Estée Lauder Companies, Barclays, Hewlett-Packard, and Siemens.

Related:[In Security, Words Matter as Much as Tools, Maybe More](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/security-words-matter)

"It is a definite shift from a political appointee to one with much more experience in the industry," he says. "Given the string of massive outages and rising geopolitical tensions, it could not come at a better time."

## An Uncertain Future for US Cyber Policy

It remains to be seen how a new National Cyber Director appointment may shape the future of cybersecurity in the US; should she be appointed, Davies stands to have substantial influence, given she would serve as Trump's primary adviser regarding cybersecurity policy and strategy.

Since Trump began his second term at the beginning of 2025, the makeup of US cyber has [shifted immensely](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/white-house-cyber-strategy-prioritizes-offense), between a culling of public sector cyber personnel, a lean into [AI technologies](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/gold-eagle-clearinghouse-targets-security-gap), the shuttering of [the Cyber Safety Review Board](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/trump-fires-cyber-safety-board-salt-typhoon-hackers), and more.

Krell says he expects Davies's appointment to potentially shift cyber funding from AI-as-opportunity toward capacity-related expenditures, such as people, test environments, and change control. And as global tensions rise, he expects to see more focus on infrastructure and [industrial control systems](https://www.techtarget.com/news/ICS-security-threats-rising-targeting-oil-and-gas-facilities/839963/) (ICS).

"Last week showed what machine-speed patching does to human-sized teams," he argues. "AI gets reclassified in the process, from capability accelerator to governed risk."

Related:[Why Holiday Security Can't Wait for Black Friday](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/why-holiday-security-cant-wait-black-friday)

[45] Dark Reading - Behind the Alias: How One Researcher Unmasked a Decade of Ransomware Actors
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 17:45:52 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/alias-researcher-unmasked-ransomware-actors

[TAM METİN - 1352 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
It's a now-familiar scenario: "Warning" abruptly displays on the computer screen as the system locks down. A ransom note follows, threatening data extortion and continued disruptions if a demand is not met. Although it may seem sudden at the moment, the attack was actually the final step in what may have been years of planning, all carried out by a sprawling, interconnected network of underground actors.

But a new framework based on an identity graph analysis, developed by Flare threat researcher Assaf Morag, aims to help defenders and law enforcement take a more proactive approach to ransomware risk by better understanding who they're up against. When applied during research, the framework can uncover the inner workings of these operations, including indicators of compromise (IoCs) that are critical to incident-response investigations. Researchers can also use it to find connections among various threat actors and groups, which is increasingly important as [new groups emerge](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/gentlemen-rapidly-rise-ransomware) each year.

Related:[As Test Takers Turn to AI, Exam Security Plays Catch-Up](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/test-takers-ai-exam-security-catch-up)

Mapping and understanding the connections among the various ransomware gangs is also vital when it comes to documenting the tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) that ransomware groups use against organizations daily. Threat actors [are highly adaptable](https://www.darkreading.com/identity-access-management-security/identity-attacks-overtake-exploits-top-ransomware-cause), developing, buying, or renting new tools all the time to bypass endpoint detection and response (EDR) and to craft more convincing phishing emails. So the more available data about their operations, the more predictable their behaviors become for defenders.

Morag plans to debut his repeatable investigative methodology for mapping criminal organizations in[a briefing at Black Hat Europe 2026.](https://blackhat.com/europe/briefings/schedule/#reconstructing-a-ransomware-organization-from-underground-breadcrumbs-mapping-the-hidden-business-ecosystem-of-modern-cybercrime-57081)He will walk through the framework step by step; anyone can use it to reproduce the research or apply the same process to a new investigation.

## Following the Breadcrumbs

Building a framework wasn't Morag's original goal. He first set out to study how [initial access brokers (IABs)](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/access-broker-russian-state-cybercrime)operate on Dark Web forums. But he realized the interconnected web of threat actors was too vast to dissect using available resources. His efforts to connect gang operators and affiliates to IABs actually took two long months of manual work, which wasn't a useful process for such a swiftly moving threat landscape. That's when Morag decided to develop a framework to help graph the activity. Once it was ready, the same research took only three hours.

To build the framework, Morag went into detective mode, gathering clues from forums and chat posts and sorting them into various buckets for "hard evidence" versus "soft evidence." The first included factors like specific messaging IDs, emails, or chat handles, while writing styles, typos, and reused phrases fell into the latter. Once he was able to verify a clue, he could use it to search for other connections. Eventually, the confirmations expanded into a massive map of connected identities with 21 personae, active over 17 years, with 5,437 posts.

Related:[NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/nist-solo-shouldnt-mean-less-secure)

Morag's findings support how ransomware attacks are rarely conducted by a single group from start to finish, and how operations often [mirror legitimate businesses](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/ransomware-explosion-thriving-dark-web-ecosystem) with everyone playing their role. That makes it hard to parse out exactly who's who.

That became obvious when Morag came across a notification posted to a [Dark Web forum](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/cybercrime-ecosystem-spawns-lucrative-underground-gig-economy), translated from Russian, that read, "We are the oldest international Russian ransomware affiliate program." The group claimed to be a private program that emerged in 2018. But based on underground forum discussions, he noticed that some members had been active since 2010.

Tracking Tox IDs was the primary way Morag traced the connections, unveiling the true identity behind the fake personae and generic aliases threat actors use, like "John" or "Michael." Ransomware ecosystems commonly use communication platforms like Tox, Discord, Telegram, and Jabber to conduct payment negotiations with organizations or to recruit new affiliates, among other actions.

Related:[Incidents Move Too Fast for Pen & Paper](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/incidents-move-fast-pen-paper)

When communicating with someone through a specific Tox address, Morag knew he was reaching one consistent endpoint, whether cloud-based or someone's personal computer. Either way, it's the same device every time, which matters because threat actors often operate under multiple aliases.

"He can change his name, but if I can take these identifiers and connect them together, that could be a way to look beyond the persona's name, which is usually fake, and that's exactly what I did," Morag tells Dark Reading.

During the course of investigation, Morag discovered six layers of operational roles across the business flow:

1.   The homemade ransom layer: Useful for defenders to study, these are the people who develop ransomware strains, loader malware that bypasses antivirus and EDR, and infostealer payloads. 
2.   The ransom deployment layer: The gang operators and their affiliates who select the ransomware/malware, deploy it, and extort the victims. 
3.   Pen-testers: Folks who probe for weaknesses to find ways into organizations. 
4.   The dealers: IABs that buy and sell that initial access, like a sales department for cybercrime. (Morag was able to pinpoint seven individuals active for over nine years, spanning 50 forums and a whopping 1,300 events.) 
5.   Fraud recovery: Organizations that perform diagnostics, negotiate with attackers, and arrange settlements with victims. 
6.   Crypto draining: Those that process stolen seed phrases and private keys at scale and cross-sell crypto services to ransomware victims to compromise and drain wallets. 

## Inside the Modern Ransomware Ecosystem

While piecing it together, one link particularly surprised Morag: a tie between supposed good-guy fraud-recovery firms and the ransomware deployers. Companies [claim to help](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ransom-busters-ransomware-actor-incident-recovery-service) ransomware victims but secretly defraud them, he notes, and victim organizations have no clue.

Up until the fraud recovery layer in the business flow, everything was done in Russian. After that, Morag noticed an intentional switch to the English language. Once the ransomware locked an organization's system down, the ransomware deployers swooped in, posing as legitimate businesses looking to provide recovery services. They installed tools on victims' systems, ran diagnostics, offered data recovery, and legal compliance services, Morag says.

"This is not new, and we've also seen a few cases in the past month in the US. These kinds of organizations that help other organizations fight against ransomware were actually defrauding them," Morag explains. "I haven't seen anyone make the connection between those who do the recovery fraud and those that actually deploy the ransom, which is really amazing."

While deep in the underground forums, it also became clear to Morag how much threat actors value their reputations. For instance, on Exploit, "a reputable Russian market" where attackers pay $100 to $150 and undergo a test to get in, Morag observed infighting as threat actors accused one another of not being legitimate. The forum is a place where "you don’t want to lose your reputation" or your access, he stresses, because it's where threat actors discuss sensitive dealings, such as zero-day and known vulnerability exploits, and forge new ransomware-as-a-service partnerships. Looking into these disputes offered critical intelligence about how operations run because threat actors will throw each other under the bus by revealing what tools they use, how they move money, or what access they are trading.

Exploit, Morag says, is "a real gold mine because it explains how they work, what they're doing, the level of leadership, and who reports to who."

Exploit was one of more than 40 underground forums he visited that, when analyzed together with the framework, revealed the inner workings of cybercrime gangs. Unmasking the players provides more evidence for organizations to fight back against ransomware as the threat disrupts everything, from Coca-Cola production to [Japanese frozen food](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ransomware-attack-japanese-frozen-food-chain).

The framework can't directly help organizations push back against ransomware, but it does provide much-needed contextual data for the private and public sectors as problems persist. Morag is applying the framework to help with attacks against financial systems — an obviously huge target for threat actors — for example. He also applied it to investigate another ransomware group more recently and says it yielded helpful results.

Real insight into a large operation like the one Morag discovered, knowing it included a fake fraud recovery layer, may also make organizations think twice about negotiating. They never know who's on the other line.

"It's [about] knowing your enemy," Morag says.

[46] Dark Reading - AI Agents Zip From Flaw Discovery to Root Access in 11 Minutes
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 16:17:21 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ai-agents-flaw-discovery-root-access-11-minutes

[TAM METİN - 1026 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: A hand using a pen to point to a screen that says "AI agents" with various AI icons surrounding it.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt068557e4f1edea18/6b0424213e73ecdcfb851a70/working-with-AI-agents-ImageFlow-shutterstock.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Image Flow via Shutterstock

A China-linked threat actor went from [discovering a vulnerability](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/ai-driven-bug-tsunami-nist-looks-to-ai) in an application to gaining root access to the server it was running on in about 11 minutes, using [Cairn AI agents](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/ai-tools-massive-attacks-little-cost). The incident, uncovered by researchers at Amazon Threat Intelligence, offers yet another example of how quickly attackers can optimize their operations using AI.

The campaign, which ran for approximately 48 hours beginning Sept. 8, targeted universities across East Asia outside mainland China, with probing and reconnaissance aimed at taking control of IT systems, according to a [report](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/) published Friday by Amazon Threat Intelligence.

"The actor gave the agents a simple goal: 'obtain shell access,'" CJ Moses, chief information security officer (CISO) of Amazon Web Services (AWS), wrote in the report. During the campaign, the agents discovered a zero-day in an exposed open source medical imaging application and used it to gain full control of a server in about 11 minutes.

Related:[China's 'Armory Castle' Lays Siege to VMware vCenter Zero-Day](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/china-armory-castle-vmware-vcenter-zero-day)

"These findings show that agentic offensive operations are already happening, and defenders should prepare for the speed and persistence these tools enable," he wrote.

The revelation follows reports of other China-linked actors using Cairn-based AI agents in various attacks, including [breaching 10 organizations](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/china-nexus-actors-breach-10-organizations-ai-driven-attacks) to steal secrets and [automating a massive attack spree](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/hackers-abuse-ai-pen-test-tool-cairn) against high-profile websites. Cairn is an open source, AI-based penetration testing tool that threat actors are using, along with [a similar tool called Artex](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/hackers-artex-github-repositories-exposed-secrets/874007/), to facilitate and automate their cyberattacks.

## Cairn Used in Extensive Autonomous Operations

In this case, the AI agents conducted two extensive operations that failed before they found and exploited the zero-day, demonstrating their adaptability and persistence even without human direction, Moses said.

The most comprehensive unsuccessful operation involved attempts to take control of a [Redis instance](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/redis-servers-infested-sophisticated-custom-built-malware) at one university that was configured to allow unauthenticated access. The effort lasted about 16 hours and involved 148 sequential actions. Along the way, the agents worked through a long list of known techniques for taking control of an exposed Redis server and also [attempted Log4Shell injection](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/java4shell-flaw-threatens-global-java-application-ecosystem) against another service running on the same system.

A separate operation lasted more than six hours and targeted a university GPU server. The agents began with a list of about 80 real usernames, which they assembled by researching the university's public presence.

Using those names, they unsuccessfully tested more than 30,000 [credential combinations](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/sweeping-credential-harvesting-heist-compromises-30k-fortinet-devices) against the server's remote login service (SSH). They also attempted access to an admin dashboard, network storage device, VPN server, and campus portal across the university network.

Related:[Nok et norsk energiselskap har blitt utsatt for et datainnbrudd på to dager](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached-norwegian)

"Neither operation succeeded, yet together they show that the agents kept adapting for nearly a full day without human direction," Moses observed in the post.

The agents eventually succeeded against a target running an open source medical imaging AI application. They first identified the application's framework and mapped its [API endpoints](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-an-API-endpoint/858757/), finding an upload endpoint with a filename parameter. When they tested the parameter for path traversal, they scored their first win: The endpoint was vulnerable, Moses said.

"In about 11 minutes, the agents went from mapping the application's API to arbitrarily writing files on the server," he wrote. The agents then used the same vulnerability to execute a Python reverse shell payload as root on the compromised server.

## Embrace Security Fundamentals, But Faster

There's been plenty of discussion about how [AI agents](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/agentic-ai-cyberattacks-harnesses-crowdstrike/833714/) operate at "machine speed," taking far less time than humans to find and exploit flaws. But in this case, the speed at which the agents found the vulnerability isn't what's most impressive in 2026, says Cris Thomas, a former hacker with the L0pht Heavy Industries collective and current security advocate at security platform provider Semgrep.

Related:[Second Norwegian Energy Company Breached in 2 Days](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached)

That would be the case "if it was 2017," he says. What's notable instead is that AI agents are doing this without a skilled human involved. That "changes the economics of vulnerability discovery, and defenders need to take advantage of the same technology," Thomas observes.

"Attackers are getting faster, but the answer isn't to abandon the fundamentals; it is to double down on them," he says. "They are fundamental for a reason."

Those fundamentals include monitoring for suspicious uploads, unexpected file writes, and unusual process execution, as well as reviewing code as it's written, Thomas says. "[These are] the same things we should have been doing for decades, only now a whole lot faster," he notes.

Just as AI agents operate at [machine speed](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ai-machine-speed-2-week-attack-10-hours), "defenders need to make those fundamentals work at machine speed, with automated detection and containment backed by human oversight for consequential decisions," Thomas advises.

Another defense against AI-driven attacks is "continuously analyzing server logs, [which] helps IT teams learn what normal activity looks like and spot unusual patterns that could signal an attack in progress," says Ross Filipek, CISO at cybersecurity consulting firm Corsica Technologies.

Given that the agents targeted exposed endpoints and servers, Filipek also recommends that organizations maintain an inventory of exposed assets, restrict access to applications that only staff need, and decide in advance who has authority to isolate a server after hours.

"That's a decision you want settled before the alert comes in," Filipek says.

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Elizabeth Montalbano](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd6f16d37895d5bfd/6a06cc5deb9f4ad4d2785f3f/LizHeadshot2026.png?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/elizabeth-montalbano)

Contributing Writer

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

[47] Dark Reading - China's 'Armory Castle' Lays Siege to VMware vCenter Zero-Day
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 21:03:27 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/china-armory-castle-vmware-vcenter-zero-day

[TAM METİN - 916 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A Chinese fortress.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt911ff7b35a2a2c1d/6b009babdd63b308b3111bc2/Chinese_fortress-Lukas_Bischoff-Getty.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Lukas Bischoff via Getty Images

Chinese state-sponsored hackers have been using a newly disclosed VMware zero-day vulnerability and an array of custom malware to compromise victims' virtualization stacks for several months.

In October, Mandiant described a [campaign by China's Armory Castle](https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/beyondtrust-remote-support-exploited-edge-bugs) (formerly UNC5221) to exploit organizations through BeyondTrust's Remote Support tool. The group used a zero-day to breach the edge appliance, then stole credentials and secrets useful for network reconnaissance and downstream compromises within organizations. Exactly what that downstream compromise looked like, though, was unclear at the time.

It appears now that BeyondTrust was merely an entrée to a deeper, more interesting, and much more complex campaign. In a follow-up blog post this week, Mandiant researchers [described](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/armory-castle-exploits-vsphere) a chained attack beginning with BeyondTrust, moving through obscure Linux appliances, and landing on VMware technologies, at which point the attackers utilized another zero-day and more than half a dozen malware programs to totally undress their targets' [virtual machine](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-a-virtual-machine-VM-and-how-does-it-work/857966/) (VM) environments.

Related:[AI Agents Zip From Flaw Discovery to Root Access in 11 Minutes](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ai-agents-flaw-discovery-root-access-11-minutes)

That zero-day has only now come to light, months since China first exploited it. In a [security advisory](https://support.broadcom.com/web/ecx/support-content-notification/-/external/content/SecurityAdvisories/0/39495), VMware owner Broadcom recommended that customers update their systems. Tracked as CVE-2026-123890, the vulnerability allows for authentication bypass in VMware vCenter and earned a "critical" 9.8 out of 10 score in the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS).

## Armory Castle Exploits VMware vCenter Flaw

From tagged Remote Support boxes, Mandiant observed Armory Castle scanning a victim's internal environment, looking for open ports associated with VMware.

Instead of going for the goods immediately, the group established forward operating bases inside other, possibly unrelated Linux appliances in the same network. These appliances lacked endpoint detection and response (EDR) visibility, allowing the group to establish persistence and stage its malware arsenal away from the parts of the network where defenders were looking. Among other malware, it was there that Armory Castle deployed "Bonesaw," a Go-based remote exploitation tool customized for vCenter.

The VMware vCenter Server is the central control plane used to manage multiple VMs and their [ESXi hosts](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/37k-vmware-esxi-instances-vulnerable-to-critical-zero-day/741749/). Because it oversees everything else, few technologies are more useful for lateral movement, ransomware attacks, etc., than vCenter is. It's why this summer, the disclosure of two critical vCenter vulnerabilities, CVE-2026-59309 and [CVE-2026-59310](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/global-threat-campaign-critical-vmware-vcenter-flaw), caused a stir.

Related:[Nok et norsk energiselskap har blitt utsatt for et datainnbrudd på to dager](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached-norwegian)

While organizations were rushing to patch the two vCenter vulnerabilities known to Broadcom, Armory Castle appears to have had an extra one up its sleeve. In September, it was able to use Bonesaw against a victim that had already patched its vCenter; Bonesaw was tuned to exploit [CVE-2026-123890,](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/chinese-espionage-zero-day-flaw-vmware-vcenter/875273/) a flaw that stems from the vCenter login system's use of predictable, time-based UUIDv1 timestamp generation in Secure Remote Password (SRP) authentication handling.

Exploiting CVE-2026-123890 afforded Armory Castle a shared session key it could use to create new authenticated sessions as an administrator, bypassing the normal authentication process entirely. Bonesaw then paid more dividends, automating the process of copying vCenter's internal directory database and creating powerful new accounts for further damage.

At this point, Armory Castle had total control over the victim's vCenter. Yet it continued on with machine-like focus; the attackers deployed a tool called "Sawsnake" to turn the Bonesaw-stolen vCenter login-signing key into forged authentication tokens, bypassing multifactor authentication (MFA) and enabling impersonation of any user. It then jumped to [ESXi hypervisors](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/state-linked-actor-targets-vmware-malware/632951/) and found and extracted system files.

## Protecting VMs from Nation-State Threats

For high-risk organizations, updating VMware instances to patched versions can't be the end of the story.

Related:[Second Norwegian Energy Company Breached in 2 Days](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/second-norwegian-energy-company-breached)

Because vCenter is just so sensitive, Mandiant urged organizations not to rely solely on patching known vulnerabilities as they come up. It pointed to its vSphere [Defender's Guide](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/vsphere-brickstorm-defender-guide), published earlier this spring. Its recommendations include the standard fare — promptly updating and isolating management systems, encrypting particularly sensitive systems, and applying especially tight controls to administrator accounts — but also offers more pointed items. It notes that vCenter doesn't necessarily record all important operating system (OS) activity, and that sophisticated attackers can delete local logs post-compromise; therefore, organizations should consider forwarding logs to a separate [security information and event management](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-analytics/siems-dying-slow-death-ai-rebirth) (SIEM) platform.

Mandiant researchers also advised that organizations not rely on vCenter's built-in firewall, as it has too many limitations. For example, it doesn't generate remote logs for rejected connection attempts or certain shell activity, doesn't enforce policies for [data egress](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-data-egress-How-it-works-and-how-to-manage-costs/842738/), and, most of all, it's simply at risk for being built into the very system it's designed to secure.

"Organizations must adopt a proactive, Zero Trust approach to infrastructure hardening to defend against current and undiscovered zero-day vulnerabilities," the researchers wrote.

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Nate Nelson](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt8edb70f1c73f8a5a/6978c3c26fdea754c9f2e2e9/Nate_Nelson_bio_pic_2-crop_2.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/nate-nelson)

Contributing Writer

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

[48] SecurityWeek - Azure Flaw Exploited to Target Secrets at Norwegian Energy Firms
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:47:40 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/azure-flaw-exploited-to-target-secrets-at-norwegian-energy-firms/

[TAM METİN - 3255 kelime]
Azure Flaw Exploited to Target Secrets at Norwegian Energy Firms

Microsoft closed an Azure API Management flaw exploited to target secrets at Norwegian energy companies, with other sectors also affected.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

A security issue discovered in Microsoft’s Azure API Management (APIM) service appears to have been responsible for the security incidents reported last week by two Norwegian energy firms, with impact extending beyond Norway. 
Microsoft is notifying customers about the issue in Azure APIM, which is designed for publishing, securing and managing APIs. The vulnerability may have allowed threat actors to gain access to secrets stored in some APIM instances.



According to a private notification shared on Reddit by one customer, the tech giant discovered the issue on November 18 and closed the access path the next day. 



“Our investigation found that customers were exposed and an unauthorized party could have used the issue to access secrets stored in Azure API Management Classic instances before Microsoft identified the security issue,” Microsoft said.



Exposed data can include secrets stored as named values, client secrets for identity providers, subscription keys, and tokens for APIM’s direct management APIs.



Microsoft noted that APIM Classic tiers that don’t follow its documented security guidance are affected. Instances on v2 tiers are not impacted.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Norway’s National Security Authority (NSM) said on November 20 that the vulnerability had affected several organizations in the country, including energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi, which independently disclosed cybersecurity incidents last week. 



Equinor reported the attack on November 17, one day before Microsoft identified the APIM flaw. The company said it found no evidence that data was stolen or altered. Skagerak Energi disclosed the incident on November 18, saying it did not impact operations or production, and it found no evidence of information compromise.



NSM said an unknown threat actor targeted tokens, secrets and named values stored in APIM. It’s unclear how widely the weakness has been exploited, but the organization noted that several sectors in Norway and abroad are impacted.



APIM users have been advised by NSM to treat their secrets and named values as compromised and replace them. They should also verify that their instances have been updated and identify the data accessible through the service.



Security expert Anders Kristiansen warned that credentials lifted from APIM could be used against outside services. The extent of the exposure depends on each credential’s permissions.



Related: N-able Patches Critical Flaw Disclosed After Mail Assure Cyberattack
Related: GL.iNet Routers, KVMs Compromised via Remote Management Service Flaw
Related: AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos

Norway’s National Security Authority (NSM) said on November 20 that the vulnerability had affected several organizations in the country, including energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi, which independently disclosed cybersecurity incidents last week. 



Equinor reported the attack on November 17, one day before Microsoft identified the APIM flaw. The company said it found no evidence that data was stolen or altered. Skagerak Energi disclosed the incident on November 18, saying it did not impact operations or production, and it found no evidence of information compromise.



NSM said an unknown threat actor targeted tokens, secrets and named values stored in APIM. It’s unclear how widely the weakness has been exploited, but the organization noted that several sectors in Norway and abroad are impacted.



APIM users have been advised by NSM to treat their secrets and named values as compromised and replace them. They should also verify that their instances have been updated and identify the data accessible through the service.



Security expert Anders Kristiansen warned that credentials lifted from APIM could be used against outside services. The extent of the exposure depends on each credential’s permissions.



Related: N-able Patches Critical Flaw Disclosed After Mail Assure Cyberattack
Related: GL.iNet Routers, KVMs Compromised via Remote Management Service Flaw
Related: AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos

[49] SecurityWeek - N-able Patches Critical Flaw Discovered After Mail Assure Cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:06:49 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/n-able-patches-critical-flaw-disclosed-after-mail-assure-cyberattack/

[TAM METİN - 2766 kelime]
N-able Patches Critical Flaw Discovered After Mail Assure Cyberattack

The company has restored access to the affected services and continues to monitor the affected environments.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

N-able has patched a critical server-side request forgery (SSRF) vulnerability discovered during its investigation into a cyberattack targeting its Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud services last week.
On November 16, the company disabled access to the portal and API and suspended related services, including logging, mailbox synchronization, the management portal, and quarantine, noting that email delivery was not affected.



Two days later, it confirmed that the services were targeted in a cyberattack and that the hackers hit a web server and likely accessed certain credentials stored there.



The company immediately initiated response and remediation actions, contained the incident, changed credentials, and revoked API tokens and OAuth tokens.



On November 21, N-able restored access to the Mail Assure and SpamExperts dashboard and APIs, while continuing to monitor the environments. The company says it has found no evidence of exploitation beyond the previously identified N-able-hosted server.



On Sunday, N-able revealed that it has patched an SSRF vulnerability identified during its investigation. Tracked as CVE-2026-134624 (CVSS score of 9.3), the flaw exists because the vulnerable service “did not restrict the destination of the LDAP synchronization connection check performed when a relay is configured”.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The flaw could allow an API user with sufficient privileges to make the vulnerable server connect to arbitrary internal hosts and ports, identify those that are reachable, and send crafted data to internal services.



“N-able has remediated the recent security incident affecting our Mail Assure and SpamExperts environments and restored the impacted functionality. Our investigation to date indicates that exploitation was confined to a single N-able hosted server and continued monitoring since remediation has found no further malicious activity,” the company told SecurityWeek.



N-able also noted that its investigation into the incident continues, adding that it would provide further details on the incident in a formal root cause analysis within the next two weeks.



Mail Assure/SpamExperts version 20261121.1943 contains patches for the security defect. 



Related: Industry Reactions to Major Outages and AI Patching Debate: Feedback Friday
Related: Tor Updates DoS Defenses Again as Relay Attacks Continue
Related: phpAfterShell Vulnerability Revives PHP2Shell’s Attack Path

================================================================================

[50] SecurityWeek - phpAfterShell Vulnerability Revives PHP2Shell’s Attack Path
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 13:58:07 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/phpaftershell-vulnerability-revives-php2shells-attack-path/

[TAM METİN - 4108 kelime]
phpAfterShell Vulnerability Revives PHP2Shell’s Attack Path

If a session overwrite operation is interrupted, an attacker who controls the fresh data could trigger data deserialization and RCE.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

PHP last week patched a critical-severity vulnerability residing in the same dangerous state as the PHP2Shell flaws, cybersecurity firm Depthfirst says.
Dubbed phpAfterShell, the security flaw is tracked as CVE-2026-126714 (CVSS score of 9.2), and was resolved on November 19 alongside six other critical issues.



According to Depthfirst, the bug could allow an attacker who can control data written to a session file and interrupt the session write operation to trigger an object deserialization and achieve remote code execution (RCE).



It exists because PHP overwrites the session file from the start without emptying it first. If the operation is interrupted mid-write, or before the subsequent truncation operation removes old trailing data, the file is left with mixed content: fresh session data followed by remnants of the old session. 



“We discovered reliable methods to interrupt the file operation during session upload-progress updates, allowing us to splice the prefix of one valid session with the suffix of another. A subsequent session operation treats the corrupted data as legitimate server-side state, giving attackers control over session values and the ability to trigger object deserialization,” Depthfirst notes in its technical write-up.



An attacker could arrange the new and old data so that the combined file would be interpreted differently from the original sessions, and parts of ordinary strings would become serialized object data. A future session would create the objects and invoke their deserialization.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Session upload progress provides one way for a remote client to influence stored session data through multipart uploads. The same underlying problem can arise when an application stores large user-controlled strings in a session. In threaded PHP builds, a hard memory-limit failure in another thread can terminate the process while the session write is in progress,” PHP notes in its advisory.



phpAfterShell, Depthfirst explains, is related to PHP2Shell, which occurred because, during the parsing of an unauthenticated multipart request, PHP’s upload-progress callback could open and decode a file-backed session before the target script would begin.



Because objects would become active during the callback, PHP added restrictions to which objects could be created in memory, but did not modify the underlying file update behavior.



“Even with the new restrictions in place, the callback still reads the existing Session, adds upload-progress state, serializes the whole thing, and writes it back to the same file,” Depthfirst explains.



Successful exploitation of phpAfterShell requires certain conditions to be met: default session storage to be active, an exploitation route (the easiest is upload progress tracking), the PHP process to stop mid-write, a request to call session_start() on the corrupted file, and a deserialization-to-code-execution path.



Patches for the vulnerability were included in PHP versions 8.6.0, 8.5.13, 8.4.28, 8.3.37, and 8.2.36. There appear to be no reports of phpAfterShell’s in-the-wild exploitation. 



Related: SAP NetWeaver, Cisco Expressway Flaws Exploited in Attacks
Related: WhatsApp Fixes Flaw Uncovered While Disrupting Scam Campaign
Related: Ruby Patches Ruby2Shell Vulnerability That Could Lead to RCE
Related: F5 Patches Critical NGINX2Root Flaws as PoC Exploits Go Public

phpAfterShell, Depthfirst explains, is related to PHP2Shell, which occurred because, during the parsing of an unauthenticated multipart request, PHP’s upload-progress callback could open and decode a file-backed session before the target script would begin.



Because objects would become active during the callback, PHP added restrictions to which objects could be created in memory, but did not modify the underlying file update behavior.



“Even with the new restrictions in place, the callback still reads the existing Session, adds upload-progress state, serializes the whole thing, and writes it back to the same file,” Depthfirst explains.



Successful exploitation of phpAfterShell requires certain conditions to be met: default session storage to be active, an exploitation route (the easiest is upload progress tracking), the PHP process to stop mid-write, a request to call session_start() on the corrupted file, and a deserialization-to-code-execution path.



Patches for the vulnerability were included in PHP versions 8.6.0, 8.5.13, 8.4.28, 8.3.37, and 8.2.36. There appear to be no reports of phpAfterShell’s in-the-wild exploitation. 



Related: SAP NetWeaver, Cisco Expressway Flaws Exploited in Attacks
Related: WhatsApp Fixes Flaw Uncovered While Disrupting Scam Campaign
Related: Ruby Patches Ruby2Shell Vulnerability That Could Lead to RCE
Related: F5 Patches Critical NGINX2Root Flaws as PoC Exploits Go Public

According to Depthfirst, the bug could allow an attacker who can control data written to a session file and interrupt the session write operation to trigger an object deserialization and achieve remote code execution (RCE).



It exists because PHP overwrites the session file from the start without emptying it first. If the operation is interrupted mid-write, or before the subsequent truncation operation removes old trailing data, the file is left with mixed content: fresh session data followed by remnants of the old session. 



“We discovered reliable methods to interrupt the file operation during session upload-progress updates, allowing us to splice the prefix of one valid session with the suffix of another. A subsequent session operation treats the corrupted data as legitimate server-side state, giving attackers control over session values and the ability to trigger object deserialization,” Depthfirst notes in its technical write-up.



An attacker could arrange the new and old data so that the combined file would be interpreted differently from the original sessions, and parts of ordinary strings would become serialized object data. A future session would create the objects and invoke their deserialization.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Session upload progress provides one way for a remote client to influence stored session data through multipart uploads. The same underlying problem can arise when an application stores large user-controlled strings in a session. In threaded PHP builds, a hard memory-limit failure in another thread can terminate the process while the session write is in progress,” PHP notes in its advisory.



phpAfterShell, Depthfirst explains, is related to PHP2Shell, which occurred because, during the parsing of an unauthenticated multipart request, PHP’s upload-progress callback could open and decode a file-backed session before the target script would begin.

“Even with the new restrictions in place, the callback still reads the existing Session, adds upload-progress state, serializes the whole thing, and writes it back to the same file,” Depthfirst explains.



Successful exploitation of phpAfterShell requires certain conditions to be met: default session storage to be active, an exploitation route (the easiest is upload progress tracking), the PHP process to stop mid-write, a request to call session_start() on the corrupted file, and a deserialization-to-code-execution path.



Patches for the vulnerability were included in PHP versions 8.6.0, 8.5.13, 8.4.28, 8.3.37, and 8.2.36. There appear to be no reports of phpAfterShell’s in-the-wild exploitation.

[51] SecurityWeek - GL.iNet Routers, KVMs Compromised via Remote Management Service Flaw
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 13:26:22 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/gl-inet-routers-kvms-compromised-via-remote-management-service-flaw/

[TAM METİN - 2724 kelime]
GL.iNet Routers, KVMs Compromised via Remote Management Service Flaw

Attackers abused a flaw in the GoodCloud remote management service to push malware to devices, and cleaning them requires new firmware.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

GL.iNet has warned customers that a threat actor exploited a vulnerability in its cloud-based remote management service to deliver malware to some routers and KVM devices.
China-based networking hardware maker GL.iNet is well known for its pocket-sized travel routers and Comet KVM devices. The devices can be managed remotely through its GoodCloud platform.



The vendor discovered the remote management service vulnerability leveraged by the attacker on November 20 and has since patched it. 



However, it noted that the fix doesn’t remove the malware from infected devices — customers need to install a separate “security firmware” for that. GL.iNet recommends installing the security firmware on all devices, even those with no signs of infection. 



It remains unclear who is behind the attack and how many devices were compromised. 



It’s also unclear whether the attackers reached the computers connected to compromised KVM devices.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“As a precaution, check for unfamiliar programs, browser extensions or user accounts; unexpected startup items or scheduled tasks; and unusual settings changes or network activity. Run a full scan with up-to-date security software,” the company recommends for KVM devices.



Security and networking firm RemoteToHome Consulting, which manages a large number of GL.iNet routers, has been investigating the incident. Based on its code analysis, the attack worked because the GoodCloud software on the routers ran commands from cloud messages without checking them. GL.iNet has not confirmed this information.



RemoteToHome reported that most of the nearly 200 routers it checked appeared to be impacted, either by this new attack or an older backdoor that appears to date back to September 2025. RemoteToHome says it doesn’t know how that older backdoor was installed.



SecurityWeek has reached out to GL.iNet for additional information and will update this article if it responds. 



Related: AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos
Related: FBI Investigating Apparent Hack of Its Key National Criminal Justice Database, AP Source Says
Related: WhatsApp Fixes Flaw Uncovered While Disrupting Scam Campaign

RemoteToHome reported that most of the nearly 200 routers it checked appeared to be impacted, either by this new attack or an older backdoor that appears to date back to September 2025. RemoteToHome says it doesn’t know how that older backdoor was installed.



SecurityWeek has reached out to GL.iNet for additional information and will update this article if it responds. 



Related: AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos
Related: FBI Investigating Apparent Hack of Its Key National Criminal Justice Database, AP Source Says
Related: WhatsApp Fixes Flaw Uncovered While Disrupting Scam Campaign

[52] SecurityWeek - AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 12:16:20 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/ai-inference-providers-failed-to-detect-hacking-and-prompt-theft-says-dragos/

[TAM METİN - 3768 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos

Dragos uncovered undetected intrusions at three AI inference providers using nearly two million prompts sent to AI services.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Threat actors breached at least three AI inference providers and stole customer prompts, but none detected the intrusions until they were notified, according to cybersecurity firm Dragos.
AI inference providers host AI models and sell access to them through APIs. The hacks were detected after a team led by Dragos chief research officer HD Moore sent out roughly two million prompts to widely used AI services between July and November. 



These prompts contained a unique, trackable bait such as URLs, hostnames, and credentials, including AWS keys. The security firm then set up a monitoring system to see whether anyone used the bait information in the wild, which meant the prompt had been compromised. 



Dragos soon found evidence indicating that someone had stolen information from one of the tested AI inference providers. “Although our prompts were bait, they were processed along with real customer requests, and those were almost certainly copied too,” Moore noted.



The biggest breach involved roughly 200 AWS test keys sent to a provider, which were stolen in late October. 



“Some of the stolen keys came from requests that the provider rejected before a model ever ran – this tells us that the attacker was copying requests at the provider’s front door, and not somewhere deeper in the inference stack,” Moore said.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Dragos saw the attackers checking which stolen keys worked, then trying to use them to obtain valuable data from the associated accounts. 



The attackers have not been identified, but Dragos said one adversary appears to have hacked two of the providers at the same time, using secrets stolen from both from a single rented server.



OpenRouter, an AI gateway that sends customer requests to dozens of inference providers, was indirectly affected. The company said its own systems were not breached, but it removed three unnamed providers in response to the incident. 



OpenRouter also listed security notices published by several providers. It did not say whether these are among the three it removed.



The list includes confidential AI cloud provider Phala, which said an attacker compromised a server running a DeepSeek model served via OpenRouter. It admitted that requests sent between October 21 and November 8 should be considered exposed.



AI cloud platform Together AI revealed that an AI coding agent leaked credentials on GitHub, which were then used to obtain stored prompt samples. Model hosting platform Novita and inference platform Atlas Cloud traced potential exposure to requests that reached the API of model developer MiniMax. 



Related: Amazon: China-Linked Hackers’ AI Agents Exploited Medical Software Zero-Day
Related: Industry Reactions to Major Outages and AI Patching Debate: Feedback Friday
Related: The Governance Gap in Open-Weight AI and What to Do About It

[53] SecurityWeek - BadBox 3.0 Ad Fraud Operation Generating 40 Billion Daily Bid Requests Disrupted
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 11:44:18 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/badbox-3-0-ad-fraud-operation-generating-40-billion-daily-bid-requests-disrupted/

[TAM METİN - 6064 kelime]
BadBox 3.0 Ad Fraud Operation Generating 40 Billion Daily Bid Requests Disrupted

Backdoors on low-cost consumer devices served modules for ad fraud and for turning devices into residential proxies.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Bot and fraud protection firm Human Security says it has disrupted the largest ad fraud botnet by bid volume.
Dubbed BadBox 3.0, it is the third iteration of a large operation targeting low-cost Android devices. The first BadBox botnet ensnared more than 190,000 devices, while BadBox 2.0 was initially found on over 1 million devices, but eventually ensnared more than 10 million.  



“At its height, BadBox 3.0 accounted for roughly 40 billion bid requests a day and more than 4% of all CTV traffic observed by the Human Defense Platform,” Human Security says, noting the bid request volume was three times the volume recorded for the VastFlux ad fraud scheme.



Portions of BadBox 3.0, the cybersecurity firm says, were operated via command-and-control (C&C) domains that were disrupted when Google took down XCPE, a botnet of over 1.3 million Android set-top boxes.



The botnet consists of low-cost, uncertified Android Open Source Project devices infected with backdoors delivering fraud modules designed to render hidden video ads and to enroll the devices into residential proxy networks.



Human Security says its Satori Threat Intelligence and Research Team has uncovered an actively maintained catalog of fraud modules hosted on public repositories, as well as links to Chinese electronics manufacturer TXCZ. Some of the modules were previously seen in BadBox and BadBox 2.0.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Most modules in the catalog are residential proxy modules and the droppers that install them, but the collection also carries new ad fraud modules, and researchers traced both payloads to the same infrastructure and code,” Human Security notes.



Mimicking video ad software development kits (SDKs), the botnet’s ad fraud modules render ads in a hidden window, making the bid requests appear as coming from known, legitimate apps that the threat actors impersonated.



While the device owner does not see the ads, the supply chain sees a premium CTV inventory consisting of well-known applications that likely aren’t even installed on the infected devices, along with residential IP addresses and full video ads. In some cases, the threat actors relied on server-side ad insertion (SSAI) to monetize application spoofing.



“The volume reached roughly 40 billion bid requests a day, and much of it arrived from IP addresses associated with residential proxy networks, which helped it pass as ordinary household viewing,” Human Security notes.



Proxyjacking modules deployed on the same devices enrolled them in eleven residential proxy networks, including a rebranded version of IpMoYu, now named PXYEDGE, the network operated by the threat actors behind BadBox and BadBox 2.0.



Human Security has found several links between BadBox 3.0 and the Chinese company TXCZ: the modules check for TXCZ build properties, a TXCZ domain serves payload download routes and configurations for the hidden ads, and Human linked TXCZ to repositories distributing ad fraud launchers.



In addition to public repositories, the threat actor uses a second code platform and a file server to host payloads. For some payloads, it also uses a free open-source CDN that mirrors public repositories. The operators also use a self-hosted GitHub proxy.



Human Security has not provided an estimated size of BadBox 3.0, but noted that the mirror for one repository served over 367,000 requests over a month, which does not reflect the number of infections.



“These are requests rather than unique devices, and some come from researchers and scanners. It should be made very clear: these numbers are only for one repository, and only of the third backup delivery mechanism. The actual number of BADBOX 3.0 infections can be presumed to be significantly higher,” the cybersecurity firm notes.



Related: WhatsApp Fixes Flaw Uncovered While Disrupting Scam Campaign
Related: Law Enforcement Disrupts Gootloader, CastleRAT, and StealC Infrastructure
Related: Microsoft Disrupts Chinese-Language Phishing Market Behind 1B+ Messages
Related: US Disrupts Chinese State-Sponsored Hacking Tools

Dubbed BadBox 3.0, it is the third iteration of a large operation targeting low-cost Android devices. The first BadBox botnet ensnared more than 190,000 devices, while BadBox 2.0 was initially found on over 1 million devices, but eventually ensnared more than 10 million.  



“At its height, BadBox 3.0 accounted for roughly 40 billion bid requests a day and more than 4% of all CTV traffic observed by the Human Defense Platform,” Human Security says, noting the bid request volume was three times the volume recorded for the VastFlux ad fraud scheme.



Portions of BadBox 3.0, the cybersecurity firm says, were operated via command-and-control (C&C) domains that were disrupted when Google took down XCPE, a botnet of over 1.3 million Android set-top boxes.



The botnet consists of low-cost, uncertified Android Open Source Project devices infected with backdoors delivering fraud modules designed to render hidden video ads and to enroll the devices into residential proxy networks.



Human Security says its Satori Threat Intelligence and Research Team has uncovered an actively maintained catalog of fraud modules hosted on public repositories, as well as links to Chinese electronics manufacturer TXCZ. Some of the modules were previously seen in BadBox and BadBox 2.0.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Most modules in the catalog are residential proxy modules and the droppers that install them, but the collection also carries new ad fraud modules, and researchers traced both payloads to the same infrastructure and code,” Human Security notes.



Mimicking video ad software development kits (SDKs), the botnet’s ad fraud modules render ads in a hidden window, making the bid requests appear as coming from known, legitimate apps that the threat actors impersonated.



While the device owner does not see the ads, the supply chain sees a premium CTV inventory consisting of well-known applications that likely aren’t even installed on the infected devices, along with residential IP addresses and full video ads. In some cases, the threat actors relied on server-side ad insertion (SSAI) to monetize application spoofing.



“The volume reached roughly 40 billion bid requests a day, and much of it arrived from IP addresses associated with residential proxy networks, which helped it pass as ordinary household viewing,” Human Security notes.



Proxyjacking modules deployed on the same devices enrolled them in eleven residential proxy networks, including a rebranded version of IpMoYu, now named PXYEDGE, the network operated by the threat actors behind BadBox and BadBox 2.0.



Human Security has found several links between BadBox 3.0 and the Chinese company TXCZ: the modules check for TXCZ build properties, a TXCZ domain serves payload download routes and configurations for the hidden ads, and Human linked TXCZ to repositories distributing ad fraud launchers.



In addition to public repositories, the threat actor uses a second code platform and a file server to host payloads. For some payloads, it also uses a free open-source CDN that mirrors public repositories. The operators also use a self-hosted GitHub proxy.



Human Security has not provided an estimated size of BadBox 3.0, but noted that the mirror for one repository served over 367,000 requests over a month, which does not reflect the number of infections.



“These are requests rather than unique devices, and some come from researchers and scanners. It should be made very clear: these numbers are only for one repository, and only of the third backup delivery mechanism. The actual number of BADBOX 3.0 infections can be presumed to be significantly higher,” the cybersecurity firm notes.

It impacts Expressway Core (Expressway-C) and Expressway Edge (Expressway-E) devices running vulnerable software versions and configured in a cluster, and was patched on October 21 in Expressway Series release X15.5.20.



On November 20, CISA added the Expressway security defect to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, urging federal agencies to patch it within three days.



CISA’s alert came two days after Russian cybercrime prevention firm F6 warned that threat actors started exploiting the vulnerability in attacks shortly after proof-of-concept (PoC) code was released publicly.

[55] Help Net Security - Claude Ship gives AI a seat on both sides of code review
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 09:02:02 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/anthropic-claude-ship-beta/

[TAM METİN - 743 kelime]
Claude Ship gives AI a seat on both sides of code review

Anthropic launched Claude Ship in beta, a suite of agents that reviews pull requests, fixes issues and approves changes permitted by a team’s policy. Teams decide where it runs and which changes require human approval.

Anthropic said Claude writes most of the code on its Claude Code team. With Claude Ship, the company aims to help teams “ship code as fast as teams write it.”

How Claude Ship works

Claude Ship combines Code Review, which is generally available, with two new agents in beta: PR Steward and Claude Approvals.

Code Review examines pull requests against the codebase from several perspectives, verifies its findings, categorizes them and posts them to the pull request. Teams can choose a Quick, Standard or Deep review level for each repository.

On the Claude Code team, where automated reviews form part of a broader review process, the number of merged pull requests more than doubled from June to September, while the share of reverted pull requests fell by more than 40%, according to Anthropic.

PR Steward addresses failing continuous integration (CI) checks, merge conflicts and feedback from Code Review, other review tools or team members. After each push, it posts a comment explaining what it changed and how to undo those changes. Developers can guide it by adding instructions to comments using @claude or stop it with @claude stop. PR Steward does not approve or merge pull requests.

“PR Steward is helping teams in our pilot spend less time working through code review feedback and more time building. We can tailor it to our teams’ processes, including how it suggests changes and organizes feedback. That flexibility has made it a useful part of the development workflow,” said Alex Devkar, Senior Vice President, Engineering and Analytics at Carvana.

Claude Approvals applies an approval policy defined by the team in a file stored in the repository. The policy can allow Claude to approve routine changes and require human sign-off in specific scenarios. Teams can base approval decisions on Code Review findings or their own classifiers written in natural language.

Anthropic recommends that most customers initially run Claude Approvals in observe-only mode before configuring branch protection rules to require its approval.

Options for developers and admins

Developers can run the new /ship command in Claude Code once a change is ready. Claude pushes the change, opens a pull request, reviews it and addresses feedback and failing checks. Merge permissions depend on the developer’s permissions and the repository’s branch rules. The command is rolling out in Claude Code and the desktop app.

Admins can enable Code Review and PR Steward for individual repositories, allowing the agents to work on pull requests automatically without developers having to invoke the /ship command.

Admins can configure the review level, review frequency and monthly spending limits for Code Review and PR Steward. Claude Approvals becomes active when a connected repository has a policy file on its default branch, and admins can disable it. Claude Ship works with existing branch protection rules and required checks.

Anthropic uses Claude Ship on many of its own pull requests. In one recent example, a customer was confused by Analytics API documentation that implied an Admin key could retrieve Pro and Team usage data.

A product manager on the Claude Code team asked Claude Tag whether a fix was already underway, and Claude Tag opened a pull request to correct the documentation. Code Review found no blocking issues, a documentation reviewer signed off, the change passed Claude Approvals, and the pull request was merged that evening.

Availability and pricing

Claude Ship is available to customers on Claude Team and Enterprise plans for repositories hosted on GitHub. Organizations with Zero Data Retention, the HIPAA configuration or customer-managed encryption keys cannot use it.

Claude Ship is billed based on token usage. Costs depend on the number and size of pull requests, the review level, how often reviews run and the amount of work PR Steward performs.

Claude Approvals has no base charge. Classifiers defined in a policy are billed based on the tokens they consume, and policies that rely on Claude’s review trigger a billable Code Review, even in observe-only mode. Running the /ship command counts as regular Claude Code usage.

Eligible Claude Enterprise organizations can claim at least $10,000 in Claude Ship credits, which are valid for 30 days from the date of claiming. The claim deadline is January 15, 2027.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/anthropic-claude-ship-beta/, helpnetsecurity.com, 24.11.2026)

[56] Help Net Security - Google Cloud CISO talks agentic AI security and the future of the SOC
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 07:00:16 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/chris-betz-google-agentic-ai-security/

[TAM METİN - 818 kelime]
Google Cloud CISO talks agentic AI security and the future of the SOC

In this interview with Help Net Security, Chris Betz, CISO of Google Cloud, answers five questions about security as AI agents spread. He says the old belief that human-led workflows would stay the norm has aged badly, since attackers use automation to move through networks in minutes.

Betz explains how agents could change the junior analyst role, why espionage groups can be hard to spot, which controls companies should drop, and what boards should ask their CISO in 2027. His main concern is tracing, auditing and reversing every agent action.

For years, the industry assumed manual, human-centric workflows would stay the baseline for enterprise defense. Defenders stitched logs across eight consoles, approved every defensive action, and made developers file tickets and wait weeks for a security review.

That assumption aged poorly, and it’s failing in real time. Autonomous threats operate at automated speed, probing networks and moving laterally in minutes. Adversaries now compress six months of reconnaissance into hours and exploit vulnerabilities before vendors even know they exist. Against agentic hacking, human reaction time is no longer a viable baseline.

Google is both a frontier lab and the operator of the world’s most comprehensive threat intelligence network. That gives us a unique vantage point: we see how LLMs are engineered, and how adversaries target them in the wild. We predicted the rise in autonomous threats and have been actively building autonomous solutions like AI Threat Defense to counter them.

For decades, SOCs have suffered from crippling alert fatigue. Legacy security tools have traditionally handed human analysts half-finished investigations, leaving junior teams buried under a mountain of raw tickets.

The use of autonomous defensive agents to handle data enrichment, correlation, and triage automatically, does not replace the analyst. Instead, it changes the starting line. The “alert” of the future is no longer a noisy, uncontextualized event that a human has to spend hours researching; it becomes the final, verified conclusion of an automated investigation. As our security experts shared in the blog, 2027 is the year analysts stop chasing alerts and start shaping outcomes.

Instead of working raw tickets at a keyboard, junior analysts move up the stack to set strategic doctrine, define risk appetite, and verify automated actions built for rapid explainability, where any defensive decision can be understood by a human in 60 seconds and reversed in 30. Future senior responders will build their craft by establishing graduated autonomy between specialized agents and a central coordinator, and by running continuous, proactive adversarial red-teaming to test how agentic systems behave under pressure before deployment.

Not every adversary embraces automated speed the same way. Cybercriminals go all-in on autonomous velocity. State-sponsored espionage actors often hold back, avoiding loud, hyper-fast scripts that trigger alarms and blow their long-term stealth.

That caution does not automatically make espionage actors easier to detect. Because they optimize for persistence and intelligence gathering, not noisy extortion or disruption, they deliberately operate below traditional behavioral alert thresholds.

Still, defenders hold a decisive architectural advantage. Attackers rely on guesswork and fragmented external visibility. Defenders own the ground truth: identity privilege graphs, internal network architecture, code lineage, and real-time runtime activity. When espionage actors move slowly to evade automation alerts, they leave subtle footprints across these internal surfaces that defenders can correlate and catch by watching point-in-time transaction authorizations, open-source libraries pulled into codebases by coding assistants, and uncoordinated standalone agents spun up across departments

Organizations should stop investing in flat trust architectures, shared human service accounts, and blanket administrator tokens, along with manual checkpoints where developers file a ticket and wait weeks for a manual security review.

It’s more about investing in capabilities to move beyond zero trust. Traditional zero trust architecture operates too broadly at the network and machine layers, leaving autonomous workflows vulnerable when agents rely on shared service accounts or blanket tokens. Moving Beyond Zero extends verification down to the individual transactions and point-in-time authorizations. This gives each agent a verifiable, least-privileged identity whose actions can be dynamically allowed or blocked based on real-time conditions.

The risks in 2027 won’t be just server outages or stolen databases. As the agentic operations world takes off, security must evolve to protect the balance sheet through decision integrity.

Board members should be asking if every agent action can be traced, audited and reversed. They should examine whether the company has built centralized guardrails with tamper-proof decision traces. These logs need to record the inputs entered into the system, how the model reasoned through its decisions, which tools the model called, what parameters it passed, and why policy allowed it, so every consequential action an autonomous agent takes can be governed before agent sprawl outpaces human oversight. Collecting this data will help ensure that teams can reconstruct, validate, and audit every automated decision before scaling.

Download: The IT and security field guide to AI adoption
