[57] Help Net Security - Ransomware gangs keep multiplying, even as fewer victims pay
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 06:30:26 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/check-point-ransomware-attacks-q3-2026-report/

[TAM METİN - 803 kelime]
Ransomware gangs keep multiplying, even as fewer victims pay

Ransomware groups listed 2,830 victims on data leak sites in the third quarter of 2026, the highest quarterly total on record, according to Check Point Research’s State of Ransomware Q3 2026 report. July accounted for 964 victims, August for 1,042 and September for 824.

“The scale of activity was accompanied by a significant expansion in the number of active groups. Q3 combined sustained activity from established operators with a wider pool of ransomware brands claiming victims, helping explain how overall volumes continued to rise while activity became less concentrated among the largest groups,” Sergey Shykevich, Director, Threat Intelligence at Check Point Software, explained.

A total of 117 ransomware brands claimed at least one victim, up from 91 in the second quarter. Of these, 51 had not posted victims in Q2, while 42 made their first observed victim claims in Q3. The ten most active groups accounted for 49% of victims, down from 71% in the first quarter. For the first time, groups outside the top ten accounted for the majority of published victims.

Leaders and decliners

Qilin led the rankings for another quarter, followed closely by The Gentlemen. The two traded the monthly lead, with Qilin taking the top spot in July and August before The Gentlemen moved ahead in September. Together, they accounted for a quarter of all published victims. INC Ransom, Akira and DragonForce remained in the top ten throughout 2026, although DragonForce’s victim count declined over the quarter.

Top 10 ransomware groups by share of total victims, Q3 2026 (Source: Check Point)

LockBit dropped out of the top ten after posting fewer victims in each successive quarter of 2026. Operation Cronos disrupted the group in 2024, and researchers suggest that affiliates who moved to rival programs may have established new partnerships and declined to return after LockBit relaunched.

Orova appeared in July, listed 35 victims in August and fell to just two in September. DeadLock, which hosts its leak site on Polygon smart contracts, published most of its victims in a single batch in July, apparently involving previously unlisted, older cases instead of new attacks.

In September, ShinyHunters reportedly breached and defaced Cl0p’s leak site, claiming to have obtained source code, internal logs and private onion-service keys. Its message demanded payment and a public apology from Cl0p, warning that its demands would increase every 24 hours. Cl0p reportedly removed references to the apology request and told visitors that the ShinyHunters contact details did not work.

Dutch authorities arrested a 24-year-old suspect linked to ShinyHunters activity in mid-September. A second alleged member was reportedly detained in Jordan later that month and was said to be cooperating with US authorities. Researchers also raised questions about whether rival actors had reused the ShinyHunters identity in connection with the Cl0p breach.

Separately, Spanish and German authorities, working with Europol, dismantled the KillSec operation in an action dubbed Operation KillSwitch. Three people were arrested, including a suspected 16-year-old operator in Alicante, Spain.

Ransomware payment trends moved in different directions. Chainalysis estimates that 28% of victims paid in 2025, the lowest rate recorded, while Coveware reports that fewer than one in four ransomware incidents now results in payment. The average payment fell 45% quarter over quarter to just over $1 million, while the median rose 167% to $400,000.

Several large organizations publicly refused ransom demands. Stadler Rail rejected a demand for 10 million Swiss francs ($12.3 million) from Everest after attackers obtained technical information through a compromised supplier-connected platform. The Berlin state government refused a 30 BTC demand from Rhysida, which subsequently published about 5.8 TB of stolen data.

Manchester Airports Group reportedly declined to pay after a breach attributed to FulcrumSec exposed data associated with approximately 8.7 million customers. Coca-Cola subsidiary Fairlife disclosed a ransomware attack by Anubis in July, with a reported $15 million demand. Although the company did not publicly reject the demand, the stolen data later appeared online, suggesting that the ransom was not paid.

Geographic and sector trends

The United States accounted for 42% of victims, followed by Germany, Canada and Italy. India and Argentina entered the top ten countries, replacing Australia and Thailand. Qilin’s growth coincided with Argentina’s rise in the rankings, while a burst of DeadLock disclosures in Southern and Central Europe in July contributed to Italy’s total.

Business services was the largest sector, accounting for 34% of victims, followed by industrial manufacturing and consumer goods and services at 13% each. Healthcare and government together accounted for more than 10% of published victim claims.

Most major ransomware groups claimed victims across a wide range of sectors, while Silent Ransom Group stood out for targeting only the business services sector, particularly professional and legal services. Check Point Research attributed most victim selection to accessibility and opportunity instead of deliberate sector targeting.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/check-point-ransomware-attacks-q3-2026-report/, helpnetsecurity.com, 24.11.2026)

[58] Help Net Security - AWS European Sovereign Cloud: Architecture, assurance, and compliance
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 06:15:37 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/aws-european-sovereign-cloud-details/

[TAM METİN - 4164 kelime]
AWS European Sovereign Cloud: Architecture, assurance, and compliance

An overview of AWS European Sovereign Cloud: architecture, sovereignty assurances, compliance, available services, and use cases for organizations seeking innovation with full data control.

Hi, I’m Mustafa Isik, Chief Technologist, Sovereign Cloud, Amazon Web Services. I lead a team of technology and compliance specialists focused on digital sovereignty, working closely with customers, partners, regulators, but also national and international cybersecurity authorities in Europe and globally. In this presentation, I will give you a comprehensive overview of the AWS European Sovereign Cloud, what it is, how it’s built, why it matters, and what’s available to you today. I’ll walk you through the sovereignty assurances we’ve designed into the architecture, the compliance milestones we’ve already achieved, the services and capabilities available, and real examples of organizations building on it today.

Whether you’re evaluating sovereign cloud options, navigating regulatory requirements like DORA or NIS2 or simply exploring how to innovate without compromising control, this session is for you.

There is no single definition of digital sovereignty. It means different things to different organizations, depending on their industry, their regulatory environment, and their risk posture. But from our conversations with thousands of customers, partners and regulators, we’ve identified consistent themes. At AWS, we organize these into two core dimensions.

First, data sovereignty.
This is about data residency: where your data physically resides? And it’s about operator access restrictions: who can access your data and under what conditions?

Second, operational sovereignty.
This is about the resilience and survivability of your infrastructure, and it’s about independence and transparency, understanding how your cloud operates and ensuring that it can function when exposed to different types of disruptive scenarios. These are the principles that guide everything we build and operate regarding digital sovereignty. They’re not abstract; they’re engineering requirements that drive real design decisions.

The AWS Cloud is sovereign-by-design. Let me explain what that means. We offer a comprehensive set of cutting-edge technical measures, operational controls, and contractual protections that give customers control over where their data is located, who can access it, and how it’s used.

Security is and has always been our top priority at AWS. It’s fundamental to digital sovereignty.

You cannot have sovereignty without security, and we’ve built both into the foundation of our cloud from day one.

We have the most proven operational experience of any cloud provider. Our customers benefit from a cloud and network architecture designed to meet the requirements of the most security sensitive organizations on the planet. Millions of customers around the world rely on AWS not simply for innovative technology, but for a secure and trustworthy foundation on which that technology operates.

To provide greater transparency and assurance, we continue to pursue and obtain third-party attestations, accreditations, and certifications. Today, AWS supports more than 140 security standards and compliance certifications globally. But we know that for some customers, particularly in the European public sector and highly regulated industries, and for some of their workloads, they need something complementary that caters to different interpretations of specific requirements in the domains of data privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and resilience and transparency. And that’s exactly why we built what I’m about to show you.

AWS offers a wide range of capabilities from distributed cloud to edge computing to meet requirements with capabilities wherever customers need them. Customers take advantage of the same services, same architecture, same APIs, and same tools across all environments. That consistency is critical. It accelerates innovation, increases operational efficiency, reduces costs, and helps you meet industry and government specific compliance standards without rearchitecting. This includes AWS regions, local zones, outposts, dedicated local zones, AI factories, and hybrid cloud and far edge solutions like EKS anywhere and IoT. And it includes the AWS European Sovereign Cloud.

We announced the general availability of the AWS European Sovereign Cloud on January 15, 2026. The AWS European Sovereign Cloud Region is located in the state of Brandenburg, Germany, supported by €7.8 billion investment in infrastructure, talent and skills development.

Everything needed to operate the AWS European Sovereign Cloud is located in the European Union. The talent, the technology, the infrastructure, the leadership.

What you find in the AWS European Sovereign Cloud is the experience you know from our other AWS Regions. The resilience of multiple availability zones, the security of the AWS Nitro system, encryption capabilities, and the full depth of AWS services.

The AWS European Sovereign Cloud also has separate billing and usage metering systems operating entirely within the region. But there is more. The AWS European Sovereign Cloud is built on four fundamental assurances.

The first assurance is enhanced data residency. Customers have always had control over the location of their data in AWS. Your customer content, the data you upload to S3 buckets, stored in RDS databases, and so on, stays within the AWS Region you select. That’s true in every AWS Region globally.

The AWS European Sovereign Cloud goes further. It allows customers to keep all the metadata they create within the EU. Elements like roles, permissions, resource tags, and configurations you use to operate on AWS. This is a meaningful distinction for organizations with broad EU data residency requirements. It’s important to note that sovereignty is a continuum.

Customers can choose to deploy their data to any of our 8 existing AWS European regions to keep data securely in Europe, or they can build on our 6 regions located within EU member states to meet specific EU data residency needs.

The AWS European Sovereign Cloud provides the broadest level of residency assurance for those who require it.

The second assurance is operational autonomy.

The AWS European Sovereign Cloud has no critical dependencies, physical or logical, on any AWS infrastructure outside of the EU. It is more than a region; it is an independent cloud.

Daily operations, technical support, and customer services are provided exclusively by EU residents who are located in EU member states and subject to EU law. Data centers are exclusively managed by EU residents located in the EU.

We are also gradually transitioning the AWS European Sovereign Cloud to be operated exclusively by EU citizens located in the EU. During this transition period we will continue to work with a blended team of EU residents and EU citizens located in the EU.

Its unique design allows it to continue operations indefinitely, even in the event of disruption of communications with the rest of the world.
To support continuity, even in extreme circumstances, authorized AWS employees of the European Sovereign Cloud have independent access to a replica of the source code necessary to maintain services.

That is a level of operational independence that is unique to the AWS European Sovereign Cloud.

The third assurance is European governance.

AWS has established a dedicated governance structure in Europe. We incorporated a new parent company, AWS European Sovereign Cloud GMBH, in Germany, along with 3 local subsidiaries, one for infrastructure, one for research and development, and one serving as a trust and certificate provider.

The AWS European Sovereign Cloud is led by EU citizens who are required to comply with European laws and act in the best interest of the region.

Two EU member state citizens are proud to serve as managing directors. Stéphane Israël, whose appointment was announced in September 2025, and Stefan Hoechbauer, who was appointed as co-managing Director in January 2026, bringing executive expertise in compliance and regulation. Hoechbauer also holds the position of VP of AWS Global Sales Germany and Europe Central.

We’ve also established an independent advisory board composed of five members, all EU citizens and residents, of which two are independent and not affiliated with Amazon.

This board serves as a source of expertise and provides accountability on all aspects related to the sovereignty of the European sovereign cloud. Zero operational control resides outside of the EU. That’s by design.

The fourth assurance is our sovereign reference framework, the AWS European Sovereign Cloud: Sovereign Reference Framework (ESC-SRF). The ESC-SRF is available today in AWS Artifact. It lists sovereignty criteria across multiple domains. Governance independence, operational control, data residency, and technical isolation.

The ESC-SRF was developed by working backwards from our customers’ sovereign use cases.

We incorporated direct customer feedback, EU regulatory requirements across multiple member states, industry leading framework guidance, AWS’s own contractual commitments, and input from our implementation partners.

It’s industry and sector agnostic, written to address fundamental sovereignty needs at the foundational layer of cloud.

The framework builds on core AWS security capabilities, encryption, key management, AWS Nitro-based isolation, and internationally recognized compliance certifications.

On top of that, it adds sovereignty specific measures, independent EU corporate structures.

Dedicated EU trust and certificate services, operations by EU resident personnel, strict residency for customer data and customer created metadata, complete separation from all other AWS regions and incident response operated from within the EU.

The ESC-SRF serves a dual purpose. First, it’s an assurance model, providing end to end visibility from each Sovereignty criterion through to its implementation in a manner that can be independently validated by a third-party auditor. Customers can use this with internal auditors, external assessors, supervisory authorities, and regulators. It reduces the need for ad hoc evidence requests.

Second, it’s a design reference. Customers and partners can leverage the ESC-SRF as a foundation upon which they build their own complementary sovereignty criteria and controls. You can refer to the framework when shaping your own sovereignty architecture, selecting configurations and defining internal controls. Because it’s sector agnostic, you apply only the criteria relevant to your specific use case. Each criterion is implemented through sovereign controls that are independently validated by a third-party auditor. This provides clear, enforceable sovereignty assurances, not marketing promises.

Now, let me share something that our customers are building on today. In March 2026, just two months after general availability, we announced our first major compliance milestone, SOC 2 and C5 Type 1 attestation reports, along with seven key ISO certifications for the AWS European Sovereign Cloud.

The attestation reports already cover 69 services operating within the AWS European Sovereign cloud. That’s 69 services with independently validated security controls. The SOC 2 report addresses three critical AICPA trust services criteria, security, availability, and confidentiality. It includes internal controls mapped directly to the ESC-SRF.

Independent auditors validated the design and implementation of our controls, addressing EU corporate structures, EU resident personnel operations, strict residency requirements, and complete separation from all other AWS regions.

The C5 attestation, which is the German government backed Cloud Computing Compliance Criteria Catalog introduced by BSI, is one of the most comprehensive cloud security standards in Europe, if not globally.

Our C5 Type 1 report confirms we meet both the basic criteria and the additional criteria. The basic criteria cover fundamental security requirements across more than a dozen domains. The additional criteria address enhanced requirements for handling sensitive data and critical applications, making this attestation particularly valuable for customers with the most stringent sovereignty requirements.

And the seven ISO certifications, ISO 27001 for information security management, ISO 27017 for cloud specific security controls, ISO 27018 for personal data protection and cloud environments, ISO 27701 for privacy information management, ISO 22301 for business continuity and organizational resilience, and ISO 20000-1 for IT service management. And finally, ISO 9001 for quality management. These certifications collectively demonstrate comprehensive operational excellence.

They confirm that the AWS European Sovereign Cloud has been integrated into Frameworks for managing security, privacy, continuity, service delivery, and quality, meeting the highest standards through systematic risk management and continuous improvement, based on certifications and criteria that are well established and highly relevant today.

By securing these foundational certifications early in our implementation, we’re demonstrating that security, transparency, and regulatory alignment have been part of the DNA of the AWS European Sovereign Cloud from day one. And this is just the beginning of our certification portfolio.

[59] Help Net Security - Linux patches can take weeks, and OpenAI and Cisco built Shirudo to help in the meantime
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 06:00:50 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/cisco-openai-shirudo/

[TAM METİN - 398 kelime]
Linux patches can take weeks, and OpenAI and Cisco built Shirudo to help in the meantime

OpenAI and Cisco released Shirudo, an open-source tool that puts temporary protections on running Linux systems while their owners work on a patch. Shirudo is Japanese for “shield,” which is also what each protection is called: a small program that blocks the specific behavior a vulnerability depends on, with no restart needed.

Testing and deploying a patch can still take days or weeks, and the vulnerable service stays exposed in the meantime. Blocking a risky Linux operation outright can take that service offline, because an attack and a normal request may use the same operation. Shirudo lets a team test whether a shield stops the attack while legitimate traffic still gets through. An operator approves the shield before it goes live.

Shirudo is built on eBPF, a Linux feature that runs small programs at set points inside the operating system. Shields are cryptographically signed and checked before they load. A Trusted Platform Module, a security chip on the machine, keeps a record of protections being loaded or removed, so operators can check later what was running.

The Elasticsearch test

In a controlled test, the team had OpenAI’s Codex generate a shield that added an input check to Java’s Base64 decoder, then attached it to a running Elasticsearch process. The shield rejected a Java4Shell exploit attempt, and Elasticsearch kept running. The people who built the tool also ran that test and set its conditions.

“We’re giving defenders better models and tools to find and fix vulnerabilities faster, but finding a bug today doesn’t mean you can patch every system tomorrow. We are partnering closely with Cisco on Shirudo to help teams protect the systems they need to keep running while they build and roll out a fix. So many teams rely on the same software that it makes sense to build these tools in the open. Now when one team finds a better way to protect a system, others don’t have to start from scratch,” said Greg Brockman, President and Co-founder of OpenAI.

Shirudo is available for free on GitHub. To run it in production, you need a Linux kernel with Shirudo’s kernel patches and a TPM 2.0 chip.

20 open-source cybersecurity tools to keep your team ready for anything

GitHub Agentic Autofix and the question of who owns a bad AI patch

[60] Help Net Security - How IT can delegate password management without losing control
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 05:30:07 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/passwork-enterprise-password-management/

[TAM METİN - 1346 kelime]
How IT can delegate password management without losing control

IT is expected to secure company accounts, but it doesn’t always control how they’re created or shared. Departments manage their own tools and grant access to colleagues or contractors, often without involving IT. This leaves IT responsible for accounts it may not even know about.

Consider a hypothetical marketing account purchased by a department, used by several colleagues and an external agency, and tied to an employee’s recovery email. When a personnel change occurs, the organization may find itself in a precarious situation where access is unclear, leaving critical business operations at risk.

Managing passwords across departments requires shared responsibility. Business owners identify the accounts their teams rely on, approve access, and review it regularly. IT and security teams provide controls and oversight, while HR and managers initiate and verify access changes.

Map the accounts and name their owners

To establish accountability, organizations should start by identifying work accounts and assigning responsibility for each. Department leads should work with procurement and finance to identify purchased applications and vendor relationships. IT can then help verify how these accounts are accessed and who has access to them.

The inventory should identify each service, its business owner, and a backup administrator. It should also record who has access, whether the account is named or shared, and how access can be recovered. Include any access dependencies and the dates of the last and next reviews.

Keep passwords and recovery codes out of the inventory.

For accounts such as payroll portals or marketing tools, the business owner and technical administrator may be different people. The business owner decides who needs access, while the technical administrator manages it. This distinction matters when access reviews are overdue or ownership changes.

Define what each team can decide

Business owners decide who needs access to their team’s accounts. They approve requests and review permissions regularly. IT sets security policies and provides the tools to enforce them.
Day-to-day access management can be delegated to designated employees within departments.

HR and managers notify the responsible teams when employees join, change roles, or leave. Procurement helps identify new applications and vendor relationships.

Make secure access part of daily work

Security rules work best when they fit into how teams already operate. Employees should have a clear way to request access, share credentials, and hand over accounts when roles change.

Start with individual accounts and single sign-on (SSO) wherever possible. Use phishing-resistant multi-factor authentication (MFA) where appropriate. For applications that don’t support SSO or require shared logins, provide an approved password vault instead of leaving teams to exchange credentials through email or chat.

Make access requests part of the regular workflow. Business owners approve requests, while IT grants access through the approved system. When employees change roles or leave, managers notify IT so permissions can be updated or revoked.

Give contractors access with a defined end date and an internal sponsor responsible for reviewing it. Assign an owner and backup for shared accounts, including their recovery procedures. Test emergency access before it’s needed.

Use vault activity logs to review credential access, but remember that they may not show what users do inside the applications themselves.

How Passwork helps IT delegate password management

Passwork, a password and secrets manager for businesses, lets IT set security rules while departments handle everyday access decisions.

IT can create separate vaults for departments or projects and configure vault types with designated administrators. For example, a sales manager can manage permissions within the team’s vaults, while corporate administrators retain oversight.

Roles and access levels help limit what employees can do. Teams can share individual passwords without exposing entire vaults, including through one-time or time-limited links for external recipients.

AD/LDAP synchronization helps keep Passwork groups aligned with directory groups as employees join, move, or leave. SSO centralizes sign-in, while activity logs and the security dashboard give IT visibility into password management.

This way, departments can manage access to their own accounts within the boundaries set by IT.

Close the gap when someone changes roles or leaves

When an employee changes roles or leaves, HR, IT, and the business owner need to coordinate access changes. Each step should have an owner, and the results must be verified.

HR or the manager records when access should change and notifies IT and the business owner.

IT disables the employee’s identity and vault access, removes group memberships, and checks application-specific accounts. Active sessions and tokens should also be revoked where possible, since disabling an identity may leave existing sessions active.

The business owner identifies shared credentials the employee could access. Once their access is blocked, passwords should be changed in the affected services and vault records updated.

The responsible account owners transfer account ownership and recovery methods. They also review vendor and agency access, assign owners to service accounts or integrations where needed, and replace affected tokens without disrupting active services.

IT and the business owner verify that access changes have taken effect and document any exceptions.

CIS Safeguards 6.1 and 6.2 call for documented processes to grant and revoke access. These processes still need to be implemented and verified across the systems involved.

Test the process with one team before scaling

Start with one department and a small set of critical accounts. Choose a team willing to participate and find out how employees currently request, retrieve, and share access. Pay particular attention to what happens when someone is absent or leaves.

Assign a business owner and an IT contact for each account. Build the inventory, configure permissions, and move approved work credentials into the vault. Keep personal passwords out of the corporate system.

Test the process with real scenarios. Can a new employee get the access they need? Can a manager approve a request? Can IT revoke access without disrupting the team’s work? Check account recovery as well, including what happens when the primary owner is unavailable.

Change passwords after migration. Credentials that lived in chats and browsers should be considered exposed. Once the migration is verified, change passwords for the imported accounts, starting with business-critical ones and those shared with contractors or former employees. Update the vault records as you go.

Treat temporary exports as sensitive data and restrict access to them. Once migration is verified and retention requirements allow, retire old sharing methods. Show employees how to use the new process and give them a support contact. Document exceptions for applications that don’t support SSO or individual accounts.

Expand to other departments once access and recovery issues are resolved. Use the pilot results to plan the next rollout rather than committing to a fixed deployment timeline.

Measure ownership and access, not software installs

Installing a password manager doesn’t mean every account is under control. Track whether teams have assigned owners, review access on time, and complete offboarding procedures.

Start with four metrics:

Owner coverage: Percentage of accounts with a named business owner and backup.

Review completion: Percentage of access reviews completed on time.

Leaver closure: Percentage of employee departures with confirmed access removal, downstream checks, and required shared-password changes.

Unowned or overdue items: Number of accounts without an owner or with overdue reviews, broken down by department and how long they’ve been outstanding.

Define who tracks each metric, where the data comes from, and how often it’s reviewed. Record exceptions and report both the number of completed cases and the total number in scope. An incomplete account inventory can make coverage appear better than it is.

Start with one team and a few business-critical accounts. Help the team identify account owners, agree on access rules, and establish a process for reviewing and revoking access. Test the process together before expanding it to other departments.

The goal is to give business teams responsibility for the accounts they use, while IT provides the tools and controls to keep access secure.

If you’re planning to extend secure password management across your organization, take advantage of Passwork’s Black Friday offer. Get up to 67% off and choose a gift: a free upgrade to 5,000 user seats or 25% off your first renewal.

[61] Help Net Security - Stalwart: Open-source Microsoft Exchange replacement
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 05:00:26 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/stalwart-microsoft-exchange-replacement/

[TAM METİN - 526 kelime]
Stalwart: Open-source Microsoft Exchange replacement

Stalwart, an open source mail server, shipped version 1.2.0 with support for the protocols Exchange clients speak: MAPI over HTTP, Exchange Web Services (EWS), Exchange ActiveSync and Autodiscover. Outlook, Apple Mail and the built-in apps on phones connect to it as they would to an Exchange server, with mail, calendars, contacts, tasks and notes in one account. The feature is part of the free Community edition, licensed under the AGPL.

If you still run Exchange on your own servers, the calendar is against you. Exchange 2016 and 2019 reached end of support on October 14, 2025, and the paid Extended Security Update program reportedly ended in October 2026, so those servers get no fixes. Subscription Edition, the one supported on-premises version left, gets none of the cloud features, and Microsoft’s new Outlook for Windows does not support on-premises accounts. Administrators are left choosing between a server that will not grow and Microsoft’s cloud, where mail sits within reach of US law, including the CLOUD Act.

Outlook stays, the server moves

“The obstacle to leaving Exchange has rarely been the server. It has been the client,” says Stalwart’s maintainer. Moving to a standard IMAP and CalDAV server meant asking every user to switch mail programs, or to run Outlook in a reduced IMAP mode where calendars and contacts stop syncing. With 1.2.0, an administrator migrates the mailboxes, points DNS at Stalwart, and staff open the same Outlook on Monday that they closed on Friday.

Classic Outlook 2016 and later connects over MAPI and EWS. Outlook for Mac and Apple Mail use EWS, and iPhones and Android apps such as Gmail and Samsung Email use ActiveSync. Every client lands in the same account, and rules built in Outlook run on the server even after Outlook closes.

Stalwart has no public folders, archive mailboxes, mobile device management, RPC over HTTP for Outlook 2010 and earlier, or NTLM and Kerberos sign-in. The new Outlook is the larger gap, and it works with no self-hosted server, Exchange included. Given an IMAP account, it sends the server name, user name and password to Microsoft, whose cloud fetches the mail. A company that self-hosts to keep its mail away from Microsoft would end up handing Microsoft the password. Stalwart tells users to leave the “Try the new Outlook” toggle off.

What it costs to carry

The Exchange code adds about 200,000 lines to Stalwart. It lives in separate crates behind one Cargo feature, ships disabled in official builds, and writes nothing to an account until an Exchange client connects. Vandelay, Stalwart’s import tool, pulls mailboxes from on-premises Exchange over EWS and from Microsoft 365 through Microsoft Graph.

The project treats all of this as a bridge with an expiry date. EWS is due to vanish from Exchange Online in April 2027, and classic Outlook is reportedly supported until 2029. Stalwart expects users to switch to clients built on open standards such as JMAP during that window.

Stalwart is available for free download on GitHub.

20 open-source cybersecurity tools to keep your team ready for anything

GitHub Agentic Autofix and the question of who owns a bad AI patch

[63] Help Net Security - GL.iNet cloud compromise hits routers and KVMs, older backdoors also found
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 15:05:50 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/23/gl-inet-cloud-compromise-malware-backdoors/

[TAM METİN - 518 kelime]
GL.iNet cloud compromise hits routers and KVMs, older backdoors also found

Attackers compromised GoodCloud, GL.iNet’s cloud remote management service, and used it to install malware on routers and KVM (keyboard, video and mouse) devices connected to it.

The Chinese company, best known for its small travel routers, is still investigating, and it’s not yet known how many devices have been affected.

The incident has also sparked additional research by RemoteToHome Consulting (RTH), a GL.iNet Services Partner, and a number of users, some of whom reported finding backdoors that have been installed on their GL.iNet routers back in 2025.

On November 20, 2026, GL.iNet discovered that GoodCloud was compromised via a still unspecified “cloud vulnerability”, which has since been fixed.

The cloud-side fix does not remove the malware from the infected devices, though.

GL.iNet advised affected users to install the latest stable security firmware, which removes that specific malware and doesn’t require them to perform a factory reset of the device.

The company also instructed users to look for a specific indicator of compromise – a process running as crond -i /tmp/.log – but more recently added that additional indicators of compromise have been identified that their official quick checks would not surface.

They also linked to a community-created script that can be used in addition to their checks, to find additional malware infections and perform a clean-up.

However, the company noted that “the two approaches have different detection coverage, and neither can guarantee that a device is free of compromise.”

Customers who find that their KVM has been infected should also check whether the connected computer has been compromised, by searching for unfamiliar programs, browser extensions, user accounts; unexpected startup items and scheduled tasks; and unusual settings changes or network activity.

GL.iNet has notified affected customers by email and will follow up with further updates. “We are still determining the scope of the incident, including whether computers connected to affected KVM devices were impacted,” the company said.

Partner finds older backdoors, possible entry point

RemoteToHome Consulting mounted its own investigation and checked routers they manage.

“Of 199 routers we have reached so far, 166 show the attack of 20 November, an older backdoor, or both: a root remote shell on 24 ([models] GL-AXT1800 and GL-MT6000), an older backdoor installed and running on 49 ([models] GL-MT2500, GL-MT3000, GL-MT6000, GL-AX1800 and GL-AXT1800), and on 93 more a command from the attacker that ran as root,” the company shared. (RTH could not determine how the older backdoors ended up on the devices.)

The company published a technical analysis of a command injection vulnerability they found in GL.iNet’s GoodCloud client they believe was used by the attackers (GL.iNet has yet to confirm this).

Affected users should check RTH’s list of indicators of compromise and search for those, as well.

“The attacker had a root shell, so every key, password and profile stored on the device could be copied. Cleaning or reinstalling the router does not take those back. You have to remove that access at the other end,” the company advised, and laid out  a comprehensive to-do list after compromise is confirmed.

[64] The Record - ‘Grand Theft Auto VI’ hacker sentenced to seven years, released the same day
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/gta-vi-hacker-arion-kurtaj-released-on-license

[TAM METİN - 402 kelime]
‘Grand Theft Auto VI’ hacker sentenced to seven years, released the same day

A British cybercriminal who hacked Rockstar Games and leaked footage of Grand Theft Auto VI was sentenced Monday to seven years in prison, and then released on license because of time served.

Arion Kurtaj, now 21, had previously been deemed unfit to stand trial by psychiatrists due to his severe autism.

In September 2022, while 17, Kurtaj broke into the systems of ride-hailing business Uber, fintech firm Revolut, and the developer of Grand Theft Auto in a spate of hacks committed while on bail for earlier offenses.

Kurtaj had been placed in a hotel for his own protection after being threatened by rival hackers. Although police had confiscated his computer, he managed to hack the companies using an Amazon Fire Stick plugged into the hotel room’s television.

He pleaded guilty last week at Woolwich Crown Court in London to six offenses, four under the Computer Misuse Act, one of fraud and one of possessing a mobile phone in prison.

Seven other charges against Kurtaj will “lie on file,” meaning prosecutors will not pursue them without a court’s permission. The charges do not count as convictions.

The judge initially set a sentence of nine years, reducing it by two years to account for the time Kurtaj had spent detained in a psychiatric hospital.

Kurtaj will serve the remainder of his sentence under supervision in the community and can be returned to prison if he breaches the conditions of his release.

“You are obviously a very talented young man,” Judge Mark Turner told him. “Don't waste those talents by spending the rest of your life in and out of prison.”

Police said they would apply for a Serious Crime Prevention Order, which allows courts to restrict a convicted offender’s activities to prevent further crimes. BBC News reported that a five-year order had already been imposed, prohibiting Kurtaj from using the messaging apps Telegram and Discord.

“Kurtaj knew what he was doing when he carried out these crimes and has finally admitted his involvement,” said Detective Inspector Mike O'Sullivan of the City of London Police.

Grand Theft Auto VI was released last Thursday.

[65] The Record - Hackers breach Austrian Foreign Ministry website, access diplomats' contact details
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/austrian-foreign-ministry-says-website-hacked

[TAM METİN - 412 kelime]
Hackers breach Austrian Foreign Ministry website, access diplomats' contact details

Austria's Foreign Ministry said late last week that hackers breached one of its websites and accessed the names and official contact information of employees at its Vienna headquarters and at Austria’s diplomatic missions abroad.

The breach occurred between November 12 and 13 and affected a website operated by an external service provider, according to a ministry statement reported by public broadcaster ORF on Friday.

The ministry said the breach had been contained and that no personal information belonging to members of the public was compromised. The hackers have not been identified, and it remains unclear how they gained access to the website or whether they stole any information.

The ministry said it was taking the incident "very seriously" and had notified Austria's cybersecurity and data protection authorities.

A spokesperson told Recorded Future News: “For security reasons, we do not disclose details of specific technical vulnerabilities or security measures. The incident has not been attributed to any particular actor.”

The attack coincided with a global Microsoft service outage that disrupted several of the company's services for approximately 24 hours. Microsoft attributed the disruption to a software bug and infrastructure failures. Austrian authorities have not established whether the two incidents were connected.

Austria's Foreign Ministry has previously been targeted by suspected state-sponsored hackers linked to Moscow.

In late 2019 and early 2020, the ministry suffered a cyberattack that lasted several weeks and was described by Austria's National Security Council as a targeted intelligence-gathering operation. In July 2026, Vienna publicly attributed the attack to Russia.

The latest breach comes amid worsening diplomatic relations between Austria and Russia.

In October, Vienna expelled four Russian diplomats, bringing the number of Russian diplomatic personnel declared persona non grata by Austria since 2020 to 18.

Tensions escalated further last week after a Russian drone strike damaged the Austrian Embassy in Kyiv. Austria condemned the strike as "unacceptable" and "a violation of international law," and Foreign Minister Beate Meinl-Reisinger summoned Russia's ambassador in Vienna.

There is no public evidence linking Russia to the latest cyberattack. Moscow has repeatedly denied allegations of involvement in cyberattacks against European countries.

[66] The Record - Signal blocks screenshots by Apple, Google operating systems to protect data from AI agents
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:37:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/signal-no-screenshots-by-apple-google-operating-systems

[TAM METİN - 613 kelime]
Signal blocks screenshots by Apple, Google operating systems to protect data from AI agents

The encrypted messaging platform Signal has announced it will by default stop users from taking screenshots of messages if they are using Google or Apple operating systems, a move fueled by concerns about how the tech giants deploy artificial intelligence agents.

Last year, the widely used messaging app restricted Microsoft 11 users from screenshotting and keeping messages. Similar restrictions will now apply to Google’s Gemini Intelligence and Apple's Siri AI, Signal Foundation president Meredith Whittaker said in a Friday blog post.

“These agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy,” the blog post said. “This is an existential threat to Signal, and our ability to responsibly uphold our mission.”

Whittaker said Signal plans to specifically restrict the agentic systems integrated into iOS 27 and versions of Android where Signal detects AI assistants trying to read text.

The operating systems’ AI agents attempt to glean information from users by scanning what’s on their screens, Whittaker said, and in doing so hoover up the data in order to “answer your questions and do your bidding,” the blog post said.

Whittaker warned the agents are making the captured data accessible to random local databases and remote servers at random.

“All of this is outside of Signal’s control once the data has been captured, and imperils the privacy of all the participants in a conversation,” the blog post said.

While screenshots won’t be permitted by default, the blog post said users who choose to can disable the block in settings.

The “remedy is incomplete,” Whittaker warned, because blocking screenshots does not address “other more sneaky ways AI agents could potentially access application data, such as retrieving content already present on a user’s device via the filesystem.”

The dearth of effective tools available to Signal and other app developers to use to stop AI agents from weakening privacy is a significant concern, the blog post said.

Whittaker condemned Microsoft, Apple and Google for failing to provide app developers with a mechanism to block agents’ access to data without significantly compromising the “core user experience.”

The same lack of cooperation from tech giants is also to blame for Signal’s inability to place the restrictions on macOS, the blog post said. Additionally, Signal has struggled with a profound lack of documentation about how the agents function.

“Without better documentation and clear technical specifications, it will be increasingly difficult for Signal to provide robust privacy and security to the many millions of people who rely on us,” Whittaker said.

“These three operating systems are the oceans in which Signal, and other applications, must swim; they dictate the conditions of life for the application ecosystem,” the blog post said of Apple, Google and Microsoft. “These agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy.”

Whittaker called on the companies to provide an easy way for users to opt-out of data access permissions tools, stop third-party agents from accessing data without users’ consent and bolster documentation lending transparency to how agents work.

Apple did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Microsoft and Google declined to comment.

Meta also has been under fire, following recent reports that its AI agent Muse broke into Signal conversations and digest them

Earlier in her career Suzanne covered the Boston Police Department for the Boston Globe and two presidential campaign cycles for Newsweek. She lives in Washington with her husband and three children.

[67] The Record - FBI reportedly facing second data breach involving National Crime Information Center
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 18:30:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/fbi-cyberattack-data-breach

[TAM METİN - 366 kelime]
FBI reportedly facing second data breach involving National Crime Information Center

The FBI is reportedly investigating a data breach involving the National Crime Information Center, a database used by U.S. law enforcement agencies that contains information on criminals, fugitives and missing people.

The FBI did not respond to requests for comment but sources told CNN and later the Associated Press that the cyberattack took place on Thursday.

The database, known as the NCIC, is used among local, state and federal agencies to share information on criminals and investigations. It offers law enforcement agencies data on outstanding warrants and contains sensitive information of value to criminals and others.

President Donald Trump told Fox News this weekend that he had been briefed on the incident, adding: “I will say that it’s not good — I don’t like that.” He went on to say the breach may allow the public to have access to investigative files related to him.

If confirmed, this would be the second cybersecurity incident affecting the FBI this year. In September, the ShinyHunters hacking group breached the FBI’s job site and allegedly stole troves of information on every FBI employee.

Multiple people were arrested as part of the investigation into the ShinyHunters breach, including a cybersecurity expert from Canada.

The U.S. government has suffered from several cybersecurity incidents this year in addition to the attacks on the FBI.

Last month, hackers took down a platform used by all federal bankruptcy courts across the country and a ransomware attack in August targeted the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF). The ATF called the breach a “major incident” and said it involved a computer system containing information about targets of investigations.

The departments of Defense and Transportation also experienced cybersecurity incidents this year, and the Department of Agriculture was forced to take offline multiple systems used to facilitate payments between the agency and farmers after a cyberattack last week.

[70] Securelist (Kaspersky) - Black Friday, red flags: too-good-to-be-true deals hiding real threats
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 10:00:43 +0000
Link: https://securelist.com/black-friday-threat-report-2026/122061/

[TAM METİN - 3337 kelime]
Black Friday remains a defining moment of the holiday shopping season and a key period for cybercriminals seeking to exploit the surge in consumer activity. The scale of this demand is reflected in seasonal spending patterns. According to PwC’s post-holiday analysis, holiday spending rose by 6.4% year over year in 2025, with Black Friday once again being the peak sales day generating nearly $20 billion. Consumer behavior points to the same trend: KPMG’s 2026 Consumer Pulse Survey found that Black Friday remains the most important sales event, with 62% of respondents pointing to its importance. Moreover, it is strongly associated with online shopping, with 83% of consumers preferring to buy online or combine online and in-person purchases on this occasion. To find the best offers, 55% of respondents in 2026 planned to research purchases through retailer websites and 46% through brand websites. As shopping activity concentrates online during peak sales periods, threat actors gain broader opportunities to exploit consumer demand, trusted brands, and heavy promotional traffic to steal credentials, payment data, and money.

At Kaspersky, we closely follow the cybersecurity risks associated with major shopping events and seasonal spikes in online retail activity. Our annual analysis examines how cybercriminals adapt their tactics to target consumers during high-demand sales periods.

This report extends our annual Securelist series published in 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, and 2025, exploring how shopping-related cyberthreats continue to evolve.

To keep pace with the evolving shopping threat landscape, we conduct an annual assessment of the most prevalent malicious techniques, including financial malware, phishing pages impersonating major retailers, e-commerce platforms, digital services, banks, and payment providers, as well as spam campaigns designed to steer users to fraudulent sites. The threat data we rely on is sourced from Kaspersky Security Network (KSN), which processes anonymized cybersecurity data shared consensually by Kaspersky users.

Most of the data in this report draws on was collected from January through October 2026.

In the first ten months of 2026, Kaspersky identified more than 19 million phishing attacks targeting users of online stores, payment systems and banks, a year-on-year increase of 1%.

As many as 5% of these attacks were directed at online shoppers.

Around 84% of all phishing attacks targeting users of online stores and subscription services involve scammers impersonating Amazon, Apple, Netflix, Shopify and Alibaba Group pages.

Netflix-themed phishing detections grew by 5% in 2026 in comparison with 2025 data.

We detected more than 220,071 sales-themed spam messages in the first two weeks of November, which is 50.2% more than in 2025.

More than 801,000 banking Trojan attacks were recorded during the first 10 months of 2026.

Between June and November 2026, Kaspersky products have already detected over 27,000 attacks by malware and potentially unwanted applications posing as GTA VI-related content.

Shopping-related phishing by the numbers

Phishing and scams remain among the most persistent threats for online shoppers, particularly during peak sales periods, when heightened transaction volumes and time-sensitive offers create conditions for abuse. One of the most notable developments in recent years has been the growing use of AI to increase the speed, scale and sophistication of such campaigns. AI-enabled tools can be used to generate convincing phishing emails, fraudulent shopping pages, and realistic seller identities, while also adapting messages and visuals to mimic legitimate brands. In the context of major retail events, where consumers often move quickly across marketplaces, social platforms, and promotional channels, this added level of automation further increases the risk of deception, credential theft, and financial loss.

Although phishing has long remained a common method of deceiving users, we are seeing a growing number of attacks in segments related to online shopping. From January through October 2026, Kaspersky products blocked 19,063,077 attempts to access phishing links targeting users of online stores, payment systems, and banks – nearly three times (198.1%) more than during the same period last year, when 6,394,854 such attempts were recorded. A breakdown of these attempts shows that 44.54% targeted online shoppers, compared with 48.21% in 2025; 28.55% targeted banking users, up from 26.10%; and 26.91% mimicked payment systems, versus 25.69% last year.

Financial phishing attacks by category, January–October 2026 (download)

Since 2021, we have conducted annual assessments of the distribution of phishing attacks targeting users across popular online platforms, including Amazon, Alibaba Group, eBay, Mercado Libre, and Walmart, in order to track how the attackers abuse trusted brands. In 2026, Kaspersky products detected and blocked 3,102,304 phishing attempts involving the misuse of Amazon’s brand, up 411.6% from 606,369 attempts in 2025. Threat actors consistently stick to leveraging well-known and trusted consumer platforms to enhance the credibility of fraudulent pages.

Other major e-commerce brands were also widely impersonated in phishing campaigns. Kaspersky detected 360,949 attempts to visit phishing pages mimicking Alibaba-owned brands including AliExpress, while eBay-themed phishing pages accounted for 163,381 alerts. The Latin American marketplace Mercado Libre was used as a lure in 109,081 cases, and Walmart-related phishing pages were identified 74,220 times.

Popular online stores mimicked by scammers, January–October 2026 (download)

The rankings of platforms most frequently impersonated by scammers include more than just brands directly associated with tangible items. The attackers are equally interested in scamming users of popular services selling digital products, such as music, TV shows, e-books, and others. The overall top five brands abused in 2026 were Amazon, Apple, Netflix, Shopify, and Alibaba Group–associated brands, which together were mimicked in 7,119,400 detections. Overall, these five platforms represented 83.83% of all phishing attacks associated with online stores and subscription services.

TOP 5 online platforms mimicked by scammers, January–October 2026 (download)

As mentioned before, Amazon was abused in 3,102,304 detections, or 36.53% of the total. Phishing pages impersonating Apple-related resources accounted for 1,622,917 detections, or 19.1%. Netflix-themed phishing accounted for 1,365,648 detections, or 16.08% of the total, marking a 70.5% increase from 801,148 detections recorded in 2025. Shopify-themed phishing accounted for 667,582 detections, or 7.86%.

What mass scam campaigns look like

Mass scam campaigns often rely on phishing kits or templates that can be used to quickly build numerous pages that target different markets, languages, and user segments and mimic widely used platforms. These platforms are broadly recognized and generally trusted by the users, while standardized scam flows allow attackers to scale operations with minimal effort. Across these campaigns, scammers combine familiar visual design, urgent calls to action, and fake login, payment, or verification pages.

Brand recognition in this scenario works against users: the more familiar an interface looks, the less likely a visitor is to question its authenticity. This logic forms the basis of a whole cluster of phishing campaigns that abuse the names of major tech platforms. Fraudulent pages copying login pages of these platforms reproduce the original layout, branding, and even alternative sign-in options, leaving credential entry forms as the only interactive element – everything typed into these forms goes straight to the attackers. Hijacked logins to an e-commerce platform potentially open the door not only to the victims’ purchase history or payment methods in this exact store, but also to other accounts that use the same login credentials.

Scammers often create pages that replicate the fonts and visual style of the original site. This example mimics an Amazon page

A common technique for stealing login credentials. Example of a fake website impersonating Shopify

A related but noisier approach relies on manufactured urgency: we detected pages masquerading as Apple support that bombard the visitor with alarming pop-ups about a suspicious Apple Pay “pre-authorization” and urge them to immediately call a “support number”. If the user contacts the phone number, fake operators will try to talk the victim into revealing sensitive details or granting remote access to their device, which can be used to steal money and data. Despite differing mechanics, both approaches exploit the same vulnerability – reflexive trust in a familiar brand whose identity the scammers have appropriated – and simply choose different triggers: routine in the first case, panic in the second.

Example of a scam page exploiting a sense of urgency

There’s usually a variety of scams abusing users’ trust and the hype surrounding Apple devices. Fake stores appearing to sell or lease iPhones for “low monthly payments” are one example of such scams.

Scammers consistently target fans of flagship smartphones. Example of a fake page impersonating Apple

The streaming platform discount trap

This concentration of promotional activity also expands the space for abuse. As subscription platforms compete for user attention with discounts, limited-time offers, and account-based promotions, threat actors can easily repurpose the same themes across phishing pages and fraudulent payment forms. In this context, brands tied to subscriptions, billing, account access, and merchant services are used as lures: they are widely recognized, naturally associated with recurring payments and promotional offers and therefore likely to prompt user engagement.

Netflix remained among the top five online-store and subscription-service brands most frequently abused by scammers, highlighting the persistent use of streaming services as lures in brand-abuse campaigns. While the majority of observed schemes affecting this platform simply sought to phish users’ credentials, we also saw “generous offers” trying to lure users with discounts. In one example, the attackers embedded the Netflix name in a bundle with other services, such as telecom or entertainment packages. The fraudulent page is designed to collect a range of personal data. Afterwards, the attackers contact the victim via WhatsApp to ask for further information or money. Another scheme imitates the platform itself, offering fake discounted subscription deals. In both scenarios, attackers exploit trust in a globally recognized entertainment brand to increase engagement and push users toward sharing sensitive details or paying money.

Scammers create Netflix-themed fake pages to harvest personal and payment data or sell nonexistent subscriptions

Subscription entertainment brands, such as Spotify and YouTube Premium, are also being used as phishing lures to collect either payment card data through fake subscription forms or account credentials through counterfeit login pages. Informal “support” channels, such as WhatsApp chats, add credibility and help attackers guide victims in real time.

Subscription-service scams impersonate Spotify and YouTube Premium to steal payment details and account credentials

Inside the Black Friday spam wave targeting shoppers

In 2026, Black Friday–related scams continued to spread across multiple channels, with fraudulent email campaigns remaining one of the ways they reached their victims. As retailers stepped up their seasonal marketing, cybercriminals took advantage of the flood of promotional messages by sending look-alike emails. During the first two weeks of November, Kaspersky detected 220,071 spam messages related to the sales season – 50,18% more than in 2025. Over the same period, 1,728 of the identified spam messages referenced Singles’ Day promotions in Asia.

Scam campaigns built around Apple products begin long before Black Friday: fraudulent activity traditionally spikes on the eve of Apple’s keynotes and then flows seamlessly into the November sales season. In one example, a link from a spam email leads to a fake store impersonating a telecom operator, which offers the new iPhone 18 Pro Max at a tiny fraction of its retail price as a “time-limited special offer”.

An example of a detected spam email impersonating a Telenor marketing proposal. The email offers a new smartphone model for sale

The page mimics the real provider’s product page and is most likely designed to trick the user into entering payment card details.

Scammers created a fake page impersonating the Telenor online store. The users land on the website through a link in a spam email

Alongside fake stores, gamified giveaways are still a persistent email scam format of the sales season: instead of simply offering a discount, fraudsters invite users to “win” a prize, which makes users lower their guard and adds an element of excitement. Pages of this kind typically impersonate well-known retail brands and use personalized greetings, limited attempts, and guaranteed “wins” to create an impression of an exclusive, harmless lottery. The prize, of course, is predetermined and serves only as a pretext to request personal and payment card details, allegedly to cover delivery. Exploiting trust in household retail names, such schemes leave victims with compromised card data or stolen money instead of a gift.

Fake REWE-branded Black Friday giveaway offering a free gift for guessing a “secret number”

Wasted: GTA VI “offers” and other lures targeting the gaming community this November

In November 2026, the gaming industry is set for one of its most anticipated launches in years: GTA VI. Highly awaited game releases have long created openings for cybercriminal activity. In our 2024 Kaspersky Security Bulletin, we predicted that cybercriminals would seek to exploit GTA VI’s popularity. In fact, the risk is also amplified by timing: the game’s November 19 launch coincides with the seasonal sales campaigns, when users are already primed to look for discounts, bundles, and limited-time offers. This means users need to be extra cautious.

Malware campaigns typically cluster around the game itself, using the surrounding attention to disguise malicious files as related content; between June and November 2026, Kaspersky detected 27,416 malware and potentially unwanted application attacks posing as GTA VI–related content. At the same time, phishing schemes follow a separate logic: they exploit purchase intent and urgency around offers, driving users to fake pages.

In the example below, a fake store offers Brazilian users a chance to buy a PS5 subscription bundle with GTA VI included. Scammers offer payment options via a banking card or Pix. If the user pays, they never see either PlayStation or the game.

Besides GTA VI, all traditional gaming phishing and scam schemes are also present. The screenshot below shows a fraudulent website mimicking Steam that allegedly gives away $50 gift cards as part of a “Winter Gift Marathon”. To get the card, users are urged to log in to their Steam accounts, compromising their credentials.

Another scheme invites users to a Discord group chat. However, when the victim tries to enter the chat, a pop-up window appears telling them that they need to install a security module. If they click the “Install” button, a suspicious file is downloaded to their device.

From self-care to scam: how attackers target beauty and wellness fans

In addition to shopping-related scams, cybercriminals have continued to exploit consumer interest in personal health, wellness, and beauty in 2026. These themes have long been present in spam and phishing activity, as they combine broad consumer appeal with topics that often involve sensitive personal decisions and data. The continued expansion of online health services, consultations, and wellness products also makes such lures appear more familiar and plausible to users.

Weight loss was one of the health-related themes observed in spam campaigns. In one identified example, a link in a spam email redirected the recipient to a page promoting a telehealth weight-loss service. The page used likely fabricated credibility markers – such as claims of “1,000s of patients” and “600+ doctors” – to make the site appear as an established medical provider. The prompt to “qualify in 5 minutes” through an online assessment acted as the first step in the conversion funnel. Depending on the scheme, such websites may further prompt users to enter their personal and medical data into a fraudulent form, may steal money, or just sell dubious products or services under the guise of a magic pill.

An example of a weight-loss telehealth page

Another spam email targeted users concerned about skin aging. It used a health-advisory style banner and a clickbait headline presenting “The 1 Vitamin You Need if You Have Wrinkled Skin” as a dermatologist’s recommendation. By combining appeals to medical authority and social proof, such as mentioning thousands who already use this vitamin, and framing the offer as free advice through the call to action “Tap For Recipe,” the campaign lowered users’ guard.

A spam email banner promoting a dubious anti-aging supplement through fake dermatologist claims

The link from the email redirects users to a fraudulent site that imitates a legitimate skincare brand and uses a sensational headline about a “$1 Japanese pink vitamin” allegedly responsible for a famous actress’s youthful appearance – a claim the celebrity never made. A fabricated comment section showing more than 36,000 “comments” creates fake social proof and guides visitors toward purchasing a dubious supplement (if it exists at all).

A fraudulent skincare-themed page using a fake celebrity endorsement

Beauty-themed scams are distributed not only by email. Moreover, they are not limited to miracle supplements; fraudsters also exploit the appeal of luxury cosmetics offered at implausibly low prices. In the example below, a fake online store impersonates a trusted retailer, Sephora, and advertises branded perfume at a fraction of its retail price. The page combines familiar pressure tactics, including a fabricated rating, a scarcity warning (“only 6 bottles left”), a one-bottle-per-customer limit and a countdown timer. Together, these elements are designed to rush victims into paying for products that will never be delivered.

Example of a fake beauty retailer page encouraging users to make a purchase on sale

Banking Trojans remained active in 2026. On PC, they can monitor browser activity, inject fraudulent content into legitimate sessions, intercept credentials and card data, and in some cases, manipulate payment details or cryptocurrency transactions. On mobile devices, they usually target a range of banking and online store apps, using various techniques to obtain user credentials or interfere with transactions. This makes banking Trojans particularly dangerous around major retail sales, when fast purchase decisions and intensified use of digital payment flows increase the risk of financial theft.

PC banker activity continued to decline year over year: in the first ten months of 2026, Kaspersky detected 801,150* attacks, compared with more than 1.09 million in 2025. This trend persists due to the continuous shift away from PC bankers toward mobile-first fraud and credential theft. Even so, PC banking malware remained active throughout the year. One example is GoPix, a Brazilian banking Trojan delivered through Google Ads malvertising and designed to abuse Pix, Boleto and cryptocurrency transactions. Other noteworthy threats are JanelaRAT, which targeted online banking users in Brazil and Mexico, and Horabot, which continued to surface in multistage campaigns combining banking malware with email-based propagation.

In the mobile segment, BeatBanker is worth mentioning: an Android banker active in Brazil, distributed via a fake Google Play page and bundled with a crypto miner.

*These statistics include globally active banking malware, and malware for ATMs and point-of-sale (PoS) systems. We excluded data on Trojan banker families that no longer use banking Trojan functionality in their attacks, such as Emotet.

Kaspersky’s phishing data points to a rise in shopping-related threats: from January to October 2026, phishing attempts targeting users of online stores, payment systems and banks increased by 198.1% compared with the same period in 2025. The main tactic remained brand impersonation: scammers abused the names of major online platforms and digital services to create fake login pages, payment forms, support alerts, and subscription offers. Amazon-themed phishing grew by 411.6% year over year, while detections of fraudulent pages abusing Netflix increased by 70.5%.

Black Friday-themed spam also intensified, rising by 50.2% compared with 2025. These campaigns blended into the broader flow of seasonal promotions and redirected users to fraudulent websites. Beyond traditional retail, attackers used lures tied to subscriptions, gaming, and health and wellness, showing how scams are adjusted to current consumer interests. Banking Trojans remained active, although PC banker detections declined year over year, as attackers continued shifting from traditional desktop banking malware toward mobile-first scenarios.

Overall, the findings show that major shopping periods remain associated with a higher concentration of recurring cyberthreats. During seasonal sales events, users should be especially cautious with unsolicited offers, verify websites before entering data, and treat unusually generous discounts or urgent requests with skepticism. To make sure that they don’t end up on a fraudulent website amid the shopping rush, we recommend that users protect their devices with a comprehensive security solution, such as Kaspersky Premium, which uses an advanced AI-powered detection technology that analyzes website characteristics and URLs to identify suspicious patterns. In 2026, Kaspersky Premium was awarded an “Approved” certificate by AV-Comparatives for the second year in a row for its ability to detect fake online stores.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://securelist.com/black-friday-threat-report-2026/122061/, securelist.com, 23.11.2026)

[75] Schneier on Security - Humans Have Rights. Machines Do Not.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: 2026-11-23T12:00:54Z
Link: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/humans-have-rights-machines-do-not.html

[TAM METİN - 750 kelime]
Humans Have Rights. Machines Do Not.

This essay originally appeared in Tech Policy Press.

Last year, Character.AI asked a federal court in Florida to dismiss a wrongful-death suit, brought by the mother of a fourteen-year-old boy who had killed himself after months of conversation with its chatbot. The company made two arguments. One, that the chatbot’s words were speech that its users had a First Amendment right to receive. And two, that Character.AI was a service rather than a product—so product-liability law could not touch it. In May 2025, Judge Anne Conway accepted neither.

Character.AI’s maneuver is part of a larger pattern. When the parents of a sixteen-year-old sued OpenAI over his suicide, the company’s answer also pleaded the First Amendment: the claims would restrain protected speech. The very same document argues ChatGPT is offered “as is,” with no promise of accuracy, and that the boy had circumvented its guardrails. Elon Musk’s xAI (since renamed SpaceXAI) told a federal court in Colorado this spring that every choice in building Grok is an expressive act, and that Grok’s outputs are protected speech. When Grok praised Hitler last summer, xAI said the posts came from a code update independent of the model, and the apology appeared under Grok’s own name. Google, which spent two decades telling American courts that its search results are its own opinion, answered a Minnesota defamation suit over an AI Overview by saying the system merely summarized the web. It also told a Munich court in May that no one should trust an AI overview without checking it. Back in 2024, Air Canada told a Canadian tribunal that its customer-service chatbot was a separate legal entity, responsible for its own actions.

When the output causes harm, the companies deny responsibility. It is the machine’s doing, or a product no sensible person would rely on, or the user’s misuse. When a state or a plaintiff would hold the words to account, the same output becomes speech, and the First Amendment applies.

Social media companies deployed an earlier incarnation of this strategy. They called themselves publishers when editorial discretion was profitable, and neutral carriers when faced with liability for what they published. The result was a privatized public square in which tech platforms were unaccountable. AI companies have taken this even further. A social media platform could at least point to a human author somewhere in the chain. A chatbot’s prose differs; no person at the company wrote it, read it, or chose it before it reached the user. Yet the companies ask that it be protected as though a human had written it.

The question grows more urgent as these systems stop talking and start acting. In July, a poorly controlled experiment at OpenAI resulted in its AI model hacking the network of HuggingFace. Since then both Anthropic and OpenAI have disclosed numerous instances of AI agents hacking outside networks in pursuit of their prompted goals. These are examples of AIs exhibiting genie-like behavior: behaving in ways its controllers neither expected nor wanted. Nothing in the actions count as speech. Breaking into a server is an act that no person at OpenAI explicitly chose. Jill Lepore, surveying this record in The New Yorker, puts the blame on Congress, which has passed no federal statute governing AI agents.

The First Amendment protects expressive choice. It presumes a speaker who can be persuaded, shamed, jailed, or moved to change their mind. Its remedy for bad speech is more speech, which assumes that speech is costly and attention plentiful. A system that can produce millions of individually tailored political messages an hour reverses both assumptions. Verbiage that no person chose is a product, and the company that built the model should answer for it.

The industry wants a self-serving third category that bridges AI speech and action: AI output is human speech when expressive rights are at issue, and the machine’s own doing when blame is. The result is agency without accountability, something no human speaker has ever possessed. That’s why Massachusetts convicted a young woman of manslaughter in 2017 for urging her boyfriend, by text and by phone, to kill himself.

The Court has already glimpsed the problem. Upholding the TikTok divestiture law in January 2025 without dissent, the justices warned that “new technologies with transformative capabilities” counseled caution, then decided the case on national-security grounds and left the larger question for another day.

Tags: AI, human rights, laws, LLM
