[76] The Cyber Express - NVIDIA Investigates Possible Third-Party Breach After Ransomware Group Threat
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 07:42:34 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/nvidia-cyberattack-data-breach-the-gentlemen/

[TAM METİN - 795 kelime]
NVIDIA Investigates Possible Third-Party Breach After Ransomware Group Threat

NVIDIA is investigating a possible NVIDIA cyberattack involving a third-party partner after the ransomware group The Gentlemen added the chipmaker to its data leak website and threatened to publish information on November 28, 2026.

The group has not provided evidence of the alleged breach, including samples of stolen files or details about the data involved. NVIDIA, meanwhile, says it has found no indication that its own systems or networks were compromised, although its investigation into the third-party incident is ongoing.

The listing puts the world’s leading AI chipmaker under fresh cybersecurity scrutiny as it faces a separate period of leadership turmoil following the resignation and subsequent return of CEO Jensen Huang.

What the Hackers Claim About the Nvidia Data Breach

On November 18, 2026, the ransomware operation known as The Gentlemen added NVIDIA to its data leak website, according to International Cyber Digest.

The listing offered little information about the alleged incident. It did not identify the files supposedly stolen, specify how much data may have been accessed, or provide evidence of a successful intrusion.

Instead, the entry displayed the message “DATA INFO – SOON” alongside a countdown ending on November 28, 2026. The timer suggests that the group may publish the allegedly stolen information on that date, potentially as part of an extortion attempt.

However, without a sample of the data or further technical details, the claim remains unverified. It is also unclear whether the alleged incident directly involved NVIDIA or a third-party organization connected to the company.

Nvidia Cyberattack Claim: No Sign Its Systems Were Hit

An Nvidia spokesperson said to the press: “We have found no indication that Nvidia systems or networks were affected. We continue to monitor the situation.”

Security professionals and law enforcement generally advise victims not to pay. Payment does not guarantee the data will stay private, and it does not stop the same group from striking again weeks or months later. If files are encrypted, there is also no assurance that the decryption tool will work, meaning a company could pay and still lose everything.

The usual advice is to keep up-to-date backups of critical data, apply the latest patches to hardware and software, and train employees to spot phishing.

Who Is Behind the Alleged Cyberattack on Nvidia

The Gentlemen emerged in 2025 and have since targeted organizations in healthcare, manufacturing, education, and finance. Its victims include Puerto Rico’s Caribbean Medical Center, which reported a breach affecting around 92,000 people, and US manufacturer Gator Cases, where the group exposed sensitive data on 1,171 individuals in April this year.

The group runs a ransomware-as-a-service operation and reportedly hands affiliates 90% of ransom payments. According to Microsoft, its encryptor is written in Go and can also move laterally, spreading quickly across a victim’s network. The Register reported that affiliates had claimed more than 320 victims as of April, 240 of them in the first half of 2026.

Nvidia has been targeted before. In 2022, the LAPSUS$ hacking collective, then one of the largest and most dangerous in operation, made off with about a terabyte of the company’s sensitive data.

Jensen Huang’s Exit and Return Rattle Markets

The Nvidia data breach claim lands during upheaval at the top of the company. Jensen Huang stepped down earlier in November after admitting to an affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. He was also accused of an affair with an Nvidia executive.

Asian markets took several days to absorb the news. On the Monday after his exit, Taiwan’s key index fell more than 6%, its second-largest single-day drop on record. TSMC, the main supplier of Nvidia’s advanced AI processors, makes up 40% of the Taiex benchmark, so sentiment around Nvidia moves the entire index. Worries over the scandal, the chip supply chain and broader AI spending made last week Taipei’s worst trading week since 1991, with the index falling 17.5% and losing more than $750bn (£575bn) in market value.

Some members of the public and major business leaders welcomed Huang’s return. In the US, however, it has drawn bipartisan anger from lawmakers ahead of his testimony before Congress next week. The Cyber Express has reached out to the firm to learn more about this Nvidia cyberattack. We will update this post once we have more information on the attack or any statements from the company.

[77] The Cyber Express - European Commission Shuts Down EU Academy After Hackers Leak User Data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 07:13:49 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/eu-academy-data-breach-exposes-users-names/

[TAM METİN - 617 kelime]
European Commission Shuts Down EU Academy After Hackers Leak User Data

EU Academy data breach warnings went out this week after the European Commission confirmed that names and email addresses were leaked from its online learning platform.

Public access to EU Academy has been shut off, the investigation is still running, and the full extent of the breach is not yet known.

The Commission has also warned staff to expect further temporary interruptions to internal systems.

What We Know About the EU Academy Data Breach

The Commission says it detected a cybersecurity incident on the EU Academy platform involving the personal data of its users. The data it has confirmed as leaked so far is names and email addresses.

In a notice to users, the Commission said public access to the platform has been disabled. It has also taken steps to contain the incident and limit its impact, including putting preventive, detective and corrective controls in place.

The Commission has not said how the breach happened, who was behind it, or how many users are affected.

Why Names and Emails Still Matter

A leak of names and email addresses can sound minor. The Commission’s own warning shows why it is not.

The notice lists the risks users now face: possible loss of user data, spearphishing attacks, identity misuse and impersonation, spam, and other attempts to gain access to their information.

With a real name and a confirmed email address, attackers can send messages that look like genuine EU Academy communications and are far more convincing than random spam.

What EU Academy Users Should Do

The Commission is asking users to take three steps:

Stay especially alert to suspicious or unsolicited emails and messages.

Avoid sharing personal information with new contacts who approach them about EU Academy or related activities.

Report to a data protection authority if they believe their personal data has been used maliciously.

Why the EU Academy Data Breach Affects Learners Beyond Their Inbox

A separate notice on the EU Academy website says the platform is offline from 11 November 2026 to 14 January 2027 while a “faster, more secure, and more user-friendly” version is built. That notice does not mention the cybersecurity incident.

With the platform offline, users cannot log in, continue courses or download certificates. When it returns, they will need to create a new account. According to the notice, learning history will not transfer to the new platform, and not all content may be available right away when it reopens.

For regular users, that means more than a security warning. Anyone partway through a course, or who needs proof of past learning, will have to wait until mid-January and then start again on a new account.

Several questions are still open:

How the attackers got in. The Commission has not shared any technical details.

How many users are affected. No figure has been given.

What else was taken. Names and emails are confirmed, but the Commission says the incident is still under investigation and has warned of a possible loss of user data.

The link to internal systems. Staff have been told to expect further temporary interruptions, but it is not clear how these relate to the incident.

Until the investigation concludes, the Commission’s advice is simple: treat any unexpected message mentioning EU Academy with caution.

[78] The Cyber Express - Oregon and Virginia Take xAI to Court Over Grok’s “Spicy Mode”
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 06:20:18 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/grok-lawsuit-oregon-sue-over-child-abuse-image/

[TAM METİN - 733 kelime]
Oregon and Virginia Take xAI to Court Over Grok’s “Spicy Mode”

Grok lawsuit filings from two US states make a stark claim: the AI assistant did not just fail to stop harmful content, it was designed in a way that invited it. Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones have taken legal action against the operators of the X platform and its Grok AI assistant. They allege the companies promoted both products as safe while Grok was used to generate sexualized images of people without their consent, including child sexual abuse material (CSAM).

According to Oregon’s announcement, X and Grok are products of Elon Musk’s SpaceX corporation, with the AI and social media subsidiaries referred to as xAI. Oregon filed its lawsuit in collaboration with Virginia, which filed a separate but similar action.

What the Grok Lawsuit Alleges

The attorneys general allege that xAI marketed Grok as an AI companion with a “rebellious streak,” while knowing its image and video tools would be used to produce CSAM, non-consensual intimate images of adults and children, and other sexualized content.

Their filings say the companies put growth and engagement ahead of safety by:

Weakening industry-standard safeguards

Promoting Grok’s ability to generate sexualized content

Enabling the wide creation and sharing of these images through Grok’s integration with X

At the centre of the case is a feature called “spicy mode,” which the states say was created and promoted specifically to allow nude and otherwise sexualized images.

“The lack of needed safety measures was considered a feature, not a bug, which they promoted as ‘spicy mode,'” Rayfield said.

How Big Is the Problem?

The states cite a study by the Center for Countering Digital Harm (CCDH). It estimated that between December 29, 2025, and January 8, 2026, Grok created and posted about three million sexualized images on X. Around 23,000 of them appeared to depict children.

That works out to roughly 190 sexualized images every minute, and one sexualized image of a child about every 41 seconds.

Removal was also a problem, according to the study. Nearly 30% of the sexualized images of children that CCDH identified were still publicly available on X a week or more later. Even many of those marked as “removed” could still be reached through separate URLs.

Why the States Say Users Were Misled

The case is not only about harmful content. It is also about what users were told.

The states say xAI publicly assured consumers that strong protections stopped Grok from creating CSAM and other sexualized images. Despite those assurances, they allege, millions of people, including in Oregon and Virginia, were exposed to harmful content and faced the risk that their own photos could be turned into explicit images without consent.

“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false,” Jones said. He added that companies deploying powerful tools “don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation.”

Jones said the Virginia lawsuit seeks justice for “the millions of Virginians who maintain accounts on X and were misled by the company’s false reassurances.”

Part of a Wider Push in the Grok Lawsuit Cases

For Oregon, the case is part of the Keeping Kids Safe Initiative, which Rayfield launched last week. The initiative includes legal actions and investigations into social media and AI companies accused of harming children and misleading parents about platform safety.

“Like the other technology companies that we’re holding accountable, xAI not only knowingly endangered kids and our communities, but they also lied to our faces about non-existent safety measures,” Rayfield said.

Rayfield’s lawsuit was filed in Multnomah County Circuit Court. Jones’s lawsuit was filed in the Circuit Court for the City of Richmond.

The announcement says the states are seeking significant new protections, but it does not spell out which protections or what penalties they want. It also does not include a response from xAI, X, or SpaceX. The allegations have not yet been tested in court.

[79] Industrial Cyber - Mythos changes the clock: What Anthropic’s cyber-capable AI models mean for OT, critical infrastructure security
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 07:45:21 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/features/mythos-changes-the-clock-what-anthropics-cyber-capable-ai-models-mean-for-ot-critical-infrastructure-security/

[TAM METİN - 3222 kelime]
Mythos changes the clock: What Anthropic’s cyber-capable AI models mean for OT, critical infrastructure security

Anthropic’s cyber-capable artificial intelligence (AI) capabilities are reshaping the speed and cost of finding and fixing software vulnerabilities across critical organizations. Claude Mythos Preview, announced in April 2026, proved so adept at exploiting vulnerabilities that access was limited to vetted organizations under Project Glasswing. That circle has since grown to 150 organizations across 15 countries, though not smoothly. Mythos 5 and Fable 5 launched in June, were suspended within days under U.S. export controls, but returned on July 1. In October, the Critical Infrastructure Defense Program launched, putting expertise and technical knowledge from Dragos, Nozomi Networks and Rockwell Automation behind the effort.

Across operational technology (OT) and industrial control systems (ICS) environments, the implications run beyond faster scanning, detection and remediation. Project Glasswing accelerated the rate at which organizations found vulnerabilities in their systems significantly. Between April and July 2026, partners discovered at least 129,000 known software vulnerabilities, and Anthropic’s own open-source scanning identified another 5,500 between April and October. More than 33,000 of the verified flaws have been flagged as critical or high severity, though the tally is an undercount as it uses survey data from a subset of Glasswing partners. The actual impact is likely at least five times higher.

Clearly, finding bugs or security loopholes became cheap; verifying and fixing them stayed slow and manual. In OT, that gap is the whole story.

Over the years, obscure firmware and scarce reverse engineers served as an accidental moat. But the emergence of AI tools that can discover and exploit software vulnerabilities at unprecedented speed should therefore be a considerable concern for industrial asset owners and technology suppliers. It has been argued that if AI substantially accelerates vulnerability discovery, asset owners may face an increasing number of vulnerabilities that require attention without the ability to address and appropriately patch them. Thus, making compensating controls especially important.

Data from TXOne’s 2026 report revealed that 96% of OT incidents begin in IT-level compromises, either through direct penetration (56%) or collateral damage (40%). While 90% of organizations perform frequent security updates, only 24% achieve asset coverage exceeding 90%. The report also cited compatibility issues and limited asset visibility as primary technical barriers to comprehensive patching. Dragos’ 2025 data identified that only 3% of vulnerabilities needed immediate action, 71% suited planned mitigation, 4% were exploited, and 25% of advisories had neither patch nor mitigation. Triage by physical consequence, not raw CVSS, starting with internet-exposed gateways.

Small utilities cannot staff frontier AI engineering as they lack the expertise that these models call for. Hence, these firms end up borrowing capacity instead of building it up, through trusted OT vendors and also through a bigger Cyber Verification Program.

When it comes to firmware, organizations remain traditional, treating AI output as an unverified lead. This comes amid warnings of false positives, and even Anthropic’s scanner expects over 90% true positives without human review.

ISA/IEC 62443 industrial cybersecurity standards already cover this ground. Anthropic expects defense to win within two years; however, for OT/ICS installations, the next three months can be productively spent to rework consequence assessments, inventory unsupported assets, rehearse manual recovery, and press vendors for realistic patch timelines.

Mythos & Fable: What actually matters for OT?

Industrial Cyber reached out to industrial cybersecurity experts to examine which of the disclosed capabilities of Mythos and Fable matter most for OT and ICS security, and which claims still require independent validation, particularly in industrial environments.

Clint Bodungen, director of AI/ML engineering at Arcova, recognized that Mythos (and in many cases Fable) changes the cost, expertise, and level of effort needed to find and exploit software vulnerabilities dropped significantly. “Anthropic claims Mythos found thousands of high-severity vulnerabilities, including some in every major operating system and web browser, and that for the cases it has published, the model found nearly all of them on its own and developed exploits for many. However, this is a bit over-hyped for the most part. What you won’t hear the media mention (because it doesn’t make for a good story) is that most of the vulnerabilities discovered would be considered low likelihood and/or low impact in the grand scheme of things. There is a difference between a high-severity CVSS score and that which has an actual high impact or likelihood in your specific environment.”

Bodungen added that several aspects of Anthropic’s disclosures still require independent validation, particularly their applicability to industrial environments. The vulnerability counts and autonomy claims are based on Anthropic’s own reporting, while many technical details remain undisclosed until fixes are available. Much of the independent testing conducted so far has measured autonomous attack chaining rather than validating the reported vulnerability findings. Statements from partner organizations are also presented on Anthropic’s own website, underscoring the need for independent assessments.

Another area requiring validation is the models’ ability to assess proprietary firmware and binaries. Most published results focus on source code and open-source projects, whereas much of the OT environment relies on closed firmware.

He mentioned to Industrial Cyber that Anthropic’s Fable 5.1 model, released in September and made generally available, can identify vulnerabilities but cannot build exploits. It relies on Anthropic’s Opus models for exploit generation and binary vulnerability scanning. In July, Anthropic also identified assessments involving ICS, SCADA, DCS, PLCs, RTUs, HMIs and safety instrumented systems, as well as OT protocol abuse involving Modbus, DNP3, OPC and IEC 61850, as high-risk activities blocked by Fable 5 safeguards. The company has not clarified whether Fable 5.1 changed those restrictions. Meanwhile, the more permissive Mythos 5.1 remains limited to vetted organizations, leaving few external researchers able to test these models against real industrial targets.

Bodungen also highlighted the gap between identifying software vulnerabilities and understanding their consequences for physical processes. Finding a vulnerability in a PLC’s web server does not necessarily demonstrate an ability to manipulate an industrial process or create unsafe operating conditions. Similarly, testing in a simulated environment does not replicate the complexity of an operational facility, where safety systems, interlocks and physical processes determine how an attack could affect operations.

“The biggest development is that AI models like Mythos and Fable are demonstrating the ability to find vulnerabilities and develop working exploits with far less time and expertise than was previously required,” Joseph Saunders, CEO of RunSafe Security, told Industrial Cyber. “That fundamentally changes the economics of cybersecurity. We’ve built security programs around identifying vulnerabilities, assessing their severity, and fixing them before they’re exploited. But as AI accelerates discovery and exploit development, the time available to respond becomes much shorter.”

He added, “We still need independent testing, but we don’t need to wait for those results to recognize that our current approach to protecting long-lived critical infrastructure is not going to scale.”

Cam Cullen, CMO at BlastWave, told Industrial Cyber that the capability to dissect open-source protocols and software is critical. “Because most industrial systems rely on this software, discovered vulnerabilities can be weaponized across multiple OT systems. Myhos will not be able to access the source code of these systems because the software is owned by proprietary vendors.”

AI and shifting barriers to ICS exploitation

The executives assess how specialized equipment, heterogeneous industrial environments, and the cost of exploit development have constrained attacks on industrial systems. They also consider how AI could lower these barriers and which assets and access paths are most likely to be affected.

Bodungen recognized that industrial equipment is too specialized, with security by obscurity being a common mantra for years, and debated for years. “Whether it holds weight, though, is almost a moot point, because so far, most attacks haven’t had to rely much on vulnerabilities in the actual industrial equipment. Most serious industrial incidents, such as the 2015 and 2016 attacks on Ukraine’s grid, relied on getting into the IT side, stealing credentials, abusing remote access and using legitimate engineering functions, not on exotic PLC zero-days. Stuxnet and TRITON are the well-known exceptions, with custom controller-level payloads, and both still started on the shared Windows layer.”

He noted that what specialized equipment and diversity did do was raise the cost of the last mile. A reliable attack on a specific control system usually needed the actual hardware or a faithful copy, knowledge of the vendor’s protocols and engineering software (not to mention the unique specifics of how each system and process works in that environment), and time. That kept the top end of OT attacks to a small number of well-funded groups. “The practical effect is that the middle tier of attackers gets more capable. Defenders should plan for more groups being able to do what only a few could do before, and for a much shorter time between a vulnerability being disclosed and being exploited.”

Industrial systems have historically presented unique challenges for attackers, requiring specialized expertise and significant time to understand and exploit, Saunders said. “Developing attacks was more expensive and time-consuming than it was worth. Complexity is no longer a shield. AI is making it easier and cheaper to analyze unfamiliar software and develop exploits, lowering barriers that previously made industrial systems difficult to attack. I’m particularly concerned about software connecting industrial operations to the outside world, including remote access systems, engineering workstations, and network-connected devices.”

He added that when an attacker has a path in, the time and expertise needed to exploit the software may be significantly reduced.

“In the past, these systems were disconnected and highly customized, so they relied on obscurity for security,” according to Cullen. “Now, many use common software modules and operating systems, making it easier for AI to discover exploitable vectors that don’t always rely on passwords or credential theft.”

Prioritizing OT flaws when patches can’t wait

When vulnerability discovery accelerates, but patches are unavailable or cannot be deployed promptly, the executives look into how operators should prioritize findings and choose compensating controls.

“OT has lived in this dichotomy for a long time,” Bodungen said. “Patching in a plant waits for maintenance windows and vendor certification, and some legacy equipment can’t be patched at all, due to how old it is or potential consequences that outweigh the mitigation. Faster discovery just ends up making the backlog bigger. So, prioritization is paramount.”

He added that prioritizing security findings depends on weighing five interconnected factors together, starting with consequence and criticality, including how a compromise affects the physical process, which requires IT, OT, and HSE teams to jointly map physical impacts and cyber attack paths. Next is reachability, or whether attackers can realistically traverse from external, business, or remote channels to the vulnerable asset, making a medium-severity exposed gateway more urgent than a severe bug isolated by strong segmentation.

Bodungen said that teams must also factor in exploitation evidence (such as CISA’s Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog or public exploit code, given how quickly exploitation now follows disclosure), fix availability and timing (distinguishing immediate patches, outage-dependent updates, and unpatchable components), and active threat activity indicating real-world adversary use.

“We cannot patch our way out of the problem. AI develops exploits faster than industrial operators can safely test and deploy patches. Segmentation and monitoring help, but they can’t stop every exploit,” Saunders said. “This is where runtime protection becomes so important. Embedded Runtime Security hardens software against entire classes of attacks without waiting for every flaw to be found and patched.”

He noted, “That’s a fundamentally different economic model for cybersecurity. Instead of a never-ending cycle of discovery, remediation, testing, and patching, we can build protections into software itself. The goal today should be to reduce exploitability. Not finding vulnerabilities faster, but making vulnerabilities impossible to exploit in the first place.”

Cullen identified that the first activity is that their OT networks should be cloaked so they cannot be discovered or accessed by external systems without passwordless MFA. “Any system considered safety- or mission-critical should be protected internally with both microsegmentation and least privilege, and all activity should be monitored.”

Overcoming AI adoption hurdle for resource-constrained utilities

The executives zero in on the hurdles smaller utilities and other resource-constrained operators face in accessing these models, and in benefiting from them once they do. They also consider how wider access could strengthen defenders while keeping misuse in check.

“Smaller utilities face two gaps. The first is access. Anthropic restricts direct access to its most capable cyber model, Mythos, to vetted programs: Project Glasswing was restricted to specific vetted organizations at launch, and since October, an expanded Cyber Verification Program with three access tiers,” Bodungen said. “As of September, Mythos 5.1 was only available to a set of US organizations. A rural water utility or small electric co-op might not have a security team that would apply and/or qualify. Fable is very capable, and the controls have been loosened recently, but many cybersecurity-related tasks (especially regarding vulnerability identification) still get blocked.”

He said that the second gap is capacity. “Even with access, someone has to run the tool, judge the findings, sort out the false positives and then fix things. Many small operators have one IT person who also covers OT, or rely entirely on their integrator. A tool that produces a hundred findings does not help an organization that cannot act on one.”

He recommends routing access through trusted intermediaries. Sector bodies such as the E-ISAC and WaterISAC, state programs and utility associations can sponsor access and turn raw findings into prioritized, practical guidance. He also urges utilities to work with their vendors, since the biggest benefit for small utilities comes from vendors fixing their own products before they ship. One vendor fixing a firmware bug protects every small customer at once, so utilities should encourage their vendors to use these models to test their own products. Finally, he says operators should be given finished defensive outputs, such as prioritized advisories and configuration checks, rather than direct access to an exploit-capable model.

“I believe that keeping the high-risk uses gated is the correct approach,” Bodungen said. “For example, Anthropic’s approach of offering more permissive access only to vetted users and blocking cyber-physical sabotage outright in Fable 5.”

Bodungen, however, cautioned that gating one company’s model buys time. “It does not stop attackers from using other models, including open-weight ones, and Anthropic itself expects these capabilities to spread. Defenders should plan as if attackers already have them. I hate saying it, but it’s true: At some point, you will need to leverage AI to combat AI-capable threats, due to the sheer speed, complexity, and volume of attacks AI will enable.”

“Smaller utilities shouldn’t need a team of AI researchers to keep their infrastructure secure. Many are already stretched thin, and an enormous increase in vulnerability discoveries could overwhelm them rather than improve their security,” according to Saunders. “The real value of these models will come from turning discoveries into actionable protections. Equipment manufacturers, security vendors, and researchers need to translate AI findings into meaningful prioritization and protections operators can actually implement.”

Cullen assessed that these operators need their vendors and larger utilities to shake out the systems, since they lack the resources to operate them, and they look to public organizations to deliver packaged solutions.

Guardrails for safe AI testing on OT firmware

The executives address when critical infrastructure operators should use these models directly and when they should rely on vendors or specialist researchers. They also examine what safeguards are needed to keep work involving OT software and firmware from affecting production systems.

“I believe operators should absolutely use these models directly for defensive work on their own (non-production) environments, provided they have access and the expertise,” Bodungen said. “Example use cases include: reviewing configurations and firewall rules, reviewing their own scripts and code, mapping their architecture and access paths, triaging advisories against their asset inventory, writing detection logic and response playbooks, and running tabletop exercises. That is where most asset owners will get the most value, at lower risk.”

He added that they should rely on vendors and specialist researchers for vulnerability research on OT products, firmware analysis and anything involving exploit development. “The vendor owns the fix, specialists have the test equipment and disclosure relationships, and the most capable versions of these models are deliberately gated anyway.”

“Operators should be working with equipment manufacturers and security researchers to conduct this work in isolated environments, with appropriate validation and human oversight,” Saunders said. “Perhaps more importantly, we need to consider how we build protection into these devices before they enter the field. If we’re relying entirely on the operator to discover and remediate vulnerabilities throughout a 20- or 30-year product lifecycle, we’re placing an enormous burden on the people who have the least flexibility to make changes.”

Cullen said that they can use them for their own network penetration testing and security vulnerability assessments, and expect vendors and researchers to use them on their own systems as well. “In today’s environment, almost all of these systems need a human in the loop to protect AI from unintended consequences.”

Evolving OT standards—and next 90 days

The executives focus on how regulators, standards bodies and vendors should adapt existing requirements and guidance. While those efforts develop, they also weigh what asset owners should prioritize over the next 90 days.

“Regulators and standards bodies should assume vulnerability discovery is now “cheap” and continuous. The concepts already exist: ISA/IEC 62443 zones and conduits, secure remote access and supply chain requirements, and NERC CIP-007, which already lets utilities with medium or high impact systems use a dated mitigation plan when an available patch is not applied,” Bodungen said. “What changes is volume, and more vulnerabilities with no patch at all. Requirements built around periodic assessments and patch timelines should put more weight on compensating controls, reachability and consequence, and give clear guidance on using AI tools safely in and around OT.”

He highlighted that standards need to change frequency guidelines. “Annual vulnerability assessments, penetration tests, and tabletop exercises are no longer sufficient. There is an inevitable increase in threat and vulnerability volume coming, and it will be significant. Assessment and testing frequency need to increase to keep up.”

“Vendors carry the largest share of the change,” Bodungen added. “They should run these models against their own products before release, provide a software bill of materials (SBOM) so customers can see the components they inherit, shorten their fix and qualification cycles, publish mitigations that work when a patch cannot be deployed, and make sure their disclosure process can handle far more reports.”

Saunders pointed to the need to change “how we measure software security. Vulnerability counts and patch speeds don’t tell us whether a system can withstand an attack. This is why we’ve been advocating for Operational Software Assurance. Security cannot end when software ships. Organizations need to understand exposure and reduce exploitability throughout a system’s life. Regulators should expect manufacturers to demonstrate these protections, especially when patches aren’t feasible.”

“Over the next 90 days, operators should identify critical software and access paths, assess risk, and work with vendors to validate protections when patches aren’t possible,” he added. “We must build the software we want for the future while protecting the systems we depend on today.”

“They should regulate these systems’ ability to directly control OT systems,” Cullen said. “Asset owners should prioritize blocking AI-accelerated attack vectors (like reconnaissance and credential theft) as securely as possible in the short term.”

Version 2.1, which shipped in June, introduced the Timeline System, multi-stage exercises and AI-controlled participants. ThreatGEN says the answer is now yes.

The update adds smarter AI facilitation, objective-based exercise stages, hardened multi-tenant security and enterprise seat management, the features that move the product from a standalone training aid toward an enterprise-wide platform.

Between versions 2.2 and 2.6.2, ThreatGEN’s work centered on six areas, starting with learning support. Sessions end immediately when an organization is disabled and are invalidated on password change or reset. Authentication tokens are hardened, role-based permissions are stricter and audit logging is expanded. Facilitator-only content stays hidden from participants, and sensitive data is removed from logs.

Administration, reporting and reliability round out the release. Version 2.6 added role-based seat management, seat limits by role and organization capacity monitoring. Team scoring, overtime scoring and turn numbering in reports are more accurate, and every scored action now lands in the after-action report. Across releases, hundreds of fixes improved live updates, countdown synchronization and document processing.

“A tabletop exercise is only as good as the conversation it forces,” said Clint Bodungen, president of ThreatGEN. “Version 2.6.2 makes that conversation about your own plans, holds teams to real objectives before the clock moves, and gives leadership scores they can defend. It’s the platform our enterprise customers asked us to build.”

Belden expands industrial networking portfolio with new cabling, firmware and security updates to strengthen OT resilience

Belden Inc., provider of complete connection solutions, recently announced the launch of new products and portfolio updates designed to help operators build resilience at every layer, from the cable to the control room, with secure, dependable connectivity in demanding industrial environments.

OT networks across manufacturing, energy and transportation are being asked to do more: connect more devices, meet stricter security requirements and recover faster – all without compromising uptime or performance. Belden’s latest launches are designed to simplify portfolio choices, improve security and uptime, and speed deployment and maintenance. They also build a stronger foundation for IT/OT convergence.

Belden has rolled out a set of product launches and expansions, starting with its cabling portfolio. AlphaWire Xtra-Guard Flex is now Belden Xtra-Guard Flex, and the Belden MachFlex name is retiring in the Americas region. The unified portfolio features four families that match the cable to the motion for robotics, drag chains, tray runs and machine tools. AlphaWire part numbers carry over, and both distributor networks can supply the full range, so there is no disruption, only more capability. A new Hirschmann Operating System (HiOS) release adds HSR multiple rings, replacing dedicated redundancy hardware with native software on GRS2000 and BRP switches, following IEC 62439-3. Coupling two HSR rings via PRP once took four RSP or RSPE switches and now runs on two. Existing 2A and 3A sites can upgrade by firmware alone, and PRP support is coming soon.

Belden has also updated its newly renamed NAC solution with versions 7.1.0, 7.0.0 and 6.6.1. Customers can now configure specific domain controllers for the satellite server from the central Server page, and the releases include library updates and fixes for three CVEs. The NAC appliance moves to Debian 12 (Bookworm) for continuous security of the product itself, and TLS 1.3 is now natively supported for 802.1X authentications. The updates also bring method enhancements for reading out FortiOS switches that use VDOMs, and CoA enforcement through a special RADIUS disconnect method for Cisco Meraki.

Belden is also introducing the RailTuff Fire Resistant Ethernet CAT7 Cable for rail applications globally, along with the FiberExpress MPO Assembly Light Version and FiberExpress Fiber Patch Cord Short Boot for the Asia-Pacific region.

Together, these innovations help keep critical connections reliable, secure the devices that rely on them, and give operators the redundancy, visibility and access control to keep operations running. The result is a more resilient OT network with stronger protection, fewer unplanned interruptions and the confidence to scale.

[83] Industrial Cyber - House report flags Chinese AI models embedded in US businesses, cites cybersecurity and national security risks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 09:25:58 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/ai/house-report-flags-chinese-ai-models-embedded-in-us-businesses-cites-cybersecurity-and-national-security-risks/

[TAM METİN - 2400 kelime]
House report flags Chinese AI models embedded in US businesses, cites cybersecurity and national security risks

A joint report by the U.S. House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China warns that the growing adoption of artificial intelligence (AI) models developed in China is creating national security and cybersecurity risks for American businesses, government agencies and critical infrastructure. Published Friday, the report finds that Chinese open-weight AI models are becoming increasingly embedded in the U.S. economy, driven by their low costs, customization capabilities and performance approaching leading American systems. The investigation found that Chinese models accounted for 41% of downloads on the leading open-model repository over the preceding year, while their deployment across American organizations remains difficult to track. The committees also identified concerns that Chinese developers used industrial-scale distillation campaigns to extract capabilities from American frontier AI models, accelerating the development of competing systems.

Titled ‘Beijing’s AI Playbook: A Joint Investigative Report,’ the document raises particular concerns about the cybersecurity implications of increasingly capable Chinese AI models, including their potential use in developing sophisticated cyber exploits targeting American organizations and critical infrastructure. It identifies a model developed by Chinese AI company Zhipu as capable of autonomously building end-to-end cyber exploits at a level comparable to a leading restricted American model, according to assessments by the National Institute of Standards and Technology and an American frontier AI developer.

The House Committee on Homeland Security and the Select Committee on the CCP launched a joint investigation in April 2026 because the same pattern seen in earlier technology cycles appears to be emerging in AI, and this time the stakes are higher. The inquiry examined national security, cybersecurity and economic security implications of American companies’ growing use of PRC-developed AI models.

It focused on why these companies are adopting the models, how PRC developers acquired the capabilities that made them competitive with American systems, what role PRC government support played in their development and distribution, and what risks come with deepening American dependence on them. As part of the work, the Committees studied three U.S. companies, Airbnb, Anysphere and DoorDash, as case studies of how and why PRC-developed models are already being built into American products and operations.

“Most Americans encounter AI every single day without knowing it: when a pharmacy texts that a prescription is ready, when a chatbot helps them rebook a trip, when an app estimates when their dinner will arrive, or when their bank texts them about an unusual purchase,” Andrew R. Garbarino, chairman of the Committee on Homeland Security, and John Moolenaar, chairman of the Select Committee on China, wrote in the 50-page report. “Behind those interactions, AI models are summarizing customer records, routing support tickets, and executing automated tasks deep inside corporate networks. When a company brings an AI model into its operations, it also brings in the influence of those who developed and trained it, including their priorities, assumptions, and choices that shape how it processes information, generates responses, and carries out tasks. Increasingly, those developers answer to Beijing.”

Over seven months, congressional committees examined why American firms, from Silicon Valley startups to Fortune 500 companies, are turning to PRC-developed open-weight AI models. They reviewed documents and briefings from Airbnb, Anysphere (maker of Cursor) and DoorDash, took classified briefings, and consulted frontier AI developers, cybersecurity executives, federal agencies and academics, along with a September 2026 NSA, CISA and FBI advisory. Companies adopt these models because they are cheap, customizable and competitive with leading American systems.

Federal investigators, however, found that major PRC developers used proxy networks and fraudulent accounts to harvest outputs from American frontier models and replicate their capabilities. Beijing is now trying to turn that progress into global market share and lasting dependence. The State Council has called for an open-source AI ecosystem with ‘international influence,’ and a state academy has described pairing low-cost exports with ‘high-stickiness ecosystem binding.’ Developers are absorbing heavy losses, backed by state funds, state-owned shareholders and subsidized compute, but terms are already tightening: new licenses push some large users into separate agreements, and Beijing has reportedly weighed limiting overseas access to its top models. What starts as cheap can become costly to depend on and hard to replace.

Cyber risk goes beyond commercial dependence. In September 2026, an American frontier developer reported that Zhipu’s newest open-weight model, from a company on the Commerce Department’s Entity List, can autonomously build complete exploits at a level comparable to Mythos Preview, which was judged too dangerous for general release only five months earlier. NIST called it the most cyber-capable open-weight model released to date, and because its weights are public, safeguards can be stripped for a few thousand dollars in compute. The assumption that PRC models trail the American frontier by about six months no longer holds in cybersecurity.

South Korea offers an early warning. In late September and early October, several of its largest banks and financial institutions were breached within days. Public reports say a single attacker used a PRC-built open-source AI penetration testing tool, powered by DeepSeek and other models, including that Zhipu model, to carry out multiple intrusions far faster than a human team could. If those reports hold, the same tools are available to anyone targeting American banks, hospitals and utilities, so a company need not adopt a PRC model to become a victim of someone using one.

The investigation found that Chinese-developed open-weight AI models are already widely used across the U.S. economy, but their deployment remains difficult to track. Open-weight models account for a majority of U.S.-originating traffic on at least one major model marketplace, with Chinese models representing most of that volume. PRC models also accounted for 41% of downloads on the leading open-model repository over the preceding year. Because downloaded model weights do not require a commercial relationship with developers, public metrics capture only part of their use, leaving customers and the federal government with limited visibility into where these models are deployed.

The committees found that low costs, customization and near-frontier performance are driving adoption, while the U.S. lacks a competitive open-weight alternative at the price and performance levels demanded by the market. Leading Chinese models cost a fraction of comparable American proprietary models at published rates. In the latest independent ranking of open-weight model capabilities reviewed by the committees, the highest-ranked American model placed 10th, behind six Chinese models. Companies examined by the committees cited the lack of competitive U.S. open-weight alternatives as a principal reason for their adoption choices.

The investigation also found that much of China’s apparent AI progress relies on capabilities extracted from American models, while U.S. data-labeling firms help supply training data to Chinese developers. U.S. agencies assessed that six leading Chinese AI developers conducted industrial-scale distillation campaigns targeting American frontier models, likely with the awareness of the Chinese government. American data-labeling firms reportedly earn approximately US$500 million annually by selling U.S. training data to Chinese AI laboratories. The committees assess with high confidence that illicit distillation materially accelerated the development of several Chinese model families now used by American companies.

The committees further found that Beijing is using open-weight distribution, state support and low prices to expand global dependence on Chinese AI models, although the terms of that dependence are beginning to change. Chinese policy documents call for open-source AI with ‘international influence’ and describe a goal of ‘high-stickiness ecosystem binding.’ Leading developers sustain substantial losses with support from state funds, state-owned investors and subsidized computing resources. However, developers are introducing commercial licensing terms and revenue demands, while Beijing has reportedly considered restricting overseas access to its most advanced models.

The report warns that risks are increasing as Chinese models become more capable, while differences in their behavior and security properties may escape conventional evaluations. Independent researchers found that some Chinese models generated less secure code when prompts referenced politically sensitive topics or a U.S. government user. Separately, the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) and an American frontier AI developer assessed that Zhipu’s latest open-weight model can autonomously build end-to-end cyber exploits at a level comparable to a leading restricted American model, trailing the U.S. frontier by about four months on aggregate government cyber benchmarks. The model’s safeguards could also be weakened, reducing refusal rates from approximately 95% to between 3% and 6% for an estimated $4,400.

The Committees’ recommendations are meant to answer their findings, build on the federal government’s action against DeepSeek, and strengthen American innovation. They begin with building a credible American alternative. The first recommendation calls for a national American open-weight AI initiative that pairs public compute, shared evaluation infrastructure and lawful access to American frontier models for training with anchor demand from federal procurement. The second would make trusted American AI the global default through export promotion, development finance and partnerships with allies building sovereign AI capacity.

The next group is about knowing which models run inside sensitive systems. The third recommendation directs NIST, in coordination with DHS through CISA, to develop a model provenance standard and an AI bill of materials, required for federal contractors and high-risk critical infrastructure functions and voluntary elsewhere, with protections for small businesses and open-source developers.

The fourth builds on the order excluding DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems by prohibiting PRC-developed AI models and their derivatives in federal information systems and in the performance of federal contracts, with a narrow and transparent waiver process for research, testing and defensive security work. It also calls for modernizing CISA’s Continuous Diagnostics and Mitigation (CDM) program so agencies can discover and inventory the AI models and agents running on their networks, and for prohibiting PRC-developed models in high-risk critical infrastructure functions, with removal schedules tied to the availability of trusted alternatives and temporary exceptions only where none yet exists. Threat-information sharing and stronger defenses make up the third cluster.

The fifth recommendation seeks a long-term reauthorization of CISA 2015 through the Widespread Information Management for the Welfare of Infrastructure and Government (WIMWIG) Act, which the Committee on Homeland Security approved unanimously and the House passed as part of the Fiscal Year 2027 National Defense Authorization Act (H.R. 8800).

Once a long-term reauthorization that extends and modernizes CISA 2015 authorities is enacted, Congress may consider whether additional narrow clarifications on sharing information about threats to AI systems are needed, as those technologies continue to evolve. It also calls for completing a long-term reauthorization of the SLCGP through the Protecting Information by Local Leaders for Agency Resilience (PILLAR) Act, which the House passed in November 2025, while prohibiting the use of program funds for PRC-developed AI models and allowing those funds to be used to replace them. In addition, DHS, acting through CISA, would establish a channel for sharing developer-specific AI threat information with industry.

The sixth recommendation directs NIST’s CAISSI to evaluate leading PRC models regularly and directs CISA to translate the results into guidance that steers critical infrastructure away from PRC models in sensitive functions. It also would codify DHS, acting through CISA, as the Sector Risk Management Agency for the Information Technology Sector, including AI models and AI compute infrastructure.

The seventh prepares DHS and CISA to defend federal networks and critical infrastructure at machine speed, through a DHS pilot program that brings private sector AI and cybersecurity expertise into the Department with a dedicated allocation for CISA, and by directing CISA to serve as the operational lead for securing AI agents on federal civilian networks.

The eighth makes CISA the single point of submission for AI-related cyber incident reports by building on the Cyber Incident Reporting for Critical Infrastructure Act of 2022 (CIRCIA), which reaches the American entities best positioned to detect incidents involving PRC-developed models, rather than creating new, duplicative reporting requirements. It would also establish within CISA an independent, nonregulatory board to investigate the root causes of significant cyber incidents and cyber incidents involving AI, including those involving foreign adversary models and illicit capability extraction.

The Committees also want costs imposed on illicit extraction.

The ninth recommendation uses sanctions, the Entity List and information and communications technology supply chain authorities against firms engaged in industrial-scale illicit distillation, and requires identity verification standards that close the gray market of proxy ‘transfer stations.’ The tenth examines the sale of American-produced frontier training data to PRC AI laboratories designated by the U.S. government and considers disclosure requirements or export controls.

The final objective is setting durable rules and working with allies. The eleventh recommendation ensures that federal AI security policy addresses both closed and open models and establishes a federal baseline for AI transparency and security that does not overburden startups, researchers, or nonprofits. The twelfth expands joint testing and standards work with allies and directs the Intelligence Community, with DHS, to deliver an assessment of PRC state involvement in open-weight AI strategy.

In conclusion, the committees identified that the question before Congress and the entire federal government is not whether Americans should have access to powerful, affordable AI. They should. The question is whether the AI that powers American businesses, public services, and critical infrastructure will be developed by companies accountable to American law and values, or by companies that must answer to the CCP.

“The record assembled by the Committees shows that PRC-developed models have become embedded in American products faster than companies, customers, or the government has been able to understand the implications,” the report detailed. “That adoption was driven by real advantages in price and flexibility. Those advantages were built in part on capabilities taken from American developers and sustained by state support and a willingness to lose money for market share.”

It added that the PRC government has made clear that it views open-weight AI as a source of international influence, and it has already considered restricting access to the models on which others now depend. The U.S. still has time to change this trajectory. American developers retain the lead at the frontier, and a new generation of American open-weight models is emerging.

The Committees’ recommendations are designed to accelerate that progress, give companies and the government the information they need, keep PRC models out of the systems where the stakes are highest, impose costs on theft, and prepare for disruptions before they occur. The nation should not wait for another rip-and-replace program to learn the lesson of Huawei, ZTE, and DJI a third time. The Committees will continue their oversight of these issues.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://industrialcyber.co/ai/house-report-flags-chinese-ai-models-embedded-in-us-businesses-cites-cybersecurity-and-national-security-risks/, industrialcyber.co, 23.11.2026)

[84] Industrial Cyber - US FCC proposes three-year Cyber Trust Mark renewal cycle, seeks stronger oversight of certified IoT products
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 23 Nov 2026 09:23:47 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/product-security/us-fcc-proposes-three-year-cyber-trust-mark-renewal-cycle-seeks-stronger-oversight-of-certified-iot-products/

[TAM METİN - 1911 kelime]
US FCC proposes three-year Cyber Trust Mark renewal cycle, seeks stronger oversight of certified IoT products

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) published Monday a notice seeking public comment on proposed requirements for the U.S. Cyber Trust Mark (USCTM), its voluntary cybersecurity labeling program for wireless consumer Internet of Things (IoT) products. The notice, issued by the FCC’s Public Safety and Homeland Security Bureau, outlines recommendations covering the design and placement of the FCC IoT Label, disclosure of product security information through an online registry, certification renewals, post-market surveillance and procedures for handling consumer complaints and noncompliant products. The proposals aim to strengthen program administration and provide consumers with accessible, standardized cybersecurity information about certified IoT devices.

In the Federal Register notice, the FCC sought comment on the design and placement of the FCC IoT Label, display of product registry information, certification renewal, post-market surveillance, and procedures for complaints, noncompliant products, and program administrators that withdraw or whose authority is terminated. These actions would support establishment and effective administration of the USCTM program and provide consumers with accessible cybersecurity information about certified IoT products.

The proposed framework also addresses certification renewal, post-market monitoring and enforcement procedures to keep the labeling program aligned with evolving IoT cybersecurity requirements. The FCC is seeking comment on recommendations for label dimensions, QR code placement, online security disclosures, renewal testing, complaint handling and procedures for addressing noncompliant products and program administrators that withdraw or lose their authorization.

Under the USCTM Program, authorized products will bear the FCC IoT Label, including the Mark and a QR code that links to understandable security-related information about the product. While the Commission retains ultimate control and oversight as the USCTM Program owner, the program is supported by a Lead Administrator and Cybersecurity Label Administrators (CLAs). The Lead Administrator acts as the point of contact between CLAs, which are responsible for certifying use of the FCC IoT Label and the day-to-day management of the program, and the Commission. Further, in collaboration with the CLAs and other stakeholders, the Lead Administrator is required to undertake tasks associated with the launch of the program, including identifying or developing certain recommendations.

The FCC is weighing how the IoT Label should look and how its credibility can be protected. The Label Standard recommends that USCTM-certified products carry the label on their packaging, either printed directly or applied as an adhesive sticker. It also recommends that the URL reached through the QR code be government-hosted, to build consumer trust in the label and the registry content. The FCC asks for comment on these points and on further steps, beyond trademark infringement enforcement, that could discourage counterfeit use and unauthorized reproduction, watermarking being one example.

On design, the FCC proposes to build the physical label requirements on CTA-2120 and incorporate it by reference, noting that the Office of Management and Budget encourages the federal government to adopt consensus standards developed by the private sector where they serve its purposes. The proposal would require the QR code and “Bounding Box” specified in CTA-2120 and allow both the Horizontal and Vertical layouts for flexibility. It would also adopt a minimum font size of 4-point, though CTA-2120 itself references 5-point for legibility, so the FCC specifically asks which size is right.

The second major question is whether a printed URL should sit alongside the QR code. The Label Standard recommends this for adhesive labels, on the assumption that the domain will be ‘.gov,’ and CTA-2120 defines a ‘URL Zone’ for it. The FCC notes, however, that the IoT Labeling Order concluded a QR code embedded URL, linking to a dynamic database, is the simple way for consumers to reach current security information. It therefore asks whether a printed URL would conflict with that conclusion. If not, it asks when one should be required.

Possible limits include adhesive labels only, smaller products that cannot fit a 1 inch by 2 inch label, or products with no flat surface for scanning, as well as whether the choice should be left to the entity authorized to use the label. It also asks whether mixed practice would confuse consumers.

More broadly, the FCC seeks comment on whether CTA-2120 adequately covers QR code design, label design and placement, including products consumers may never see packaged. It acknowledges stakeholder concerns that paid membership is required to take part in the CTA technical committee, which may be infeasible for some, and says it does not propose to incorporate CTA-2120 in its entirety at this time.

The FCC seeks comment on whether this statement should instead use the term ‘baseline cybersecurity standards,’ since that term is used consistently throughout the IoT Labeling Order and aligns with NISTIR 8425. NIST notes the core baseline is ‘a starting point for manufacturers to use in identifying the cybersecurity capabilities their customers may expect from the IoT devices they create.’

The IoT Labeling Order has tasked the Lead Administrator with submitting recommendations on whether the registry information should be available in additional languages and, if so, which specific languages to include. The Label Standard notes that the Stakeholder Committee recommended presenting the relevant information in both English and Spanish to encourage use and adoption of the program.

Where the cyber nutrition label links to external websites maintained by the manufacturer, the Label Standard recommends that the manufacturer must provide the translated text on those sites.

Further, the recommendation indicates that providing the information in both languages ‘should not pose a burden on manufacturers/label grantees because the HTML template for the linked information need only be translated into multiple languages once by the U.S. Cyber Trust Mark Program to ensure a consistent list of valid responses for each field, as specified in CTA 2120.’ The FCC seeks comment on these recommendations and on how best to ensure multilingual adaptability for the registry information and nutrition label.

On renewal, certification would confirm that the product still meets the requirements in force at initial authorization, along with any new or revised requirements adopted since the initial certification or the previous renewal. The Renewal and Surveillance Document recommends that grantees renew every five years with an approved CLA. According to the Renewal and Surveillance Cover Letter, the Stakeholder Committee preferred five years because a shorter period, such as three years, ‘would be overly burdensome on manufacturers and negatively impact program participation.’ The Cover Letter separately recommends that the Bureau consider whether a three-year cycle is ‘more appropriate for a new program that is targeting evolving cybersecurity risks and seeking to build consumer trust,’ noting that three years is the typical timeframe for many cybersecurity certification programs in use globally.

“We are concerned that a five-year renewal period does not adequately align with the fast-paced nature of IoT threat evolution, potentially leaving consumers with outdated cybersecurity protections. As such, we propose to adopt a three-year renewal cycle, which we believe strikes a balance between the Stakeholder Committee’s recommended five-year renewal cycle and the annual renewal cycle the Commission initially proposed,” the notice identified. “We believe that ensuring consumers receive up-to-date information about the cybersecurity of the IoT product is more critical than the added, yet manageable, burden of more frequent manufacturer certification renewals. We seek comment on this belief. Are there other modifications to the recommended certification renewal process, including streamlined processes that might reduce burdens on manufacturers (e.g., automated reporting/patching, reduced scope/targeted review for stable products, leveraging existing certifications, etc.)?”

The Renewal and Surveillance Document recommends that CLAs run ad hoc surveillance evaluations, beyond annual post-market surveillance, after large cyber events affecting consumer IoT products, initiated by the FCC. Within 30 days, a committee of CLA representatives would decide whether an audit is justified, build a decision tree (scope, logistics, limitations, risk model) to support that decision, and give the FCC either a surveillance plan or a justification for not auditing. Once the Commission approves a plan, sampling, testing and reporting would follow post-market surveillance requirements, and the FCC would share plans with accreditation bodies.

The FCC proposes that the Bureau, not only the full Commission, may initiate evaluations and asks whether other circumstances should qualify. It also proposes that the Lead Administrator convene the CLA representatives and make the submissions to the Bureau, and asks whether supplying a representative should be a condition of final CLA approval.

The FCC further asks whether the Lead Administrator or a CLA committee should be able to start certain activities without Bureau initiation, what deadline should apply to the committee’s submission, and whether submissions should be presumed confidential and filed on paper through the Office of the Secretary. The Bureau would be the final arbiter of whether an evaluation is justified.

The FCC believes ad hoc surveillance will likely be justified after incidents with known impacts on consumer IoT products and asks whether any foreseeable situation would justify no audit. It also proposes that the Lead Administrator submit a post-audit report, with remediation timelines, identifying gaps in surveillance processes or systemic breakdowns in product security, and seeks comment on this requirement.

The FCC also noted that the IoT Labeling Order recognized ‘that certain IoT products, depending on their lifespan and risk level, may need different standards for renewal to achieve the FCC IoT Label,’ and tasked the Lead Administrator with considering such distinctions in recommending appropriate renewal timeframes. “We seek comment on whether there are certain products or product classes for which a different renewal timeframe is appropriate. If so, what products and/or product classes and what timeframes would be appropriate? We also seek comment on the cost implications (in terms of renewal fees) of particular renewal timeframes.”

The FCC seeks comment on modifying, in two ways, the recommended requirement that audit samples be drawn from products certified or renewed by a CLA over the current year or the preceding four years. First, it is concerned that a five-year renewal period may leave consumers exposed to threats arising from outdated product security, and that auditing a product only once every five years may not ensure ongoing compliance given how quickly cybersecurity threats evolve. Consistent with its proposed three-year renewal cycle, it proposes that samples come from products certified or renewed over the current year or the preceding two years, and that a product be selected for audit only once within its three-year certification period unless the Bureau requests otherwise.

Second, it proposes changing the language on submission of the sampling plan from ‘to the FCC’ to ‘to the Lead Administrator,’ in line with its proposal that CLAs submit their Sample Test Plans to the Lead Administrator.

The FCC also asks commenters to address whether IoT products posing ‘higher risks’ to consumers if exploited, such as remote monitoring and home security cameras, baby monitors and products marketed to children, should face more frequent surveillance. It asks whether the seven proposed criteria effectively prioritize the products most likely to harm consumers if their security flaws are exploited, such as smart home devices and children’s products. It further asks whether additional criteria should be mandated for audit selection.

The Cyber Trust Mark is aimed at wireless consumer gadgets, not factory floors, but the line between the two has been thinning for a while, and recent research and threat reporting show how. Forescout found that network convergence across IT, OT, IoT and IoMT could widen lateral movement and magnify the impact of an attack. The Cling botnet, reported in October, hid its command and control inside STUN traffic while going after vulnerable IoT devices.
